Dosing regimen
Initiation Option 1: 927 mg by subcutaneous injection (2×1.5 mL
injections) and 600 mg orally (2 tablets) on Day 1; 600 mg orally
(2 tablets) on Day 2. Tablets may be taken without regard to food.
Initiation Option 2: 600 mg orally (2 tablets) on Day 1; 600 mg
orally (2 tablets) on day 2; 300 mg orally (1 tablet) on Day 8;
927 mg by subcutaneous injection (2×1.5 mL injections) on
Day 15.
Maintenance: 927 mg by subcutaneous injection (2×1.5 mL
injections) every 6 months (26 weeks) from the date of the last
injection ±2 weeks.

(b) (4)

(b) (4)

Approval
Initiation with one of two options [see dosing regimen above]
followed by once every 6-months maintenance dosing. Tablets
may be taken without regard to food.
Sunlenca, a human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1) capsid
inhibitor, in combination with other antiretroviral(s), is indicated for
the treatment of HIV-1 infection in heavily treatment-experienced
adults with multidrug resistant HIV-1 infection failing their current
antiretroviral regimen due to resistance, intolerance, or safety
considerations.

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
These new drug applications (NDAs) for oral (NDA 215974) and injectable (NDA 215973)
formulations of lenacapavir (LEN) were submitted by Gilead Sciences. LEN is a first-in-class
human immunodeficiency type 1 (HIV-1) capsid inhibitor with Breakthrough Therapy
Designation. These NDAs were initially submitted on June 28, 2021 and reviewed by the
interdisciplinary review team.
The intended indication for LEN is to be used in combination with other antiretroviral(s) for the
treatment of HIV-1 infection in heavily treatment-experienced adult patients with multidrugresistant HIV-1 infection failing their current antiretroviral regimen due to resistance,
intolerance, or safety considerations.
The product quality deficiencies identified in the first cycle review were: (1) product quality
information to support the proposed container presentation in borosilicate glass vials was not
acceptable for commercial use due to delamination of the vial containers and concern for glass
particulate contamination, and (2) the data supporting aluminosilicate glass vials as an alternative
were insufficient for full review. The Product Quality assessment team recommended a complete
response based on these deficiencies. On February 28, 2022, Complete Response (CR) letters
were issued to NDAs 215973 and 215974 due to product quality deficiencies.
On June 27, 2022, the Applicant resubmitted both NDAs with additional product quality,
nonclinical and additional long-term data from clinical Study GS-US-200-4625 (Study 4625).
The Clinical, Clinical Virology, Clinical Pharmacology, Pharmacology/Toxicology, Statistics,
Product Quality, and Regulatory disciplines did not identify issues that preclude approval.
I, the signatory authority for these applications, agree that the benefit-risk assessment favors
approval as described in the Benefit-Risk Framework below. The proposed proprietary name,
Sunlenca, was found acceptable.
The Applicant submitted one adequately designed phase 2/3 study, Study 4625, that provided
substantial evidence of efficacy for the proposed indication. Supportive evidence of efficacy was
provided by Study GS-US-200-4334 (Study 4334).
The available safety data from the clinical trials demonstrate that LEN is safe for its intended use
in the heavily treatment-experienced population. Based on the additional nonclinical and clinical
data submitted for this second cycle review, I concur that the risks identified in the review of the
clinical studies’ data can be mitigated through labeling and further evaluated via routine
pharmacovigilance and issuance of postmarketing commitments and postmarketing
requirements. The risk of persistent injection site nodules and indurations was added to the
Warnings and Precautions section of the proposed label, and a subsection for injection site
reactions (ISRs) was included in the Adverse Reactions section in labeling. Additionally, the
prolonged residual exposure of LEN is an important consideration for potential drug-drug
interactions and development of resistance, and these risks have been clearly conveyed in
product labeling.

3

premature death in almost all cases.
• The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention estimates that
approximately 1.2 million people in the United States are living with
HIV. In 2019, there were 15,815 deaths among people diagnosed
with HIV (CDC 2022).
• Maximal and durable suppression of HIV-1 RNA restores and/or
preserves the immune system and reduces HIV-associated
morbidity and mortality. Most treatment-adherent patients with
limited previous HIV treatment experience can maximally and
durably suppress HIV-1 RNA using a combination of two or more
commercially available drugs from different drug classes.
• Treatment failure occurs as a result of drug resistance, drug
intolerance, and suboptimal adherence. Resistance-associated
substitution(s) selected by one drug often confer(s) resistance to
other drugs within that drug class, which further limits treatment
options.
• Heavily treatment-experienced (HTE) patients have limited
remaining options for constructing a fully suppressive antiretroviral
(ARV) regimen due to multidrug resistance, intolerance, or safety
considerations.
Current
• For patients with multidrug-resistant (MDR) HIV-1 infection,
• HTE patients with limited treatment options need
Treatment
providers must individually tailor combination treatment regimens
new and effective therapies that lack crossOptions
based on previous ARV exposure, cumulative viral resistance
resistance with commercially available products,
testing, drug safety and tolerability, and comorbid conditions.
have a favorable tolerability profile, and allow for
convenient dosing.
• The resulting ARV regimens are often burdensome, less welltolerated, and associated with inadequate HIV-1 RNA suppression.
5
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

with multidrug-resistant HIV-1 who cannot achieve complete virologic suppression with antiretroviral therapy (ART) are at high risk
for AIDS-related morbidity and mortality. This population needs new and effective ARV products that lack cross-resistance with
commercially available products, have a favorable tolerability profile, and allow for convenient dosing. LEN, a first-in-class HIV-1
capsid inhibitor with Breakthrough Therapy Drug designation, is one such product. The injectable formulation of LEN provides an
option with the advantage of providing a new mechanism of action and maintenance dosing every 6 months following a lead-in with
oral tablets. However, the prolonged residual exposure is an important consideration for potential drug-drug interactions and risk of
development of resistance.
The submitted evidence demonstrates LEN is effective in patients with multidrug resistant HIV-1 infection failing their current
antiretroviral regimen due to resistance, intolerance, or safety considerations. The pivotal study, Study 4625 (CAPELLA),
demonstrated superiority of LEN compared to placebo, when added to a failing regimen, for the proportion of subjects achieving a
reduction in HIV-1 RNA of ≥0.5 log10 copies/mL after 14 days of treatment. The lack of a control group after Day 14 in Study 4625
limits the ability to precisely quantify the contribution of LEN to long-term virologic suppression. However, the contribution of LEN
is reflected by the relatively high rate of virologic suppression achieved with LEN plus an optimized background regimen (OBR)
through the Week 52 analysis in a population previously unable to achieve virologic suppression. Additional supportive data from the
phase 2 study in treatment-naïve people living with HIV, Study 4334, demonstrated that regimens containing LEN achieved and
maintained comparable virologic suppression through the Week 54 analysis to a standard-of-care control arm based on the results of
the primary endpoint.
The safety evaluation for LEN was adequate and the demonstrated clinical safety profile of LEN is acceptable for the proposed
indication. With the exception of local injection site reactions and the risk of treatment-emergent resistance to LEN, the safety profile
of LEN was generally favorable. While generally reported as mild to moderate in severity, injection site indurations and nodules
remained persistent in several cases and were a cause of study drug discontinuations in Studies 4625 and 4334. Additional
information, however, is needed to understand the contributing factors of ISRs with LEN, particularly the mechanism driving the
persistence of injection site nodules and indurations in some subjects. These issues will be further addressed via routine
pharmacovigilance and the issuance of a clinical PMR. Resistance to LEN emerges readily in virologic failures, thus it is important to
use LEN as a component of a complete active ARV regimen.
Based upon review of all available efficacy and safety data, the benefits of LEN, in combination with other ARVs, clearly outweigh
the risks for treatment of HIV-1 infection in heavily treatment-experienced adults with multidrug-resistant HIV-1 infection failing
their current ARV regimen due to resistance, intolerance, or safety considerations. The availability of LEN will provide a new and
effective treatment option for this patient population.

NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

meeting was to discuss the deficiencies noted in the Complete Response letters and to obtain
feedback on the Applicant’s plan for resubmission of NDAs 215973 and 215974. On June 27,
2022, Gilead resubmitted NDAs 215973 and 215974.
The primary efficacy results from Study 4625 were confirmed during the first NDA review
cycle. Superiority of LEN compared to placebo was demonstrated for the proportion of HIV-1
RNA reduction ≥0.5 log10 from baseline to the end of the functional monotherapy period (FMP)
at Day 14. The Week 26 and Week 52 HIV-1 RNA were planned to be evaluated as secondary
endpoints; however, the majority of subjects did not complete the Week 52 assessment at the
time of the original NDA submissions. The resubmissions of NDAs 215973 and 215974 provide
additional long-term safety and efficacy data (Week 52) from Study 4625, as well as safety and
efficacy data from other ongoing and completed clinical studies with LEN.

3.1. Review Issue List
Refer to Section 3.1 of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 from the first cycle.
During the first cycle, the review team identified five review issues relevant to the evaluation of
benefit and three review issues relevant to the evaluation of risk.
Key Review Issues Related to Evaluation of Benefit (First Cycle)
•

The baseline imbalances of HIV-1 RNA level and cluster of differentiation (CD) 4+ cell
count for Cohort 1 between the two randomized arms. The conclusion was that these baseline
imbalances did not impact the interpretability of the primary efficacy result.

•

The Applicant proposed a simplified dosing regimen that was not studied during the LEN
clinical development program. The review team concluded that the simplified dosing
regimen was acceptable, and the label should include recommendations for both the
simplified regimen and the regimen used in Study 4625.

•

In a clinical study designed for study in an HTE patient population, it is important to
determine if the subjects are truly failing on their pre-study ARV regimen rather than due to
nonadherence to the ARV drugs with no evidence of resistance in their supposed “failing”
regimen. If subjects are nonadherent to the regimen and do not have resistance to the
“failing” regimen during the LEN functional monotherapy period, it will be difficult to
determine the contribution of LEN to efficacy in the background of other active or partially
active ARVs. Supplementary sensitivity efficacy analyses removing subjects that had
evidence they were not failing on their background regimens at study entry confirmed the
primary endpoint analysis of LEN.

•

The proposed subcutaneous maintenance dosing interval and the approach to missed doses
outside of the recommended window (±2 weeks). The review team concluded that the
optimal dosing interval for SC LEN injection is 26 weeks, and the ±2-week dosing window is
acceptable to provide flexibility and facilitate adherence to the SC LEN regimen.

3.1.2. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
3.1.2.1. Resistance Assessments of Virologic Failures at
Week 52
The assessment of resistance emergence in VFs of an LEN-containing ARV regimen is critical to
interpreting the overall risk-benefit of LEN and understanding the durability of LEN as a
component of an ARV regimen in a HTE patient population.
3.1.2.2. Injection Site Reactions
ISRs were identified as the most commonly reported ADRs among subjects who received SC
LEN injections; however, the long-term experience of subjects with multiple SC LEN injections
was limited in Studies 4625 and 4334.

3.2. Approach to the Review
Table 4 provides an overview of the clinical studies that substantially contributed to the benefit
and risk assessment for LEN. Data from Study 4625 (CAPELLA) were the primary clinical
study data submitted in support of efficacy. Study 4334 (CALIBRATE) provided additional
descriptive support for the evaluation of safety and efficacy.
Additional phase 1 studies, such as Study GS-US-200-5709 (Study 5709), were used to support
the simplified dosing regimen, as well as adding supportive safety experience to the overall
safety database.

compared to those at Week 26 (Table 7 and Table 8).

Efficacy Endpoints
The primary endpoint for this study was the proportion of subjects with HIV-1 RNA
<50 copies/mL at Week 54. For virologic suppression (HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL), the LEN
combined arm had a similar response rate with Study 4625, which support the efficacy results of
Study 4625; the difference between the LEN and BVY arms had a wide 95% CI with the lower
bound at −17.3. For VF (HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL), the LEN combined arm had a
significantly worse result compared to the B/F/TAF arm. Also, the difference between the two
arms was greater than that at Week 28 (Table 10).

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

Assessment
From Day 14 onward, all subjects in Cohort 1 were transitioned from their initial (failing)
background regimen to an optimized regimen in combination with open-label LEN. There was
no comparator arm during the maintenance period, and the interim analysis included follow-up
through Week 52.
The proportion of subjects in Cohort 1 who achieved HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL was 81% at
Week 26 (presented at Cycle 1 of the review), and 83% at Week 52. The durability of virologic
response was maintained. Details of the analyses for virologic outcomes at Week 52 for
Cohorts 1 and 2 are presented in Table 8. Additional extended follow-up is anticipated (optional
through 104 weeks).
Comparing the virologic response of LEN to other drugs indicated for the HTE population,
proportions of subjects who achieved HIV-1 RNA <40 copies/mL was 54% at Week 48 for
fostemsavir, and proportions of subjects who achieved HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL was 43% at
Week 25 for ibalizumab-uiyk. These comparisons across these different studies, however, should
be considered with caution.
Conclusion
Despite the limitations in the assessment of extended efficacy data, such as the lack of a
comparator arm during the maintenance period and uncertainties associated with cross-study
comparisons, the available data suggest that LEN contributes to the durable antiretroviral effect
as part of an optimized background regimen.

7. Risk and Risk Management
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
Refer to Section 7.1 of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 from the first cycle.
Please also refer to Section 13. No new potential risks were identified based on the
Pharmacology Toxicology review of additional nonclinical data.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Refer to Section 7.2 of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 from the first cycle.
LEN is the first in a new class of antiretroviral drugs; therefore, no previously described clinical
experience is available to inform any specific safety concern regarding CA inhibitors.
Immune reconstitution syndrome has been reported with combination antiretroviral therapy,
particularly in the initial phase of treatment, in patients whose immune systems develop an
inflammatory response to indolent or residual opportunistic infections. This is also often
particularly relevant in a HTE patient population.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

•

Study 4334 (a phase 2 study in treatment-naïve PLWH): Analyses were conducted after all
subjects had completed the Week 54 visit (September 2021 data cut date).

In addition, the Applicant included a summary of overall LEN exposure from all ongoing and
completed studies.
In the NDA resubmissions safety update report, updated cumulative safety data from the first
dose of study drug through the safety update data cut date were provided for Studies 4625, 4334,
and 5709. For Study 4625, the NDA resubmission safety update data-cut date was in February
2022, after all subjects in both Cohorts 1 and 2 had completed their Week 52 visit (i.e., 52 weeks
after their first dose of SC LEN) or prematurely discontinued study drug. In addition, the safety
update included safety data from six phase 1 studies with LEN that were not included in the
original NDA submissions; these studies are evaluating the safety of SC, intramuscular, and oral
LEN in healthy adult subjects. One of the six phase 1 studies (Study GS-US-536-5816 [Study
5816]) is evaluating the safety of SC LEN in combination with two broadly neutralizing antiHIV-1 antibodies in virologically suppressed adults with HIV-1 infection. The studies providing
data for this safety report are listed in Table 11.

For the phase 1 studies, Study 5709 was included in the original NDA submissions; the other
studies are newly added to the NDA resubmissions and were not included in the original NDA
submissions. The Integrated Summary of Safety submitted for the original NDA submissions
included pooled data from Studies 4538 and 5709. Study 4538 and Study 5709 Cohort 3 had
completed and final data were used in the original NDA submissions; therefore, there was no
new update.
Approach to Assessment of Clinical Trial Data
The review approach for assessment of risk consisted of the evaluation of safety data from Study
4625 and Study 4334 separately, as well as the pooled safety data from phase 1 studies. These
25
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

studies were reviewed separately because the studies enrolled different patient populations.
Study 4625 enrolled an HTE population, whereas Study 4334 enrolled a treatment-naïve
population, and the phase 1 studies enrolled healthy adults. Results of individual studies are
highlighted where important differences may have emerged.
Clinical-study data from Studies 4635 and 4334 were independently analyzed using JMP
v. 16.2.0, Analysis Studio v. 1.6.0, and JMP Clinical v. 8.0. Additional analyses were provided
by the Clinical Data Scientist support team. All safety assessments and conclusions are those of
the clinical review team unless otherwise specified. Prespecified hypothesis testing was not
proposed for safety outcomes in Study 4625 or 4334; comparisons are therefore based on
descriptive analyses.
Comparisons across arms in Study 4625 were limited because assessment during the randomized
portion of the study were limited to the 14-day FMP with only the oral administration of LEN.
As a general approach, attribution of events due to LEN comparisons across study arms were
derived largely from Study 4334 and selected cohorts from phase 1. For relevance to the
proposed SC dosing, study arms that included SC LEN were combined and compared to another
active control arm randomized to B/F/TAF. Because the pooled phase 1 data were placebocontrolled in healthy volunteers, comparisons across arms were generally more acceptable for
the purpose of assessing contribution of LEN; however, the overall sample size achieved in this
analysis was small and duration of follow-up was shorter.
In the Applicant’s proposed labeling for LEN, Adverse Reactions (Section 6.1) included only
data from Study 4625 as this study included the relevant patient population (HTE) for the
proposed indication, whereas Study 4334 included treatment-naïve subjects anticipated to have
significant differences in underlying disease severity and comorbid conditions.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
Refer to Section 7.5 of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 from the first cycle.
Overall, the safety database is adequate to assess the safety of LEN for the proposed indication,
dosage regimen, and patient population (HTE).
At the time of the NDA resubmissions, 1508 subjects were enrolled in clinical studies and
received at least one dose of LEN, regardless of route (SC, by mouth, or intravenous),
formulation, or population (healthy, PLWH). Excluding subjects who received LEN or LEN
placebo for two ongoing blinded studies, the total number of subjects are at least 1272. A total of
797 subjects (healthy, PLWH) have received at least one subcutaneous dose of LEN, and a total
of 303 subjects with HIV have received LEN (any route). The total number of PLWH who
received one dose of 927 mg SC LEN, with the same proposed SC formulation or equivalent oral
dose that is expected to achieve the targeted exposure for at least 6 months, was 195 (Table 12).

Study 4625 Clinical Safety Experience
The median duration on-study was 84 and 64 weeks for Cohorts 1 and 2, respectively; this period
of exposure enabled the review team to identify both early- and late-onset adverse events (AEs).
Table 13 summarizes the exposure periods for Study 4625, which is ongoing. A total of 72
subjects in Cohorts 1 and 2 received at least one dose of SC LEN. Safety comparisons between
randomized subjects, however, were limited to 36 subjects in Cohort 1 during the 14-day FMP,
24 of whom received oral doses of LEN. During the maintenance phase of Study 4625, during
which all subjects received SC LEN:

- Cohort 1: N=34, Cohort 2: N=19

7.6. Safety Findings and Concerns Based on
Review of Clinical Safety Database
The demonstrated safety profile of LEN in HTE subjects with MDR HIV-1 infection is
acceptable at the proposed doses by mouth and SC. Overall, there is no clear pattern of LENrelated safety issues considered severe or life-threatening (Grade 3 or 4). Deaths were
uncommon, and there were no patterns observed among serious adverse events (SAEs). None of
the deaths nor SAEs were considered to be study-drug related in Studies 4625 and 4334. A small
number of subjects from Studies 4625 and 4334 experienced ISRs resulting in discontinuation of
LEN, which is consistent with a long-acting injectable drug.

7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Studies 4625 and
4334
7.6.1.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event
Summary, Studies 4625 and 4334
An overview of treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) in Study 4625 through at least the
Week 52 visit date is shown in Table 15. Overall, 96% of subjects in the total LEN group
experienced at least one TEAE.
An overview of the TEAEs in Study 4334 through at least the Week 54 visit date is shown in
Table 16. Overall, 88% of subjects in the total LEN group, and 84% of subjects in the B/F/TAF
group experienced at least one TEAE.
Across Studies 4625 and 4334, there were no drug-related SAEs, very few discontinuations
because of AEs, and very few Grade ≥3 AEs considered by the investigator to be study drugrelated. Differences in AEs reported across these two studies likely reflect underlying differences
in the patient populations.

Analysis of clinical safety outcomes by demographic subgroups were intentionally not included

hamper meaningful interpretation of the analyses.
7.6.1.2. Deaths, Studies 4625 and 4334
In Study 4625, one additional death was reported after the Week 26 interim analysis (original
NDA submissions); this subject died on Day 568 of hypoxemic respiratory failure and
mycoplasma pneumonia. In total from Study 4625, two subjects who received LEN died; none of
the events were considered to be study drug-related by the investigators; the clinical reviewer
agrees with these assessments. Details of the two deaths are provided in Section 7.6.3.2.
There were no deaths reported in Study 4334 by Week 54.

Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

Clinical Reviewer’s Assessment: The one additional death reported in Study 4625 since the
original NDA submissions does not represent a new safety concern and does not change the
overall safety profile of LEN.
7.6.1.3. Serious Adverse Events, Studies 4625 and 4334
In Study 4625, since the Week 26 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), six additional
subjects experienced the following SAEs: coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19; two subjects),
genital herpes simplex (one subject), pneumonia (one subject), pneumonia mycoplasmal,
pancytopenia, dehydration, and acute respiratory failure (one subject), and septic shock, renal
impairment, shock (one subject).
In Study 4334, after the Week 28 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), one subject
experienced an SAE of uterine leiomyoma (SC LEN + emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide
coformulated [F/TAF]→tenofovir alafenamide [TAF] group), and one subject experienced an
SAE of hepatitis A (LEN + [F/TAF→bictegravir [BIC] group), which is discussed further in
Section 7.6.4.6).
An overview of all the SAEs in Study 4625 through at least the Week 52 visit dates is shown in
Table 17. An overview of the SAEs in Study 4334 through at least the Week 54 visit dates is
shown in Table 18. None of the SAEs was considered to be study-drug related in Studies 4625
and 4334. See Section 17.2 for additional details.
Clinical Reviewer’s Assessment: Overall, no events were disproportionally reported as SAEs to
suggest a pattern. Treatment-emergent SAEs were the most commonly reported within the
Infections SOC in Studies 4625 and 4334; however, these SAEs are reflective of the patient
population rather than the safety profile of LEN.

7.6.1.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events, Studies 4625 and 4334
In Study 4625, after the Week 26 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), one subject
(1.4%) in Cohort 1 experienced a nonserious TEAE of injection site nodule (Grade 1) after
receiving the Week 52 SC LEN injections, which lead to premature discontinuation of LEN
during the Extension Phase (data collected after Week 52). The TEAE of injection site nodule
was assessed as related to LEN by the investigator and had not resolved by the data cut date. At
the time of the original NDA submission, no subject had prematurely discontinued study drug
because of an AE; however, this subject’s premature discontinuation was included in the 90-Day
Safety Update from the original NDA submissions. See Sections 7.6.3.4 and 7.7.2.
In Study 4334, after the Week 28 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), one subject in
the total SC LEN group experienced nonserious TEAEs of injection site erythema (Grade 1) and
injection site swelling (Grade 1) that led to premature discontinuation of LEN. In total, 3 of 105
subjects (2.9%) in the SC LEN total group discontinued study drug due to ISRs (injection site
induration [2 subjects], injection site erythema [1 subject], and injection site swelling [1
subject]); all the ISRs were nonserious and Grade 1 in severity. See Section 7.7.2.
Clinical Reviewer’s Assessment: Nonserious, Grade 1 ISRs were the only AEs that led to the
discontinuation of LEN in Studies 4625 and 4334. The LEN labeling includes the one subject
from Study 4625 who discontinued LEN because of an AE (Grade 1 injection site nodule); in
addition, ISRs are adequately described as adverse drug reactions (ADRs) associated with SC
LEN in labeling. See Section 7.7.2 for details.
7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Studies 4625
and 4334
In Study 4625, a TEAE was defined as an AE that began on or after the first dose date of LEN;
for participants who discontinued from the oral LEN, only AEs collected up to 60 days after the
last dose date were considered treatment emergent. In Study 4334, TEAEs began on or after the
first dose date of study drug up to last exposure date after permanent discontinuation of study
drug, or led to premature study drug discontinuation.
A combination of data from Studies 4625 and 4334 were used to assess the general AE profile of
LEN. Although details of safety information from Study 4334 will not be presented in the LEN

labeling, Study 4334 was important in this assessment because it serves as supplementary
support for the safety evaluation of LEN.

7.6.2. Laboratory Findings Studies 4625 and 4334
Most laboratory abnormalities in Studies 4625 and 4334 were generally not clinically significant
(Grade 1 or 2), and occurred in subjects who had previous underlying conditions (e.g., diabetes),
or there was an alternative explanation (e.g., creatine kinase elevation after strenuous activity).
In Study 4625, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 18 (25.0%) of 72 subjects.
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 5 (6.9%) of 72 subjects. Grade 3 or 4
abnormalities that occurred in more than two subjects included high creatinine, high fasting
glucose, high nonfasting glucose, glycosuria, and proteinuria. The frequency of subjects with
Grade 3 or Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities at Week 52 (23 subjects; 31.9%) was similar to
Week 26 (19 subjects; 26.4%). See the discussion in Section 7.6.3.6.
In Study 4334, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 26 (16.6%) of 157 subjects
who received LEN (the total LEN group) and 6 (24.0%) of the 25 subjects in the B/F/TAF group.
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 13 (8.3%) of 157 subjects in the total LEN
group and no subjects in the B/F/TAF group. Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in
13 (8.3%) of 157 subjects in the total LEN group, including the following in more than one
subject: high creatine kinase, high alanine aminotransferase (ALT), hyperbilirubinemia, and
glycosuria. The frequency of subjects with Grade 3 or Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities at
Week 54 (39 subjects; 24.8%) was similar to Week 28 (26 subjects; 16.6%). See the discussion
in Section 7.6.4.6.
Trends in vital signs and body weight in Studies 4625 (Week 52 analysis) and 4334 (Week 54
analysis) were also reviewed. There were no clinically relevant changes from baseline or in

median values for systolic blood pressure, diastolic blood pressure, pulse, respiration rate, or
body temperature. In Study 4625, the median (quartile 1, quartile 3) increase in body weight was
1.3 (−2.0, 4.3) kg; only one subject had an ADR of weight increased (Grade 1). In Study 4334,
the median increase in body weight was 2.6 (0.2, 6.8) kg and 2.3 (−3.1, 7.3) kg in the total LEN
group and the B/F/TAF group, respectively; two subjects receiving LEN had ADRs of weight
increased (oral LEN + F/TAF [Grade 1]; SC LEN + [F/TAF→TAF] [Grade 2]), and 1 subject
receiving B/F/TAF had an ADR of weight increased (Grade 1).

7.6.3.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event
Summary
An overview of TEAEs for Study 4625 was discussed in Section 7.6.1.1. Table 22 lists the

regimens during the maintenance period (and all subjects in Cohort 1 received SC LEN
following the FMP), comparisons across treatment arms were not expected to provide trends that
demonstrate the impact of LEN on safety outcomes (Table 23).

7.6.3.2. Deaths
After the Week 26 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), one additional death was
reported and is summarized below; this death was not considered related to LEN by the
investigator. In total, from both cohorts of Study 4625, two subjects (2.8%) who received LEN
died (Table 24).
Subject

(b) (6)

This was a 48-year-old female in the United States with an extensive ongoing medical history
that included the following: hypertension, asthma, hyperlipidemia, colitis, depression, and
esophageal candidiasis. At baseline, HIV-1 RNA was 880 copies/mL and CD4+ cell count was
495/μL (15.8%). The subject’s HIV-1 background regimen included doravirine, BIC,
emtricitabine (FTC), and TAF. On Study Day 514, experienced mycoplasma pneumonia
(Grade 3, assessed as not related); Study Day 528, developed pancytopenia (Grade 3, assessed as
not related); Study Day 543, developed dehydration (Grade 3, assessed as not related); and Study
Day 553, developed acute respiratory failure (Grade 5, assessed as not related). The subject died
on Day 568 of hypoxemic respiratory failure and mycoplasma pneumonia. Autopsy findings
revealed “consolidated lungs with superimposed severe acute bronchopneumonia on pre-existing
acute and organizing acute respiratory distress syndrome leading to respiratory failure and
sepsis.”

7.6.3.6. Laboratory Findings
Most laboratory abnormalities in Study 4625 were generally not clinically significant (Grade 1 or
2), and occurred in subjects who had previous underlying conditions such as diabetes.
In Study 4625, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 15 (21%) of 72 subjects.
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 4 (6%) of 72 subjects. Grade 3 and 4
abnormalities that occurred in more than two subjects included high creatinine and high fasting
glucose.
Since the Week 28 analysis, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported for an additional
nine subjects (LEN total group: six subjects; B/F/TAF group: three subjects) and Grade 4
laboratory abnormalities were reported for an additional six subjects in the LEN total group. No
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in the B/F/TAF group. None of these laboratory

Hepatic Safety
Overall, there were no clinically relevant changes from baseline in ALT, AST, or total bilirubin.
Subjects with marked elevations in hepatic transaminases and/or bilirubin were evaluated for
potential drug-induced liver injury (DILI). The subjects were reviewed to determine the etiology
of the biochemistry elevations. If no other likely causes of DILI could be found, the case could
be attributed to LEN.
Overall, two subjects in Cohort 2 (one in the Week 26 interim analysis and the other in the
Week 52 interim analysis) each experienced a Grade 3 increase in ALT and a Grade 4 increase in
AST. Both subjects had concurrent alternative etiologies and are described below.
In a screening analysis for DILI from all subjects during Study 4625, one subject (
)
in Cohort 2 (large blue dot [●] in Figure 5, who was a 27-year-old Asian male, experienced a
Grade 4 increase in AST, a Grade 3 increase in ALT, and a Grade 2 increase in total bilirubin;
these increases were also reported as one TEAE of alcohol abuse (Grade 3) and three TEAEs of
alcoholic hepatitis (Grade 2/3). Laboratory trends are shown in Figure 6. The increases in ALT,
AST, and ratio of AST to ALT were not considered related to LEN by the investigator. Although
not shown in Figure 6, additional information provided by the Applicant showed improvement in
AST, ALT, and total bilirubin when the subject abstained from alcohol while LEN continued.
Because of the alternative cause reported, the increases in ALT, AST, and total bilirubin were
not considered related to LEN.

7.6.4.2. Deaths
No subjects died during Study 4334 as of the Week 54 interim analysis data cut date.
7.6.4.3. Serious Adverse Events
Since the Week 28 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), treatment-emergent SAEs were
reported for two additional subjects (1.3%) in the total LEN group; these were uterine
leiomyoma (worsening; Days 240 to 249) in one subject in the SC LEN + F/TAF→TAF group,
and hepatitis A (Days 572 to 573) in one subject in the SC LEN + F/TAF→BIC group (discussed
further in Section 7.6.4.6). An additional subject in the Oral LEN + F/TAF group, who had
experienced previous SAEs of concurrent bipolar disorder and major depression (Days 67 to 77),
had an additional SAE of mental disorder (Days 534 to 543). None of the SAEs were considered
related to LEN by the investigator, and none were disproportionally reported as SAEs to suggest
a pattern. None of the subjects in the B/F/TAF group experienced any SAEs.
In total, SAEs were reported in 10 subjects (6.4%) in the total LEN group and none of the
subjects in the B/F/TAF group. None of the SAEs were reported in more than one subject in any
treatment group, and all SAEs were considered not related to the study drug by the investigator.

Since the Week 28 interim analysis (original NDA submissions), Grade 3 laboratory

explanation (e.g., creatine kinase or elevation after strenuous activity).

elevations. If no other causes could be found, the case would be attributed to LEN. Since the

three subjects also experienced high AST, and one of the three subjects also experienced
(b) (6)
hyperbilirubinemia and had a Grade 3 TEAE of hepatitis A (Subject #
; Figure 8).
For all subjects, the laboratory abnormalities either improved or returned to normal while on
study drug.

7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
7.7.1. Resistance Assessments of Virologic Failures
(VFs) at Week 52
Issue
The assessment of resistance emergence in VFs of a LEN-containing ARV regimen is critical to
interpreting the overall risk-benefit of LEN and understanding the durability of LEN as a
component of an ARV regimen in an HTE patient population.
Background
Genotypic and phenotypic analyses of the HIV-1 protease, reverse transcriptase (RT), integrase,
and capsid (CA) proteins were performed at screening for all subjects enrolled in Study 4625.
Resistance testing at postscreening timepoints was performed for any subject meeting the criteria
of the resistance analysis, which included any subject who received at least one dose of study
drug and met one of the following VF criteria:
•

HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL and <1 log10 HIV-1 RNA reduction from the start of oral LEN
at the Week 4 visit, confirmed at a scheduled or unscheduled visit at least 2 weeks following
Week 4.

•

At any visit, after achieving HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL, a rebound in HIV-1 RNA to
≥50 copies/mL, which was subsequently confirmed at the following scheduled or
unscheduled visit; OR

55
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

•

At any visit, a >1 log10 increase in HIV-1 RNA from the nadir, which was subsequently
confirmed at the following scheduled or unscheduled visit.

•

Any study subject with HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL at the study endpoint or study
discontinuation who did not meet any of the criteria above.

Monogram Biosciences, Inc. (South San Francisco, CA, USA) was the designated reference
laboratory for all resistance analyses at screening and virologic failure. The PhenoSense GT®
assay (Monogram Biosciences), which determines the genotype for the entire HIV-1 protease
and amino acids 1 to 305 of RT and measures the drug susceptibility of all marketed protease
and RT inhibitors based on that sequence, was performed for all subjects at screening. The
GeneSeq Integrase assay was used to determine the sequence of the entire IN protein, and the
PhenoSense Integrase assay measured the drug susceptibility of all marketed integrase strandtransfer inhibitors (INSTIs) based on that sequence (Monogram Biosciences). In cases of assay
failure, alternative assays such as GeneSeq, GenoSure mg, or GenoSure Integrase (Monogram
Biosciences) were used to obtain genotypic information.
HIV-1 CA genotype was determined using the GenoSure Gag-Pro assay, which uses nextgeneration sequencing (NGS) to detect codon variants present at >10% frequency (Monogram
Biosciences). The PhenoSense Gag-Pro assay (Monogram Biosciences) was used to assess
phenotypic response to LEN.
The phenotypic susceptibility of screening samples from enrolled subjects to entry inhibitors
(enfuvirtide, fostemsavir, ibalizumab, maraviroc) was analyzed at the end of the enrollment
period. These analyses were conducted using a combination of the Trofile and PhenoSense Entry
assays (Monogram Biosciences), which determine the coreceptor tropism and the susceptibility
to the inhibitors, respectively, based on the HIV-1 envelope from the subject’s sample.
Assessment
Week 52 Clinical Resistance Emergence
In Study 4625, 31% (22 of 72) met the criteria for resistance analyses through Week 52 (HIV-1
RNA ≥50 copies/mL at confirmed VF [suboptimal virologic response at Week 4, virologic
rebound, or viremia at last visit]) (Table 33 and Table 34; see also Table 54). The 22 confirmed
VFs had postbaseline CA genotypic resistance data, comprising 11 from Cohort 1 (six in the
LEN arm and five in the placebo arm) and 11 from Cohort 2. LEN resistance-associated
substitutions in CA emerged in 41% (9 of 22) of the VFs (Table 33 and Table 34). The LEN
resistance-associated CA substitution M66I was observed most frequently in six confirmed VFs
(five with HIV-1 subtype B, one with HIV-1 subtype AE), alone or with other substitutions
including Q67Q/H, Q67Q/H/K/N, K70K/R, K70N/S, N74D, N74N/H, A105T, and T107A. The
other three VFs had emergent LEN resistance-associated CA substitutions Q67K+K70H,
Q67H+K70R+T107S, and Q67Q/H. An independent assessment of NGS data was performed and
in general, corroborated the genotypic results submitted in the NDA.

NGS Analysis
An independent resistance assessment was performed using the NGS data derived from subjects
in Study 4625 who were determined to be VFs. Several CA amino acid substitutions were
identified as treatment-emergent among multiple subjects from different genotypes and
subgroups who experienced VF, including substitutions at positions M66 (six subjects: five B
and one AE), Q67 (five subjects: three B, one BF, and one C), K70 (five subjects: three B, one
AE, and one BF), N74 (four subjects: two B, one AE, and one C), A105 (four subjects: three B
and one AE), and T107 (two subjects: two B). These treatment-emergent CA substitutions
identified in the independent analysis were also reported by the Applicant and the phenotypic
data were provided in the NDA submissions.
Conclusion
In Study 4625, 31% (22 of 72) of HTE subjects met the criteria for resistance analyses through
Week 52 (HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL at confirmed VF [suboptimal virologic response at
Week 4, virologic rebound, or viremia at last visit]) and were analyzed for LEN-associated
substitution emergence. LEN resistance-associated CA substitutions were found in 41% (n=9) of
the confirmed VF subjects with postbaseline CA genotypic resistance data (n=22). The M66I CA
substitution was observed in 27% (6 of 22) of subjects, alone or in combination with other LEN
resistance-associated CA substitutions, including Q67Q/H, Q67Q/H/K/N, K70K/R, K70N/S,
N74D, N74N/H, A105T, and T107A. The other three VFs had emergent LEN resistanceassociated CA substitutions Q67K +K70H, Q67H+K70R+T107S, and Q67Q/H. The treatmentemergent CA resistance substitutions were confirmed by FDA independent analyses.
The VF isolates with emergent CA resistance-associated substitutions had reduced LEN
susceptibility of 6- to >1428-fold.
Four VF subjects had emergent resistance substitutions to components of the OBR. Additionally,
among the nine VFs who developed LEN resistance substitutions in CA, four received LEN in
combination with a background regimen with no fully active ARVs based on the baseline
genotypic and/or phenotypic resistance. Therefore, given the higher risk of developing resistance
to LEN, careful consideration should be given to inclusion of active drugs in addition to LEN in
the treatment regimen.
•

LEN resistance emerges readily in VFs and thus it is important to use LEN as a component
of a complete active ARV regimen.

•

LEN resistance substitutions do not confer cross-resistance to other ARVs.

7.7.2. Injection Site Reactions
Issue
ISRs were identified as the most commonly reported ADRs among subjects who received SC
injections of LEN; however, long-term experience of subjects after multiple SC injections of
LEN was limited in Studies 4625 and 4334.

61
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

Background
For other ARVs administered in an injectable form, such as CABENUVA (a long-acting
formulation of two drugs, cabotegravir and rilpivirine), ISRs are the most commonly reported
ADRs. ISRs associated with intramuscular CABENUVA were most commonly described as
localized pain/discomfort (71% to 79% of subjects). The CABENUVA label also includes the
following other ISRs: nodules (10% to 17% of subjects), induration (17% to 12% of subjects),
swelling (5% to 8% of subjects), erythema (2% to 4% of subjects), pruritus (4% to 5% of
subjects), bruising/discoloration (2% to 3% of subjects), and warmth (1% to 2% of subjects).
Anesthesia, abscess, cellulitis, and hemorrhage at the injection site were each reported in less
than 1% of subjects (ViiV-Healthcare 2022). Serious postinjection reactions were reported
within minutes after the injection of rilpivirine and began to resolve within a few minutes after
the injection (<1% of subjects). These reactions were characterized by signs and symptoms
including dyspnea, bronchospasm, agitation, abdominal cramping, rash/urticaria, dizziness,
flushing, sweating, oral numbness, changes in blood pressure, and back pain (e.g., back and
chest). These events may have been associated with accidental intravenous administration during
the intramuscular injection procedure (ViiV-Healthcare 2022).
Injection site nodules have been reported with a number of other drug classes, including
glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor agonists used for treatment of patients with type 2 diabetes.
General ISRs (e.g., erythema, pain, or rash) have been reported with all commercially available
glucagon-like peptide 1 receptor agonists (i.e., exenatide, lixisenatide, liraglutide, dulaglutide,
and semaglutide) (Hearn and Sherman 2021). Injection site nodules have rarely been described
with other long-acting or extended-release drug products, such as exenatide. Although the
mechanism for development of such nodules is not entirely clear, available histopathology data
reveal the presence of eosinophils and granuloma at these sites, and therefore, hypersensitivity to
the formulation is a plausible mechanism (Jones et al. 2015). Surgical excision or some other
medical intervention, such as serial intralesional corticosteroid injections, may be required for
treatment of persistent lesions.
LEN is a CA inhibitor with not only a new mechanism of action but also unique, albeit not fully
(b) (4)
understood, physical properties when injected
Assessment
ISRs were the most commonly reported ADRs with SC LEN injections. Preferred terms related
to ISRs in Studies 4625 and 4334 are listed in Table 35. Injection site swelling, erythema, pain,
nodule, and induration were the most frequently (>10%) reported preferred terms. None of the
ISRs was considered serious.
•

In Study 4625, 47 of 72 (65.3%) subjects experienced ISRs assessed as related to SC LEN;
most ISRs were Grade 1 or 2 in severity. Three subjects (4.2%) experienced nonserious,
Grade 3 ISRs that were considered by the investigator to be related to SC LEN and are
described below:
- Subject
experienced two Grade 3 ISRs: “bilateral swelling at injection site
max diameter cm 10” reported on Day 16 and ending on Day 19 (Injection site swelling);
“bilateral redness at the injection site max diameter cm 10” reported on Day 16 and
ending on Day 23 (Injection site erythema).
(b) (6)

Abbreviations: HIV, human immunodeficiency virus; ID, identifier; ISR, injection site reaction; LEN, lenacapavir; PrEP, pre-exposure
prophylaxis

In Study 4625, a total of three subjects had biopsies performed. Two of the three subjects
(injection site induration and injection site nodule) had biopsies that were consistent with foreign
body granuloma, and the third subject (injection site nodule) likely had secondary amyloidosis
associated with SC injections of enfuvirtide (Table 53 in Section 17.4.2 for additional detail).
In Study 4334, a total three subjects had biopsies performed. Two of the three subjects (injection
site swelling and injection site nodule) had biopsies that were consistent with foreign body
granuloma.
The Applicant provided their assessment on the role of physical and chemical characteristics of
LEN on ISRs; this included an assessment of nonclinical and clinical data for LEN and other
ARVs. In summary, the Applicant stated that their review of ISRs and pH for various SC ARVs
as well as other LEN formulations did not identify a clear association between high pH of a
formulation and ISRs. The Applicant states that their available nonclinical data suggest that
chronic granulomatous inflammation and foreign body response at the SC injection site are
expected to occur due to the presence of a SC drug depot, and irrespective of the pH of the
formulation. Finally, the Applicant believes that the ISRs observed in clinical studies with SC
LEN, including nodules and indurations, are likely associated with the expected host foreign
68
Integrated Review Template, version 3.0 (10/21/2021)
Reference ID: 5097739

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

indurations, including the time to resolution in Section 6 (Adverse Reactions). Later in the
review cycle, DAV requested the addition of ISRs in Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions) to
address the concern of persistent injection site nodules and indurations. The Product Quality
team has also recommended that the provider carefully inspect the syringe after withdrawing the
LEN solution from the vial for visible particulates.
Based upon our assessment of the available data, including the addendum submitted by the
Applicant on December 14, 2022, the potential of risk of ISRs associated with SC LEN do not
outweigh the benefit for the treatment of MDR HIV-1 infection in HTE subjects with the
appropriate labeling as mentioned above. From a clinical safety perspective, however, additional
information is needed to fully understand if there are any contributing factors of injection site
reactions with LEN, particularly for subjects who have prolonged resolution with nodules and
indurations. The Clinical team has recommended a PMR to assess the completion of long-term
follow-up of ISRs in Studies 4625 and 4334, and the Product Quality team has recommended
(b) (4)
postmarketing commitments (PMCs) to further assess vial and
compatibility, and
evaluate an alternative device for withdrawing the LEN injection from the vial.

Based upon the 18-month long-term storage data, Gilead proposed the shelf life of 24 months for
the drug product. The proposed shelf life is acceptable. The proposed storage condition for drug
product is “Store at 20°C to 25°C (68°F to 77°F), excursions permitted 15°C to 30°C (59°F to
86°F), protect from light.” The photostability data demonstrate that the secondary packaging
(b) (4)
(
tray with paperboard carton) protects LEN injection
from photodegradation. The supporting in-use stability data to confirm the stability of LEN
injection during the proposed in-use period in the clear glass vials and syringe is provided in the
NDA. The proposed drug product label also includes a statement in Section 16; i.e., ‘keep the
vials in the original carton until just prior to preparation of the injections, in order to protect
from light’.
Drug Substance
The drug substance review was adequate in the previous review cycle, and no new drug
substance information was provided in the resubmission. The NDA is recommended for approval
from the drug substance perspective.
Drug Product
The submission is adequate from a drug product perspective. LEN Injection is sterile,
preservative-free, for subcutaneous administration. Marketing of a single strength is proposed
(463.5 mg/1.5 mL [309 mg/mL]) in a glass vial. The dosage strength is based on the lenacapavir
(b) (4)
free acid. Each vial contains a nominal 1.5 mL clear, yellow
solution consisting of
(b) (4)
473.1 mg of lenacapavir sodium, 896.3 mg polyethylene glycol 300, and
water for
(b)
(b)
injection. Each vial contains sufficient volume ( (4) mL with (4) mL overfill) to allow
withdrawal of 1.5 mL.
Drug product kit: The proposed container closure system for the drug product consists of a
(b) (4)
single-dose, clear glass vial. Each vial is sealed with an
closure and aluminum
overseal with flip-off cap. The drug product is supplied as a co-packaged kit which includes all
the components needed for healthcare professionals to administer the injection; i.e., two
lenacapavir filled vials, two vial access devices, two disposable syringes, and two injection
safety needles.
Devices
All components (devices) of the proposed kit are 510(k) cleared; therefore, the CDRH
determined that no additional review is needed. Gilead had previously submitted the device
validation studies in Section 3.2.R, therefore, the DMEPA team was also consulted during the
first review cycle and determined that a human factor study and results were not required for
NDA submission. There is no change in the device design since that determination (February
2021), thus no additional review was needed from the DMEPA team and their review will be
limited to the labeling.

III. Appendices
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Refer to the Review Cycle 1 Integrated Review for complete Summary of Regulatory History
prior to submission of the initial new drug applications (NDAs) on June 28, 2021.
On February 28, 2022, Complete Response letters were issued to NDAs 215973 and 215974 due
to product quality deficiencies including 1) incompatibility between drug product and
borosilicate glass vials, and 2) incomplete information with respect to container closure integrity
testing.
On May 24, 2022, a Type A, postaction teleconference was held between Gilead and the Food
and Drug Administration (FDA or the Agency). The purpose of this meeting was to discuss the
deficiencies noted in the Complete Response letters and to obtain feedback on Gilead’s plan for
resubmission of the NDAs. The Agency agreed with the plan to submit data from nonclinical
Study TX-200-2068 and additional long-term data from clinical Study 4625 in the resubmission.
In addition, Gilead’s approach to responding to the product quality issues seemed acceptable.
On June 27, 2022, Gilead submitted their resubmissions to NDAs 215973 and 215974.
On July 27, 2022, the Agency acknowledged receipt of the applications and notified Gilead that
the submissions had be classified as Class 2 Resubmissions.

Information and Assessment
Please refer to Section 13 of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 for the Nonclinical
review from the first cycle. Additional data submitted in this cycle include a 26-week
carcinogenicity study in transgenic mouse and a single-dose intramuscular local tolerance study
in rabbit.

The carcinogenic potential of lenacapavir (LEN) was tested via subcutaneous (SC)
administration in a 26-week mouse study. RasH2 hemizygous mice were administered 0, 30,
(b) (4)
(b) (4)

% polyethylene glycol (PEG)-300 and
% sterile water for injection once every 13 weeks for 26 weeks (two total
doses).
There were no variations of tumor incidences considered related to administration of LEN based
on the FDA statistical review. The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee concurred
that this study was negative.

enrolled in Cohort 2, each of whom had received ≥1 dose of study drug, was 307 days

17.1.2. Phase 1 Studies Not Included in the Original
NDA Submissions
The following phase 1 studies were not included in the original NDA submissions or the
previous NDA 90-day safety update report. The provided information is based on the NDA
resubmission safety update data cut date.
17.1.2.1. Study 4540
Study 4540 is an ongoing, phase 1, open-label study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics (PK),
safety, and tolerability of SC LEN in healthy adult subjects, who received a single dose of SC
LEN (927 mg as the sodium salt) on Day 1, administered into alternative injection sites (thigh
[Cohort 1] and upper arm [Cohort 2]) in comparison with the abdomen (Cohort 3) as the
reference site. A total of 30 subjects was enrolled in the study, 10 in each of Cohorts 1, 2, and 3,
all of whom received a single dose (927 mg) of SC LEN.
17.1.2.2. Study 5710
Study 5710 is an ongoing, phase 1, open-label, multiple-cohort study to evaluate the PK, safety,
and tolerability of single doses of SC and intramuscular (IM) LEN formulations containing
(b) (4)
different concentrations of both LEN and
, administered to healthy adult
subjects, with or without an optional oral LEN loading dose as determined prior to cohort
initiation based on available data. Dose escalation through cohorts was performed in an adaptive
manner. A total of 98 subjects was enrolled in Cohorts 1 (1200 mg; n=22), 3 (300 mg; n=21), 4

(100 mg; n=12), 7 (125 mg; n=11), 10 (150 mg; n=12), and 13 (300 mg; n=20), and each had
received the protocol-specified single SC LEN injection on Day 1, with doses ranging from 100
(b) (4)

. No optional oral loading dose was required to be

the specified cohorts completed study drug and there were no premature study drug
discontinuations due to an adverse event (AE). Not all cohorts had enrolled.
17.1.2.3. Study 5717
Study 5717 is an ongoing, phase 1, open-label, multiple-cohort study to evaluate the PK, safety,
and tolerability of single doses of SC or IM LEN formulations containing different
(b) (4)
concentrations of both LEN and
, administered to healthy adult subjects,
with or without an optional oral LEN loading dose for subjects receiving an IM LEN injection,
depending on cohort and based upon available data. A total of 57 subjects had been enrolled
variously in Cohorts 1 (2400 mg; n=20), 2 (2400 mg; n=10), 4 (3200 mg; n=10), 6 (2400 mg;
n=7), and 11 (2400 mg; n=10). Subjects in Cohorts 1, 2, 4, and 6 had received the protocolspecified single SC LEN injection on Day 1, with doses ranging from 2400 mg to 3200 mg,
while subjects in Cohort 11 had received a single IM LEN injection on Day 1 with no protocol
requirement for an optional oral loading dose. All subjects in the cohorts identified completed
study drug, and there were no premature discontinuations from study drug because of an AE. Not
all cohorts had enrolled.
17.1.2.4. Study 5977
Study 5977 was a phase 1, open-label study to evaluate the PK, safety, and tolerability of oral
LEN in healthy adult subjects, who received a single oral dose of LEN (25 mg) on Day 1
(Cohort 1), or multiple oral doses of LEN (25 mg) once daily for 56 days (Cohort 2). A total of
34 subjects was enrolled in the study, Cohort 1 (n=8) and Cohort 2 (n=26), all of whom received
at least one dose of study drug. There were no discontinuations due to AEs.
17.1.2.5. Study 5816
Study 5816 is an ongoing phase 1, randomized, blinded, proof-of-concept study to evaluate the
safety and efficacy of the broadly neutralizing antibodies GS-5423 and GS-2872 in combination
with LEN in virologically suppressed adults with human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1)
infection. Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio into one of two treatment groups, each of
which received on Day 1 a loading dose of oral LEN 600 mg, followed by SC LEN 927 mg,
followed by intravenous infusions of, first, GS-5423 30 mg/kg body weight, and, second,
GS-2872 at a dose of 10 or 30 mg/kg depending on treatment group. A further loading dose of
oral LEN 600 mg was administered on Day 2. A total of 21 subjects was randomized and
received at least one dose of study drug. No subject discontinued study drug due to an AE.

NDA 215974 Sunlenca (lenacapavir) tablets, for oral use

17.1.2.6. Study 6148
Study 6148 was a phase 1, open-label, parallel-design, single-dose, three-cohort study to evaluate
the potential drug-drug interaction between islatravir (ISL) and LEN in healthy adult subjects
who received coadministered oral ISL 20 mg and oral LEN 600 mg (Cohort 1), oral islatravir
20 mg (Cohort 2), or oral LEN 600 mg (Cohort 3) under fasted conditions on the morning of
Day 1. A total of 55 subjects was enrolled in the study, 18 in each of Cohorts 1 and 2, and 19 in
Cohort 3; each subject received their assigned single dose(s) of study drug. No subject
prematurely discontinued study drug due to an AE.

17.4. Supplemental Data for the Safety Review:
Injection Site Reactions
17.4.1. Resolved and Ongoing Injection Site
Indurations and Injection Site Nodules by Study
As requested by the Agency, the Applicant submitted a cumulative review of injection-site
reactions (ISRs), including ongoing and resolved injection site nodules and indurations and
general updates on all types of ISRs from LEN studies (as appropriate and feasible, that include
SC dosing regardless of the specific formulation, dose or injection volume).
Study 4625: LEN–Related Injection Site Nodules and Indurations
Overall, 18 of 72 subjects (25.0%) experienced study drug–related injection site nodules, and 11
subjects (15.3%) experienced study drug–related injection site indurations; all events were
Grade 1 or 2 in severity.
The duration of nodules and indurations occurring as a result of the first SC LEN injections was
longer than those occurring as a result of the second LEN SC injections (since their follow-up
was longer) and longer for ongoing ones (compared to resolved ones).

Adverse events were coded according to MedDRA v. 24.1.
Study drug–related ISR is defined as an adverse event marked as related on the Adverse Event Electronic Case Report Form with
the high-level term, injection site reactions.
Each SC dose consisted of two SC injections into the abdomen.
Each injection site reaction was counted in the total. One subject could have multiple ISRs.
If a subject had more than one injection site induration or nodule events, the subject will be counted in the Ongoing category unless
all study drug–related injection site induration or nodule events have been resolved.
Duration = stop date − onset date + 1.

Study 4334: LEN-Related Injection Site Nodules and Indurations
In the Week 54 analysis of Study 4334, 15 of 103 subjects (14.6%) experienced study drug–
related injection site nodules, and 13 of 103 subjects (12.6%) experienced study drug–related
injection site indurations (Table 50). All events were Grade 1 or 2 in severity, except for one
subject with Grade 3 injection site nodules.
The duration of nodules and indurations occurring as a result of the first SC LEN injections dose
was longer than those occurring as a result of the second LEN SC injections (since their followup was longer) and longer for ongoing ones (compared to resolved ones).

17.4.2. Photographic and Dermatopathological
Reports
During the review of the original NDA submissions, photographic documentation of injection
site indurations or injection site nodules from Studies 5709, 4334, and 4625 were provided in
response to the Agency’s information request dated September 08, 2021. Refer to Section 7.7.2
of the Integrated Review dated February 28, 2022 from the first cycle.
For the NDA resubmissions, no additional photographic reports from Study 5709, 4334, or 4625
were included. The Applicant reported that although a few investigators were contacted
regarding ongoing injection site nodules or injection site indurations, the investigators did not
refer the study subjects for dermatological consultations because the ISRs had resolved or were
not warranted. See Table 52.

dermatologist assessments for two subjects from Study 4625 who met the aforementioned ISR
criteria; a summary of the biopsy reports is provided in Table 53.

Abbreviations: ARV, antiretroviral; ID, identifier; LEN, lenacapavir; SC, subcutaneous

17.4.3. Assessment of the Role of the Physical and
Chemical Characteristics of Lenacapavir in ISRs
The Applicant provided their assessment on the role of physical and chemical characteristics of
LEN on ISRs; this included an assessment of nonclinical and clinical data for LEN and other
antiretrovirals (ARVs). In summary, the Applicant states that their review of ISRs and pH for
various SC ARVs as well as other LEN formulations did not identify a clear association between
high pH of a formulation and ISRs. In addition, the Applicant believes that the ISRs observed in
clinical studies with SC LEN, including nodules and indurations, are likely associated with the
presence of a SC LEN depot.
The Applicant states that their available nonclinical data suggest that chronic granulomatous
inflammation and foreign body response at the SC injection site are expected to occur for an
antiretroviral compound, including LEN, due to the presence of a SC drug depot, and
irrespective of the pH of the formulation.
Clinical Reviewer’s Comment: Based on the information provided, the review team agrees that
the Applicant’s conclusions are reasonable.

Reviewer: Deacqunita L. Diggs, Ph.D.

Mortality
Animals were checked twice daily for mortality and signs of pain or distress. The survival rates are
presented in Table 1. The animal was found dead or sacrificed early due to its moribund condition
were noted as the causes of death. The overall lowest survival rate (76%) was in males dosed
with 100 mg/kg, whereas, in high dosed males (300 mg/kg), the survival rate was 92% which is
similar to that seen in control males (96% survival). The higher incident of mortality in males at
100 mg/kg is unlikely related to the test article because the mortality incidence in males at 300
mg/kg was similar to controls and this effect was not seen in females.
Table 2: Overall Survival Rates

F = female; M = male
aNo GS-6207-related effect on survival was noted.

Clinical Signs
Cageside observations were conducted once daily. Detailed observations were conducted at
predose, prior to dosing on day 1, and weekly throughout the study.
Test article administration resulted injection site reactions of raised areas at dosing site A in 12
out of 25 males at 100 mg/kg and in 10 out of 25 males at 300 mg/kg. Additionally at dosing site
B, the area was raised in 12 out of 25 males at 100 mg/kg and in 19 out of 25 males at 300
mg/kg. The raised areas had pale discoloration.
Similar to males, test article administration in females resulted in a raised area at dosing site A in
1 out of 25 females at 100 mg/kg and in 15 out of 25 females at 300 mg/kg. At dosing site B, the
area was raised in 7 out of 25 females at 100 mg/kg and in 18 out of 25 females at 300 mg/kg.
The raised areas had pale discoloration.
Body Weights
Animals were weighed once during predose, prior to dosing on day 1, and weekly thereafter.
There were no test article related effects.
Feed Consumption
Food intake was recorded weekly. Any wet or spilled food was also noted.
There were no test article related effects.
Gross Pathology
Animals were necropsied at the end of the study on day 183 via isoflurane inhalation.
There was a dose related increase in injection site reactions (raised area) for both injections sites
A and B in males and females. This finding correlates to the pseudocyst finding noted during
histopathology analysis.
All other findings were considering sporadic or incidental due to their low incidences, presence in
dosed and control animals, or common in this animal model.
6
Reference ID: 5072516

NDAs # 215973/215974

Reviewer: Deacqunita L. Diggs, Ph.D.

Histopathology
Peer Review Conducted: Yes

(b) (4)

Historical Control Provided for Tumor Incidence: The sponsor did not provide historical
control data from the lab where the study was conducted but stated that findings were within
historical control ranges from the lab as well as published incidence data in RasH2 mice
(Paranjpe et al., 2019)
Subcutaneous Injection site A:
Pseudocyst: There was a dose dependent increase in males at 100 and 300 mg/kg only. In
females there was a dose dependent increase across doses. This finding correlated with
injection site reactions noted during gross pathology.
Subcutaneous Injection site B:
Pseudocyst: There was a dose dependent increase in males and females across doses.
This finding correlated with injection site reactions noted during gross pathology.
Skin:
There was one skin papilloma noted in a single female dosed 300 mg/kg that was
terminated early due to its moribund condition at week 15. The sponsor stated this finding
was within historical control. This finding is unlikely related to the test article because it was
a single incidence and animals that reached their scheduled necropsy of 27 weeks did not
have this finding.

Neoplastic
There were no statistically significant GS-6207-related findings.
Non Neoplastic
There were no statistically significant GS-6207-related findings.

inTg.rasH2 mice in 26-week carcinogenicity studies conducted at a single test facility
during 2004 to 2012 and 2013 to 2018. Toxicol Pathol; 47(1):18-25.

Nonclinical Histopathology:
The Applicant notes that nonclinical studies of rats (TX-200-2014), rabbits (TX-200-2033) and
dogs (TX-200-2047) showed microscopic findings of granulomatous inflammation at
subcutaneous (SC) injection sites. The Applicant notes further that granulomatous inflammation
has been observed after SC and intramuscular implantation of bioactive material, including those
for HIV treatment and concludes that inflammation and foreign body response to the lenacapavir
(LEN) SC depot is an expected host response. (IR response received 9/23/2021, p. 12)
Discussion of Injection Site Reactions/Nodules:
There was no standardized definition of nodule or induration provided to Investigators for the
pivotal clinical trial, GS-US-200-4625 or for the supportive trial, GS-US-200-4334.
The Applicant, however, included the following definitions of induration and nodule in Injection
site manuals for the Phase 1 trials: GS-US-4070, GS-US-200-4538. (IR response received
10/26/2021, pp. 41/65 and 49/65)
Induration: (Palpable thickening, firmness, or hardening of soft tissue). Usually well demarcated
with palpable borders, can be visible (raised or sunken compared to surrounding skin), is often
‘woody’ to touch and has a flat shape (versus the rounder shape of a nodule); it can best be
described by palpation.2
Nodule: Discrete or well-demarcated soft tissue mass or lump that is firm.3

 In the current submission, the Applicant states that…” ‘nodule’ has a commonly understood
clinical definition1 with size range (a larger [0.5- to 5.0-cm], firm lesion raised above the
surface of the surrounding skin).”
 Review of widely used dermatology/internal medicine references is supportive of the
Applicant’s statement that nodule has a commonly understood clinical definition.
Harrison's Principles of Internal Medicine2
Nodule: A larger (0.5–5.0 cm), firm lesion raised above the surface of the surrounding skin. This
differs from a papule only in size (e.g., a large dermal nevomelanocytic nevus).
Papule: A small, solid lesion, <0.5 cm in diameter, raised above the surface of the surrounding
skin and thus palpable (e.g., a closed comedone, or whitehead, in acne).
Bolognia, Jean, L. et al. Dermatology3
Nodule
• Palpable, circumscribed
• Larger volume than papule, usually >1 cm in diameter
• Involves the dermis and/or the subcutis
• Greatest portion may be beneath the skin surface or exophytic
Papule
• Elevated (palpable), circumscribed
• <1 cm in diameter
• Elevation due to increased thickness of the epidermis and/or cells or deposits within the dermis
• May have secondary changes (e.g., scale, crust)
• The profile can be flat-topped (lichenoid), dome-shaped, umbilicated, or verrucous
Fitzpatrick's Dermatology4
Nodule
A nodule is a palpable lesion greater than 1 cm with a domed, spherical or ovoid shape. They may
be solid or cystic. Depending on the anatomic component(s) primarily involved, nodules are of 5
main types: (1) epidermal, (2) epidermal–dermal, (3) dermal, (4) dermal–subdermal, and (5)
as an adverse event following immunization: case definition and guidelines for data collection, analysis, and
presentation. Vaccine. 2004 Jan 26;22(5-6):575-85. doi: 10.1016/j.vaccine.2003.09.005. PMID: 14741147.

subcutaneous. Texture is an important additional feature of nodules: firm, soft, boggy, fluctuant,
etc. Similarly, different surfaces of nodules, such as smooth, keratotic, ulcerated, or fungating, also
help direct diagnostic considerations. Tumor, also sometimes included under the heading of
nodule, may be used to describe a more irregularly shaped mass, benign or malignant.
Papule
A papule is an elevated or depressed lesion less than 1 cm in size, which may be solid or cystic.
Among other characteristics, papules may be further described by their topography. Some
examples include papules that are sessile, pedunculated, dome-shaped, flat-topped, filiform,
mamillated, acuminate (conical), or umbilicated.
 Regarding the current NDAs under review: 215973, 215974
The primary study that supports the safety and efficacy of LEN is the Phase 2/3 study in HTE
(heavily treatment experienced) people with HIV (PWH) (Study GS-US-200-4625), which is
further supported by a Phase 2 study of LEN in treatment-naive PWH (Study GS-US-200-4334).
Supportive safety data are also provided from 2 Phase 1 studies in healthy participants
(Studies GS-US-200-4538 and GS-US-200-5709); these Phase 1 studies also contributed to the
characterization of the pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles of LEN and its metabolites, supporting the
clinical pharmacology of LEN and the dosing interval.
The primary data (for establishing safety and efficacy) is based on 26-week data from GS-US-2004625 (CAPELLA), described by the Applicant as a “partially randomized, placebo-controlled,
double-blind multicenter trial.” The proposed indication, as of the time of this consult (11/17/22 –
labeling sent to applicant 11/04/2022), is “the treatment of (HIV-1) infection in heavily treatment
experienced adults with multidrug resistant HIV-1 infection failing their current antiretroviral
regimen due to resistance, intolerance, or safety considerations.”

Study Endpoints:
The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of subjects in Cohort 1 achieving ≥ 0.5 log10
copies/mL reduction from baseline in HIV-1 RNA at the end of the functional monotherapy
period.
The secondary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of participants in Cohort 1 with plasma HIV-1
RNA < 50 copies/mL and < 200 copies/mL at Weeks 26 and 52 of treatment
based on the US Food and Drug Administration (FDA)-defined snapshot algorithm

7.3.1. Adverse Events (Protocol GS-US-200-4625: Final Amendment 2, Sept 1, 2020, p 65)
Following initiation of study medication, all AEs, regardless of cause or relationship,
throughout the duration of the study, including the protocol-defined follow-up visit, must
be reported on the eCRFs as instructed. All AEs should be followed up until resolution or
until the AE is stable, if possible. Gilead may request that certain AEs be followed beyond
the protocol-defined follow-up period.
11.2.1 Study GS-US-200-4625 Interim Week 26 Clinical Study Report Final, p 87
AEs were coded according to MedDRA Version 23.1.
Treatment-emergent AE was defined as an AE that began on or after the first dose date of
LEN. Multiple AEs were counted only once per participant for the highest severity grade
for each PT. PTs were presented by descending order of the total frequencies.

Day 1 SC, Weeks 26, 52, and every 6 months (26 weeks) thereafter (Week 26 Clinical Study for
trial GS-US-200-4625 Report, p. 32).

 Primary Study Supporting the Safety of LEN (Study GS-US-200-4625) Summary of
Clinical Safety Addendum (2.7.4 p.11/88)
A total of 72 participants were enrolled, 36 into Cohort 1 and 36 into Cohort 2.
Per Applicant:
Of the 72 participants in Cohorts 1 and 2 who received oral LEN;
 72 participants received SC LEN on Day 1 SC
 70 participants (Cohort 1: 36 participants, Cohort 2: 34 participants) received
a second dose of SC LEN at Week 26
 53 participants (Cohort 1: 34 participants, Cohort 2:19 participants) received a third dose
of SC LEN at Week 52.
The median (first quartile [Q1], third quartile [Q3]) duration on study was 591 (532, 675) and 445
(394, 480) days for Cohorts 1 and 2, respectively.

Premature Study Drug Withdrawal: Prior to April 1, 2021, the original NDA data cut-off date,
no study subjects had discontinued study drug due to an AE. However, since that date, one
(b) (6)
participant (
) in Study GS-US-200-4625 prematurely discontinued study drug (day
379) due to treatment-emergent AEs of injection site nodules. The subject was a 56-year-old black
female in the USA. The subject experienced an injection site nodule on Day 18 and on Day 380.
Both nodules were Grade 1 (defined as 2.5 to < 5 cm in diameter OR 6.25 to < 25 cm2 surface area
AND symptoms causing no or minimal interference with usual social and functional activities see
also DAIDS Table in Appendix) and were ongoing (GS-US-200-4625, 90-Day Safety Update,
Listing 4). The investigator reported that “the nodules were uncomfortable, and the subject did not
want the nodules to return.”

 Review and assessment of the Applicant’s Cumulative Review of Injection Site Reactions,
including the Applicant’s assessment of high pH formulations and ISRs. (Focusing on
nodules and indurations)
Overall Conclusion (nodules and indurations):
Nodules and indurations were seen commonly across trials in the development program,
affecting subjects in a fashion generally similar to that seen in the pivotal trial GS-US-2004625 [nodule (25%), induration (15%)]. Nodules and indurations generally persisted longer
than other injection site reactions.
More details are provided in Appendix D.

Overall conclusion regarding the Applicant’s assessment of high pH formulations
Review of the applicant’s presented nonclinical data for LEN, clinical data for LEN and
clinical data of other approved ARVs does not identify a clear association between high
formulation pH and duration of nodules and indurations.
More details are provided in Appendix D.
