 Review of Applicant’s Responses to FDA Additional Comments in Complete Response
Letters dated February 28, 2022: 2.2.1 Clinical Comment 6
The Applicant states that information submitted (literature and nonclinical and clinical
evidence from injectable ARVs) support the position that ISRs, including nodules and
induration, most likely result from the presence of the drug depot rather than the potential
presence of subvisible glass particulates in the closure system with borosilicate glass vials.
This reviewer agrees that this is a reasonable conclusion. More details are provided in
Appendix E.
 Applicant’s assessment of injection site reactions in “Cumulative Review of Injection Site
Reactions” (Seq 0015, m1.11.3, Section 2.2, p 62)
The Applicant states that: “Given the majority of ISRs are of low grade, rarely lead to
discontinuation, and frequently resolve over a short period of time, the benefit:risk of LEN
remains favorable and consistent with Gilead’s overall assessment of ISRs previously provided
to the Agency (Seq 0015, m1.11.3, Section 2.2).” (Response to IR of 10/12/2021 received
10/26/202, pp. 11, 12). See below for Applicant’s previously provided overall assessment:

“To summarize, we have conducted a comprehensive assessment of injection site reactions
(ISRs) across the LEN program, including detailed review of individual cases with the
investigators. Our overall assessment is as follows. In Studies GS-US-200-4625 and GS-US200-4334, approximately half or fewer of the participants (55.6% and 38.8%, respectively)
experienced at least 1 AEs of LEN-related ISRs, which were mostly non-serous, Grade 1 or
2, and rarely led to study drug discontinuation. Most AEs of ISRs resolved within days, but
those of induration and nodules lasted longer (as described in Gilead's previous response,
Seq 0008). These AEs of induration or nodules were generally small (less than a few
centimeters), not visible to the participant or the investigator, not causing persistent
discomfort to participants, and only detectable upon deep palpation. There were rarely any
gross inflammatory components, such as tenderness or erythema, to these AEs of induration
or nodules, and when there was coincident pain or erythema, they resolved quickly. There
were no reports of tissue destruction (e.g., necrosis), or, upon careful review, suggestion of
other more serious reactions such as sterile abscess or persistent tissue damage. No
participants appeared to have any safety signal suggesting that LEN was being administered
into a deeper tissue than the intended subcutaneous space (e.g., muscle). In written, verbal,
and video communication with investigators who took care of participants with AEs of
persistent induration or nodule, none were concerned with any potential long term permanent
adverse effect, none felt the AEs required additional consultation (e.g., with a dermatologist),
and all agreed that those AEs were resolving slowly over time. In general investigators
commented on the benign nature of nodules, with some noting that nodules might be more
visible in a person who is lean and self-conscious of body image.”
Nodules:
“Based on the totality of Gilead's nonclinical assessment, the induration or nodules after SC
LEN injection appears to be a normal and expected tissue body response, manifesting as
chronic granulomatous inflammation histologically, to a foreign body (i.e., a drug depot)
18

Reference ID: 5101026
5080345

DDD Consult #2133

without permanent adverse effect. We have not found evidence of injection site nodules or
induration being reflective of more serious complications, or resulting from aberrant
injections, nor have we found evidence that nodules are related to body habitus or
immunologic status.... In summary, our assessment is that nodules that rise to the level of AE
reporting occur in a subset of individuals receiving LEN, represent subcutaneous deposition of the
drug itself and expected tissue reaction, and are not associated with adverse clinical or
pharmacologic consequences.”
 This reviewer finds the Applicant’s assessment, especially as it relates to injection site nodules
and indurations, reasonable.

 Labeling:
 Labeling: Other Products
Approved Drug Product Labeling:
Approved FDA labeling for a number of drug products includes the adverse reaction of injection
site nodules. For some products information regarding injection site nodules is present in Warnings
and Precautions. Among the most extensive descriptions of injection site nodules occurs in
approved FDA labeling for exenatide injection, suspension, extended-release product
BYDUREON BCISE® (see Appendix B).

Approved labeling CABENUVA (cabotegravir extended-release injectable suspension;
rilpivirine extended-release injectable suspension) 1/2021: Section 6.1 Clinical Trials
Experience
“The most commonly reported ISR was localized pain/discomfort (79%) regardless of severity or
relatedness. Other manifestations of ISR’s reported in more than 1% of subjects over the duration
of the analysis period included nodules (14%), induration (12%), swelling (8%), erythema (4%),
pruritus (4%), bruising (3%), warmth (2%), and hematoma (2%).”
Approved labeling FUZEON (enfuvirtide) 8/2019: Section 6.1 Clinical Trials Experience
“The majority of ISRs were associated with erythema, induration, the presence of nodules or cysts,
and mild to moderate pain at the injection site (Table 2)”: pain/discomfort (96%), induration
(90%), erythema (91%), nodules and cysts (80%), pruritus (65%), and ecchymosis (52%),
including participants with Grade 3 or 4 ISRs.

Appendix B: Examples of Labeling Related to Injection Site Nodules
BYDUREON BCISE® (exenatide extended-release) injectable suspension, for subcutaneous
use. Initial U.S. Approval: 2005
5.9 Injection-Site Reactions There have been postmarketing reports of serious injection-site
reactions (e.g., abscess, cellulitis, and necrosis), with or without subcutaneous nodules, with the
use of BYDUREON [see Adverse Reactions (6.3)]. Isolated cases required surgical intervention.
6.1 Clinical Trial Experience

Adverse Reactions Leading to Discontinuation of Therapy
The incidence of discontinuation of therapy due to adverse reactions was 3.9% for BYDUREON
BCISE-treated patients in the two comparator-controlled 28-week trials in adults. The most
common classes of adverse reactions leading to discontinuation of therapy for BYDUREON
BCISE-treated patients were Gastrointestinal Disorders 2.0% and General Disorders and
Administration Site Conditions 1.2%. For BYDUREON BCISE-treated patients, the most frequent
adverse reactions leading to discontinuation of therapy within each of these respective classes were
diarrhea (0.7%), nausea (0.7%), vomiting (0.5%) and injection-site nodule (0.5%).
Injection-Site Adverse Reactions In the two comparator-controlled 28-week trials in adults,
injection site reactions (including injection site nodule, injection site pruritus, injection site
bruising) were observed in 23.9% of patients treated with BYDUREON BCISE. The formation of
subcutaneous nodules is consistent with the properties of the microspheres used in BYDUREON
BCISE.
Other drug products having labeling describing injection site nodules in Section 5: Warnings and
Precautions include; granisetron (SUSTOL), enfuvirtide (FUZEON), and medroxyprogesterone
acetate (DEPO-SUBQ-PROVERA).

include; cabotegravir extended-release injectable suspension;rilpivirine extended-release injectable

CABENUVA (cabotegravir extended-release injectable; rilpivirine extended-release
injectable suspension), co-packaged for intramuscular use
Initial U.S. Approval: 2021
Section 6.1 Clinical Trials Experience
Injection-Associated Adverse Reactions
Local Injection Site Reactions (ISRs): The most frequent adverse reactions associated with the
intramuscular administration of CABENUVA were ISRs. After 14,682 injections, 3,663 ISRs were
reported. One percent (1%) of subjects discontinued treatment with CABENUVA because of ISRs.
Most ISRs were mild (Grade 1, 75%) or moderate (Grade 2, 36%). Four percent (4%) of subjects
experienced severe (Grade 3) ISRs, and no subjects experienced Grade 4 ISRs. The most
commonly reported ISR was localized pain/discomfort (79%) regardless of severity or relatedness.
Other manifestations of ISRs reported in more than 1% of subjects over the duration of the
analysis period included nodules (14%),* induration (12%), swelling (8%), erythema (4%),
pruritus (4%), bruising (3%), warmth (2%), and hematoma (2%). Abscess and cellulitis at the
injection site were each reported in less than 1% of subjects. The median duration of ISR events
was 3 days.

 Comments Regarding the Effects of Formulation pH:
The question arises whether high pH could be contributing to long duration of indurations and
nodules.
The applicant’s assessment of high pH formulations and ISRs: submitted in module 5.3.5.3 in the
submission dated 6, 27, 2022 (SDN#38) includes:
 Nonclinical Data for LEN
 Clinical Data for LEN:
 Clinical Data of Other Approved antiretrovirals (ARVs):
Nonclinical Data for LEN
The applicant describes a number of non-clinical studies regarding the effect of pH on chronic
granulomatous inflammation and foreign body response at the SC injection sites.
37

Reference ID: 5101026
5080345

DDD Consult #2133

●A single-dose SC injection local tolerance study (TX-200-2019) of LEN using a solution of LEN
free acid (approximately pH 7) and LEN sodium (approximately pH 9.5) was conducted in
rabbits. Edema correlated with macroscopic observation of thickening at the injection site and
microscopic observation of granulomatous inflammation were observed on Day 92. The
macroscopic and microscopic findings did not differ between the different LEN formulations
(approximately pH 7 or approximately pH 9.5). The granulomatous inflammation decreased in
incidence and/or severity by Day 183 and Day 274, suggestive of a process of maturation or
recovery.
●A single- (Day 1) and second-dose (Day 1 and Day 92) SC injection toxicity study with a
13-week observation period (TX-200-2014) of LEN using a solution of LEN free acid
(approximately pH 7.0) and LEN sodium (approximately pH 9.5) was conducted in rats.
LEN-related edema (very slight to moderate), macroscopic thickening and/or tan
discoloration, correlative granulomatous inflammation (minimal to moderate; with or without
necrosis), and macrophage infiltrate were present at the SC injection sites of animals
administered either formulation of LEN (approximately pH 7 and approximately pH 9.5).
Evidence of LEN-related granulomatous inflammation reversibility included decreased severity
relative to SC injection site(s) used more recently, and/or resolution to a minimal macrophage
infiltrate.
●A 9-month (once monthly) SC injection toxicity study (TX-200-2017) of LEN using a solution
of LEN free acid (approximately pH 7.0) and LEN sodium (approximately pH 9.5) was
conducted in dogs. Lenacapavir-related microscopic observations of necrosis, granulomatous
inflammation, mixed cell inflammation, and/or macrophage infiltrate were observed at the SC
injection sites of animals administered either formulation of LEN (approximately pH 7.0 and
approximately pH 9.5). At each sacrifice interval, granulomatous inflammation, with variable
central necrosis, was generally observed in all but the last site dosed and was considered
indicative of the maturation/recovery process. In the oldest injection sites, inflammation had
recovered to a macrophage infiltrate in some animals. Overall, an increased interval since dosing
was generally associated with a successive decrease in the overall incidence/severity of
microscopic findings.
●A SC injection local tolerance study (TX-200-2033) of LEN using a solution of LEN free acid
(pH 7.1) and LEN sodium (pH 9.4) was conducted in rabbits. On Day 4, LEN-related
microscopic findings at the injection site consisted of slight to marked necrosis, minimal to
moderate mixed cell inflammation, and minimal to slight mononuclear cell infiltrates, that
correlated with the macroscopic observation of thickening in all animals administered LEN with
either formulation (pH 7.1 and pH 9.4). On Day 29, LEN-related microscopic findings of marked
granulomatous inflammation and slight to marked necrosis correlated with the macroscopic
observation of thickening in all animals administered LEN. Overall, the incidence and severity of
mixed cell and/or granulomatous inflammation and necrosis were similar between dose levels
and formulations, regardless of pH. LEN-related edema correlated with the macroscopic
observation of thickening and the microscopic observations of mixed cell or granulomatous
inflammation, necrosis, and mononuclear cell infiltrates at the SC injection site of animals
administered LEN.

38

Reference ID: 5101026
5080345

DDD Consult #2133

●A SC injection local tolerance study (TX-200-2010) of LEN (LEN was suspended in vehicle
(b) (4)
pH 7.4) was conducted in rabbits (NDA 215973, Seq 0001, m4.2.3.6).
Lenacapavir-related dermal observations of very slight edema, very slight to well-defined
erythema, and desquamation were noted on Day 4; these findings were resolved by Day 29.
These observations were minor and recovered by the end of the study; therefore, they were
considered not adverse. On Day 4, LEN-related microscopic findings included slight or marked
granulomatous inflammation of the subcutis and slight to moderate granulomatous inflammation
of the dermis. SC granulomatous inflammation correlated with the macroscopic observation of
white discoloration. On Day 29, LEN-related findings of minimal to moderate granulomatous
inflammation of the subcutis and was correlated with the macroscopic observation of white
discolorations. Minimal mixed cell infiltrates of the dermis were present. No granulomatous
inflammation of the dermis was present, indicating reversal.
Non-clinical conclusion:
The Applicant states that their available nonclinical data suggest that chronic granulomatous
inflammation and foreign body response at the SC injection site are expected to occur for any
antiretroviral compound including LEN due to the presence of a SC drug depot, and irrespective
of the pH of the formulation.
This reviewer agrees this is a reasonable conclusion.
Clinical Data for LEN:
From Applicant presented information:
The same SC formulation of LEN 309 mg/mL (2 x 1.5 mL injection – 927mg, initial (pH = 9.0 to
10.0)
was used in a total of 219 participants across a Phase 1 study in healthy participants (Study
GS-US-200-5709, n = 44), a Phase 2 study in treatment-naive PWH (Study GS-US-200-4334,
n = 103) and a Phase 2/3 study in heavily treatment-experienced PWH with multidrug resistance
(Study GS-US-200-4625, n = 72).
Most AEs of ISRs resolved within days, but those of indurations and nodules lasted longer
(median [Q1, Q3]: for resolved = 70 [15, 196] and 148 [70, 307] days in Study GS-US-200-4625,
for resolved = 278 [136, 366] and 202 [101, 213] days in Study GS-US-200-4334, and for resolved
= 27 [15, 127] and 421 [113, 421] in Study GS-US-200-5709, respectively). There were rarely any
gross inflammatory components, such as tenderness or erythema, to these AEs of indurations or
nodules, and when there was coincident pain or erythema, they resolved quickly. Investigators
treating participants with AEs of persistent indurations or nodules reported that those AEs were
resolving slowly over time. In general, investigators commented on the benign nature of the
nodules.
A SC aqueous formulation of LEN free acid suspension (pH 7.1) was used in a total of
61 participants in one Phase 1 trial in healthy participants (Study GS-US-200-4070, n = 32),
and a Phase 1b proof-of-concept trial in treatment-naive PWH (Study GS-US-200-4072,
n = 29). In trial GS-US-200-4070, single doses 30 mg, 100mg, 300mg, and 450 mg were
examined. In trial GS-US-200-4072, single doses 150mg, 50 mg, 20 mg, and multiple doses 450
mg, and 750 mg were examined.
39

Reference ID: 5101026
5080345

DDD Consult #2133

Nodules and indurations were reported in 28.1% and 25.0% of participants in Study GS-US-2004070, and 20.7% and 24.1% of participants in Study GS-US-200-4072, respectively. Median (Q1,
Q3) duration (days) of resolved indurations and nodules was 4 (2, 12), 51 (7, 169) in Study
GS-US-200-4070 and 7 (6, 45), 49 (4, 225) in Study GS-US-200-4072.
Clinical conclusion:
Regarding nodules and indurations, the data from the trials described above is suggestive that
nodules and indurations persist longer in the trials with initially higher pH (initial pH = 9.0 to
10.0). However, doses examined in in the lower pH trials were generally lower than those
examined in the higher pH trials. Additionally, the number of subjects (sample size) in the lower
pH trials is smaller, 61 versus 219, for the higher pH trials. It is noted also that non-clinical
findings presented by the applicant do not show a significant pH effect.
Clinical Data of Other Approved ARVs:
The Applicant notes that other approved ARVs for SC or intramuscular (IM) injections have been
associated with similar or greater incidence of ISRs.
● Approved labeling CABENUVA (cabotegravir extended-release injectable suspension;
rilpivirine extended-release injectable suspension) 1/2021: Section 6.1 Clinical Trials
Experience
“The most commonly reported ISR was localized pain/discomfort (79%) regardless of
severity or relatedness. Other manifestations of ISR’s reported in more than 1% of subjects
over the duration of the analysis period included nodules (14%), induration (12%),
swelling (8%), erythema (4%), pruritus (4%), bruising (3%), warmth (2%), and hematoma
(2%).”
● Approved labeling FUZEON (enfuvirtide) 8/2019: Section 6.1 Clinical Trials Experience
“The majority of ISRs were associated with erythema, induration, the presence of nodules
or cysts, and mild to moderate pain at the injection site (Table 2)”: pain/discomfort (96%),
induration (90%), erythema (91%), nodules and cysts (80%), pruritus (65%), and
ecchymosis (52%), including participants with Grade 3 or 4 ISRs.
Data for Other SC Administered Alkaline Products
The Applicant notes that limited data are available in the published literature describing the safety
risk to patients who have received SC injections of an alkaline product.

Overall Conclusion regarding effect of pH:
Review of the applicant’s presented nonclinical data for LEN, clinical data for LEN and clinical
data of other approved ARVs does not identify a clear association between high formulation pH
and duration of nodules and indurations.

 Appendix E: Review of Response to FDA Additional Comments (specifically Comment 6) in
Complete Response Letters dated 28 February 2022 (included in consult email dated 7/18/22)
2.2.1. Comment 6
Please submit a complete assessment of the extent to which glass particulate contamination,
including sub-visible particles, may be associated with injection site nodules and indurations or
other clinical effects. In addition, submit independent dermatologist evaluation assessments for
subjects in Study GS-US-200-4625 or Study GS-US-200-4334 who met Grade 3 or higher adverse
event injection site reaction criteria, or who had any injection site reactions (particularly
indurations or nodules) that are not resolved within 24 weeks.
 The Applicant provided a response that includes:
 Evidence from the literature
 Nonclinical data for LEN and other antiretrovirals (ARVs)
 Assessment of ISRs from clinical studies of LEN and other ARVs.
● Literature:
Pertinent information provided by the applicant includes a study (Dorris1) wherein dogs were
randomized to control (with in-line filtration) and experimental (without in-line filtration) groups
and received concentrated solutions of particles. When particles were injected by the SC route, no
inflammatory response was observed in either group.
The Applicant also provided information that includes the observation that granulomatous
inflammation has been observed after SC and IM implantation of bioactive material, including
those for HIV treatment (Anderson 19972, Shah 20173, Van’t Klooster 20104).
● Non-clinical data for LEN and other ARVs:
A single-dose SC injection local tolerance study (Gilead Sciences: TX-200-2019) of LEN in
(b)
rabbits was conducted using LEN formulations which were pharmacy compounded at the site (4)
, and therefore were not expected to contain any
intrinsic glass particulates resulting from an interaction between the LEN injection formulation and
the borosilicate glass vials. Non-adverse edema correlated with macroscopic observation of
thickening at the injection site and microscopic observation of granulomatous inflammation
observed on Day 92. The granulomatous inflammation decreased in incidence and/or severity by
Day 183 and Day 274, indicative of the maturation/recovery process.

1. Background
In this submission, the sponsor included a carcinogenicity study report in mice. This study was intended to
(b)
assess the carcinogenic potential of Sunlenca (Lenacapavir): GS-6207 (4) (Na salt, hereafter referred to as
GS-6207) when administered once every 13 weeks by subcutaneous injection (total of 2 doses) to 001178-T
(hemizygous) RasH2 mice, with a 13-week observation period following each dose. Results of this review
have been discussed with the reviewing pharmacologist Dr. Diggs.
In this review, the phrase "dose response relationship" (trend) refers to the linear component of the effect of
treatment, and not necessarily to a strictly increasing or decreasing mortality or tumor incidence rate as dose
increases.
2. Mouse Study
Two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. In each of these two
experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive control group.
One hundred and ten rasH2 transgenic mice of each sex were assigned randomly to one of the five groups
by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in equal size
of 25 animals except the positive control group which had 10 animals. The dose levels for the three
treated groups were at 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg/dose, respectively, via SC injection once every 13 weeks
(total of 2 doses), for both males and females, as indicated in Table 1. In this review, these dose groups
would be referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The positive control group
was administered with one intraperitoneal dose of 75 mg/kg N-methyl-N-nitrosourea on Day 1 of the
dosing phase. This group was included to verify sensitivity of the test system to detect carcinogenicity
(b) (6)
(b) (4)
effect. The vehicle control group received
% w/w polyethylene glycol 300 and
% w/w sterile water for injection and were administered in the same manner as the treated
groups.

Animals in group 5 were dosed with one intraperitoneal dose of positive control article on Day 1 of the dosing phase.
Group 1, 2, 3, and 4, were dosed via SC injection once every 13 weeks (total of 2 doses)

During the administration period, all animals were checked for morbidity, mortality, injury, twice daily
(a.m. and p.m.), abnormal findings were recorded for toxicity animals as they were observed. Cage side
observations were conducted for each carcinogenicity animal once daily during the dosing phase, except
on days when detailed observations were conducted. The animals were removed from the cage, and
detailed observations were conducted for each animal once during the pre-dose phase, on Day 1, and
weekly (based on Day 1) thereafter to Week 26, and on Day 182 of the dosing phase. Detailed
observations were also collected for each carcinogenicity animal on the day of scheduled sacrifice (Day
183, only those animals scheduled for sacrifice on that day). Abnormal findings or an indication of
normal was recorded. Detailed examinations for palpable masses were done weekly, the time of onset,
location, size, appearance, and progression of each grossly visible or palpable mass, observed in

Reference ID: 5052595

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (Lenacapavir)

Page 4 of 14

carcinogenicity mice, were recorded, any animal showing signs of severe debility or intoxication, and if
determined to be moribund or suffering excessively was euthanized. Observations included, but will not
be limited to, evaluation for reaction to treatment. Histopathological examinations were performed on all
animals found dead or killed moribund or sacrificed at the end of the experiment. Body weights were
recorded for carcinogenicity animals once during the predose phase, before dosing on Day 1, and weekly
(based on Day 1) thereafter to Week 26 and on Day 182.
2.1.

Sponsor's analyses

2.1.1. Survival analysis
In the sponsor’s analysis, the tests for survival comparisons were performed with a two-sided risk for
increasing and decreasing mortality with dose. Tests were performed for dose response and for each
dosed group against control using Kaplan-Meier product-limit estimates, along with log-rank and
Wilcoxon tests. These were performed using LIFETEST procedure in SAS. The time to death or sacrifice
(in weeks) was the dependent variable. Treatment group was included as the strata. Animals with a death
or sacrifice status recorded as a planned sacrifice (interim or terminal) or an accidental death were
censored in the analysis.
Sponsor’s findings:
Sponsor’s analysis showed the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy were 24 (96%), 24
(96%), 19 (76%), 23 (92%), and 3 (30%) in the vehicle control, low, medium, high, and positive control
groups, in male mice, respectively, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 21 (84%), 22 (88%), and 4 (40%) in the
vehicle control, low, medium, high, and positive control groups, in females, respectively. The sponsor’s
report concluded that, there were statistically significant higher mortality in the medium dose group, when
compared to the vehicle control group in male mice (p-value = 0.0423 for the Log-Rank test and P =
0.0409 for the Wilcoxon test.).
2.1.2. Tumor data analysis
In the sponsor’s analysis, tests to compare tumor incidence were performed, with a one-sided risk for
increasing or decreasing incidence with dose. Tests were performed for dose response and for each dosed
group against the vehicle control group. Occult or non-palpable and fatal tumors were analyzed by the
IARC asymptotic fixed interval-based prevalence test and death-rate test (Peto et al., 1980). The cut-off
points for the interval-based test were Weeks 1 to 13, 14 to before terminal sacrifice, and the terminal
sacrifice. Actual target dose levels were used as the scores for all groups. Fatal and non-fatal tumors were
analyzed together, with separate stratum for each. Tumors of uncertain context were included in the
analysis as non-fatal. The test was implemented using PROC MULTTEST in the SAS system (SAS,
2008). In the case of sparse tables (<10 total tumor bearing animals across the groups analyzed for the
trend or pairwise test), the exact form of the test was used. Otherwise, the asymptotic version of the test
was used. For each given tumor type, a statistical analysis was performed if the incidence in at least one
dosed group was increased by at least two occurrences over the vehicle control group.
Observable or palpable (superficial as in mammary or skin) tumors were analyzed using the methods
previously described for analyzing survival (on-set rate method described in Peto et al., 1980), using the
time to death or time of detection of the tumor (in weeks) as a surrogate for the tumor onset time.

Reference ID: 5052595

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (Lenacapavir)

Page 5 of 14

Adjustment for the multiplicity:
For multiplicity adjustment testing, the sponsor did not present any adjustment, unadjusted P-values were
reported, and statistically significant results are given at the 5% level.
Sponsor’s findings:
The sponsor’s analysis showed statistically significant increasing dose response relationships at the 5%
level, for the incidence of benign papilloma, squamous cell in skin/subcutis in female mice (p-value =
0.0498 for the Log-Rank test and P = 0.0498 for the Wilcoxon test.).
2.2

Reviewer's analyses

To verify sponsor’s analysis and to perform additional analyses suggested by the reviewing pharmacologist,
this reviewer independently performed the survival and tumor data analyses. Data used in this reviewer's
analyses were provided by the sponsor electronically on June 27, 2022, via SN0038.
2.2.1

Survival analysis

In the reviewer’s analysis, intercurrent mortality data were analyzed using the Kaplan-Meier product limit
method. The Kaplan-Meier’s curves were presented graphically for male and female mice separately. The
dose response relationship and homogeneity of survival distributions were tested for the treatment groups
using the Likelihood Ratio test and the Log-Rank test. The intercurrent mortality data are given in Tables
1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively. The Kaplan-Meier curves for survival
rate are given in Figures 1A and 1B in the appendix for male and female mice, respectively. Results of the
tests for dose response relationship and homogeneity of survivals, are given in Tables 2A and 2B in the
appendix for male and female mice, respectively.
Reviewer’s findings:
This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy were 24 (96%),
24 (96%), 19 (76%), 23 (92%), and 3 (30%) in the vehicle control, low, medium, high, and positive
control groups, in male mice, respectively, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 21 (84%), 22 (88%), and 4 (40%) in
the vehicle control, low, medium, high, and positive control groups, in females, respectively. The
reviewer’s report showed no statistically significant dose response relationship in the mortality of both
male and female mice. The pairwise comparisons showed a statistically significant increase in mortality
between the medium dose group and the vehicle control group in male mice (p-value = 0.0343).
2.2.2. Tumor data analysis
In the reviewer’s analysis, the tumor data were analyzed for dose response relationship across vehicle
control group and the treated groups, as well as the pairwise comparisons of vehicle control group with
each of the treated groups using the Poly-k method described in the paper of Bailer and Portier (1988) and
Bieler and Williams (1993). In this method, an animal that lives the full study period ( wmax ) or dies before
the terminal sacrifice with development of the tumor type being tested gets a score of s h =1. An animal that
dies at Week w h without development of the given tumor type before the end of the study gets a score of

Reference ID: 5052595

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (Lenacapavir)

Page 6 of 14

k

 w 
s h =  h  <1. The adjusted group size is defined as Σ s h . As an interpretation, an animal with score
 wmax 
s h =1 can be considered as a whole animal, while an animal with score s h <1 can be considered as a partial
animal. The adjusted group size Σ s h is equal to N (the original group size) if all animals live up to the end of
the study or if each animal develops the given tumor being tested, otherwise the adjusted group size is less
than N. These adjusted group sizes are then used for the dose response relationship (or the pairwise
comparison) tests using the Cochran-Armitage test. One critical point for Poly-k test is the choice of the
appropriate value of k. For long term 104-week standard rat study, a value of k=3 is suggested in the
literature [Gebregziabher and Hoel (2009), Moon et al. (2003), Portier, et al. (1986)]. Hence, this reviewer
used k=3 for the analysis of the data. Based on the intent to treat (ITT) principle Wmax was considered as
105 for both male and female mice.
For the calculation of p-values, if there were less than 10 tumors bearing animals across all treatment
groups for a given tumor type, the exact tests based on the discrete permutation distribution were used,
with dose levels (0, 30, 100, and 300 for male mice and female mice) as scores, and asymptotic tests were
used for tumor types with higher incidences. The tumor rates and the p-values of the tested tumor types
are listed in Tables 3A and 3B in the appendix for male mice and female mice, respectively.
Multiple testing adjustments:
Following the FDA more recently revised draft guidance for the carcinogenicity study design and data
analysis, for the 26 weeks mouse study this reviewer used significance levels of 0.05 for common and
rare tumors, for dose response relationship (trend) tests and for pairwise comparisons.
A tumor is defined as a rare tumor if the published spontaneous rate or the spontaneous rate of the vehicle
control of the tumor is less than 1%, and a common tumor is defined as one with tumor rate greater than
or equal to 1%.
Reviewer’s findings:
Following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, this reviewer’s analysis showed no
tumor types with a statistically significant dose response relationship in tumor incidences with increased
Sunlenca (Lenacapavir) dose in both male and female mice. The pairwise comparisons test also showed
no statistically significant increases in tumor incidences in any groups, when compared to the vehicle
control group in both male and female mice.
The pairwise comparisons showed statistically significant increases in the positive control group for the
incidences of malignant lymphoma in hematolymphoid system, benign papilloma, squamous cell in the
skin/subcutis, benign papilloma, squamous cell in stomach nonglandular, when compared to the vehicle
control group in both male and female mice (p-values <0.0001, =0.0010, =0.0010, for males, and
<0.0001, =0.0002, =0.0002, for females, respectively). Also, the pairwise comparisons showed
statistically significant increases in the positive control group for the incidences of malignant carcinoma,
Squamous Cell in stomach nonglandular in male mice, (p-values =0.0423).
3. Summary
In this submission, the sponsor included a carcinogenicity study report in mice. This study was intended to
(b)
assess the carcinogenic potential of Sunlenca (Lenacapavir): GS-6207 (4) (Na salt, hereafter referred to as

Reference ID: 5052595

NDA 215973 Sunlenca (Lenacapavir)

Page 7 of 14

GS-6207) when administered once every 13 weeks by subcutaneous injection (total of 2 doses) to 001178-T
(hemizygous) RasH2 mice, with a 13-week observation period following each dose.
Mouse Study:
In this study two separate experiments were conducted, one in male mice and one in female mice. In each of
these two experiments there were three treated groups, one vehicle control group, and one positive control
group. One hundred and ten rasH2 transgenic mice of each sex were assigned randomly to one of the five
groups by a stratified randomization scheme designed to achieve similar group mean body weights in
equal size of 25 animals except the positive control group which had 10 animals. The dose levels for the
three treated groups were at 30, 100, or 300 mg/kg/dose, respectively, via SC injection once every 13
weeks (total of 2 doses), for both males and females, as indicated in Table 1. In this review, these dose
groups would be referred to as the low, medium, and high dose group, respectively. The positive control
group was administered with one intraperitoneal dose of 75 mg/kg N-methyl-N-nitrosourea on Day 1 of
the dosing phase. This group was included to verify sensitivity of the test system to detect carcinogenicity
(b) (4)
(b) (6)
effect. The vehicle control group received
% w/w polyethylene glycol 300 and
% w/w sterile water for injection, were administered in the same manner as the treated groups.
This reviewer’s analysis showed the numbers of mice surviving to their terminal necropsy were 24 (96%), 24
(96%), 19 (76%), 23 (92%), and 3 (30%) in the vehicle control, low, medium, high, and positive control
groups, in male mice, respectively, and 24 (96%), 24 (96%), 21 (84%), 22 (88%), and 4 (40%) in the vehicle
control, low, medium, high, and positive control groups, in females, respectively. The reviewer’s report
showed no statistically significant dose response relationship in the mortality of both male and female mice.
The pairwise comparisons showed a statistically significant increase in mortality between the medium dose
group and the vehicle control group in male mice (p-value = 0.0343).
For tumor data, following the multiple testing adjustment method described above, this reviewer’s analysis
showed no tumor types with a statistically significant dose response relationship in tumor incidences with
increased Sunlenca (Lenacapavir) dose in both male and female mice. The pairwise comparisons test also
showed no statistically significant increases in tumor incidences in any groups, when compared to the vehicle
control group in both male and female mice.
The pairwise comparisons showed statistically significant increases in the positive control group for the
incidences of malignant lymphoma in hematolymphoid system, benign papilloma, squamous cell in the
skin/subcutis, benign papilloma, squamous cell in stomach nonglandular, when compared to the vehicle
control group in both male and female mice (p-values <0.0001, =0.0010, =0.0010, for males, and <0.0001,
=0.0002, =0.0002, for females, respectively). Also, the pairwise comparisons showed statistically significant
increases in the positive control group for the incidences of malignant carcinoma, Squamous Cell in stomach
nonglandular in male mice, (p-values =0.0423).

I. Executive Summary
1. Summary of Regulatory Action
These new drug applications (NDAs) for both oral (NDA 215974) and injectable formulations
(NDA 215973) of lenacapavir (LEN) were submitted by Gilead Sciences. LEN is a first-in-class
human immunodeficiency type 1 (HIV-1) capsid inhibitor with Breakthrough Therapy
Designation. These NDAs were reviewed by the interdisciplinary review team.
The intended indication for LEN is to be used in combination with other antiretroviral(s) for the
treatment of HIV-1 infection in heavily treatment-experienced adult patients with multidrug
resistant HIV-1 infection failing their current antiretroviral regimen due to resistance,
intolerance, or safety considerations.
The clinical, virology, clinical pharmacology, pharmacology/toxicology, statistics, and
regulatory disciplines did not identify issues that preclude approval. Product quality deficiencies
were identified which include: (1) product quality information to support the proposed container
presentation in borosilicate glass vials was not acceptable for commercial use due to
delamination of the vial containers and concern for glass particulate contamination, (2) data
supporting aluminosilicate glass vials as an alternative were insufficient for full review. The
Product Quality assessment team recommends a complete response based on these deficiencies.
I, the signatory authority for these applications, concur with the complete response
recommendation and therefore the Agency will issue a complete response for both NDAs
because the approval of NDA 215974 is dependent on the approval of NDA 215973. The
proposed proprietary name, Sunlenca, was found acceptable only pending approval of the
application; therefore, the Applicant will need to resubmit their proposed name when the
deficiencies are addressed. Please refer to the Complete Response letter for further details.
The Applicant submitted one adequately designed phase 2/3 trial, Study 4625, that provided
substantial evidence of efficacy for the proposed indication. Supportive evidence of efficacy was
provided by Study 4334.
The available safety data from the clinical trials demonstrate that LEN is safe for its intended
use. I concur that the risks identified in the review of the clinical trials’ data can be mitigated
through labeling and further evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.
The long-acting properties of the LEN injectable formulation have potential advantages and
disadvantages. The long-acting injectable formulation can be administered every 6 months in
combination with other active antiretrovirals. However, because residual concentrations of LEN
can remain for prolonged periods, careful selection of patients who agree to the injection dosing
schedule is imperative. Nonadherence to the injection dosing schedule can lead to loss of
virologic response and development of resistance to this new drug class.
For detailed information supporting the basis for the benefit-risk assessment please refer to the
details in this Integrated Assessment document. Please also refer to the Product Quality Reviews
for the deficiencies observed resulting in the overall Complete Response action.
4
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

• The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC)
estimates that approximately 1.2 million people in the U.S.
are living with HIV. In 2019, there were 15,815 deaths
among people diagnosed with HIV (CDC 2021).
• Maximal and durable suppression of HIV-1 RNA restores
and/or preserves the immune system and reduces HIVassociated morbidity and mortality. Most treatment-adherent
patients with limited previous HIV treatment experience can
maximally and durably suppress HIV-1 RNA using a
combination of two or more commercially available drugs
from different drug classes.
• Treatment failure occurs as a result of drug resistance, drug
intolerance, and suboptimal adherence. Resistanceassociated substitution(s) selected by one drug often
confer(s) resistance to other drugs within that drug class,
which further limits treatment options.
• Heavily treatment-experienced (HTE) patients have limited
remaining options for constructing a fully suppressive
antiretroviral (ARV) regimen due to multidrug resistance,
intolerance, or safety considerations.

• A review of deaths, serious adverse events (SAEs), and

Conclusions and Reasons

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
Infection with HIV-1 continues to be a significant public health concern both domestically and
internationally. Although the HIV treatment armamentarium is robust, additional effective ARV
treatment options that provide greater flexibility in meeting patients’ individual needs and
facilitating their tolerance for and adherence to lifelong HIV-1 treatment are needed.
HTE patients infected with MDR HIV-1 represent a rare but important subset of patients living
with HIV. Patients with MDR HIV-1 who cannot achieve complete virologic suppression with
antiretroviral therapy (ART) are at high risk for AIDS-related morbidity and mortality. This
population needs new and effective ARV products that lack cross-resistance with commercially
available products, have a favorable tolerability profile, and allow for convenient dosing. LEN, a
first-in-class HIV-1 capsid inhibitor with Breakthrough Therapy Drug designation, is one such
product. The injectable formulation of LEN provides an option with the advantage of providing a
new mechanism of action and maintenance dosing every six months following a lead-in with oral
tablets. However, the prolonged residual exposure is an important consideration when LEN is
combined with background regimens that have a different dosing schedule and reduced antiviral
activity due to resistance or concerns with adherence.
The submitted evidence clearly demonstrates LEN is effective in combination with an OBR in
patients with limited options due to multi-drug resistance, intolerance or safety concerns with
other ARVs. The pivotal trial, Study 4625 (CAPELLA), demonstrated superiority of LEN
compared to placebo, when added to a failing regimen, for the proportion of subjects achieving a
reduction in HIV-1 RNA of ≥0.5 log10 copies/mL after 14 days of treatment. The lack of a
control group after Day 14 in Study 4625 limits the ability to precisely quantify the contribution
of LEN to long-term virologic suppression. However, the contribution of LEN is reflected by the
relatively high rate of virologic suppression achieved with LEN plus an optimized background
regimen (OBR) through the Week 26 interim analysis in a population previously unable to
achieve virologic suppression. Additional supportive data from the phase 2 study in treatmentnaïve PLWH, Study 4334, demonstrated that regimens containing LEN achieved and maintained
virologic suppression through the Week 28 interim analysis.
The safety evaluation for LEN was adequate and the demonstrated clinical safety profile of LEN
is acceptable for the proposed indication, dose and HTE population. With the exception of local
(b) (4)
ISRs, treatment-emergent resistance to LEN, and
, the safety profile of LEN was generally favorable. Injection site
indurations and nodules remained persistent in several cases and were a significant cause of
study drug discontinuations. The rate of virologic failure reported in Study 4625 reflects the
targeted patient population: heavily treatment-experienced patients with advanced HIV/AIDS
who are failing current ART and have very few remaining treatment options. Longer-term data
will be needed to determine durability of LEN and the other ARVs in the OBR.
With all factors considered, the benefits of LEN when administered with other ARVs clearly
outweigh the risks; however, the product quality information to support the proposed container
presentation in borosilicate glass vials was not acceptable due to incompatibility of the LEN
solution with the vial containers and concern for glass particulate contamination. In addition,
there was lack of sufficient data to rule out any contribution of the product quality issues to ISRs.

3.1.1. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Benefit
3.1.1.1. Imbalance in Study 4625 Baseline Characteristics
•
•

More subjects in the LEN arm of Cohort 1 in Study 4625 had baseline HIV-1 RNA
≤100,000 copies/mL compared to the placebo arm.
The mean baseline CD4+ cell count and CD4+ percentage were higher in the LEN arm than
those in the placebo arm.
3.1.1.2. Justification for the Proposed Simplified Dosing
Regimen

•
•

The Applicant is proposing a simplified dosing regimen that was not studied during the
phase 2/3 LEN clinical development program.
The Applicant used PK data to support the proposed simplified regimen.
3.1.1.3. Impact of Subjects Not Maintainting Eligibility
Criteria: Not Failing on Background Regimen or
Nonadherence to Background Regimen

•

Several subjects did not meet the established enrollment criteria for Study 4625, including
resistance to at least two ARVs from each of at least three of the four main classes or
demonstration of virologic failure despite adherence to a failing background regimen.
3.1.1.4. Dosing Interval and Approach to Missed
Subcutaneous Maintenance Doses

•

The Applicant proposed that during the LEN SC maintenance period, if more than
28 weeks have elapsed since the last LEN SC injection and if it is clinically appropriate to
continue LEN treatment, then the indicated dosing regimen should be reinitiated.
3.1.1.5.

(b) (4)
(b) (4)

•
•

3.1.2. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
3.1.2.1. Resistance Assessments of Virologic Failures
•

The assessment of resistance emergence in virologic failures of an LEN-containing ARV
regimen is critical to interpreting the overall risk-benefit of LEN and understanding the
durability of LEN as a component of an ARV regimen in a heavily treatment-experienced
patient population.

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
3.1.2.2. Injection Site Reactions
•

Injection site reactions were identified as the most common adverse drug reaction among
subjects who received subcutaneous injections of lenacapavir; however, long-term
experience of subjects after multiple injections was limited in Studies 4625 and 4334.
3.1.2.3. Background Regimens Containing Boosted Protease
Inhibitors

•

The risk of supratherapeutic LEN exposures when LEN is combined with a background
regimen containing boosted protease inhibitors was not prospectively assessed in
Study 4625.

3.2. Approach to the Review
Table 4 provides an overview of the clinical trials that substantially contributed to the benefit and
risk assessment for LEN. Data from Study GS-US-200-4625 (Study 4625) were the primary
clinical trial data submitted in support of efficacy. Study GS-US-200-4334 (Study 4334)
provided additional descriptive support for the evaluation of safety and efficacy.
Additional phase 1 studies, such as Study GS-US-200-5709, were used to support a simplified
dosing regimen as well as adding supportive safety experience to the overall safety database.
Data from the 90-Safety Update Report also provided additional safety data from the ongoing
evaluation of subjects enrolled in Studies 4625 and 4334.

Table 6. Summary of General Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics of LEN
Characteristic
Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class
A human immunodeficiency virus type 1 (HIV-1) capsid inhibitor.
(EPC)
Mechanism of action
Lenacapavir (LEN) is a multistage selective inhibitor of HIV-1 capsid function that directly binds to the interface between
capsid protein subunits. LEN inhibits HIV-1 replication by interfering with multiple essential steps of the viral lifecycle,
including capsid-mediated nuclear uptake of HIV-1 linear unintegrated DNA (by blocking nuclear import proteins binding
to capsid), virus assembly and release (by interfering with Gag/Gag-Pol functioning, reducing production of capsid
protein subunits), and capsid core formation (by disrupting the rate of capsid subunit association, leading to malformed
capsids).
Antiviral activity
In MT-4 cells, LEN demonstrated antiviral activity with an EC50 value of 0.19nM and a selectivity index (CC50 value/EC50
value) of 140,000. LEN predominately targets at least one essential capsid-mediated step involved in the early stages of
HIV replication with an EC50 value of 0.023nM. LEN also showed an antiviral effect against at least one capsid-mediated
process essential for the late stages of HIV replication with a mean late stage EC50 value of 0.439nM.
Active moieties
LEN
QT prolongation
At LEN concentrations nine-fold higher than the therapeutic concentrations from the Applicant’s proposed dosing
regimen, LEN does not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.

NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential
Effectiveness
5.1.1. Antiviral Activity Against HIV-1 Subtypes
LEN displayed antiviral activity against 23 diverse HIV-1 clinical isolates representing all major
subtypes with a median 50% effective concentration (EC50) value of 0.04nM (range 0.02 to
0.16nM) in peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) (Table 124). LEN also showed
antiviral activity against two HIV-2 isolates but was 15- to 25-fold less active relative to HIV-1.
In a separate study testing the activity of LEN against 40 clinical HIV-1 isolates of diverse
subtypes using the PhenoSense Gag-Pro Antiviral Assay, the range of means across subtypes
was 0.166 to 0.297nM, with less than two-fold difference in activity across subtypes (Table 125).
The range of means for subtype B isolates was 0.03 to 0.08nM with a median of 0.04nM.

5.1.2. Antiviral Activity of Lenacapavir Against NonHIV Viruses
LEN was tested against hepatitis B virus (HBV), hepatitis C virus (HCV), human rhinovirus
(HRV), and respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) in cell-based assays. LEN did not display selective
antiviral activity in cell culture against HCV or HRV up to the highest concentration tested (44 to
50μM) (Table 129). However, LEN did demonstrate antiviral activity against HBV and RSV at
low micromolar concentrations, with EC50 values and corresponding 50% cytotoxic
concentration (CC50)/EC50 ratios of 1.5µM and >34, and 8.2µM and >6.1, respectively. These
data indicate that LEN is approximately 25,000-fold more active against HIV as compared to
HBV. However, a proper clinically active therapy against HBV should be used in HIV/HBVcoinfected patients to maintain the HBV suppression.

6. Assessment of Effectiveness
6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
6.1.1. Study 4625 (CAPELLA)
The dose selection for LEN in this study was based on data from nonclinical and clinical studies.
Prior to initiation of Study 4625, phase 1 PK and safety data were available in healthy subjects in
Studies GS-US-200-4071 and GS-US-200-4538 (Studies 4071 and 4538) where LEN was
administered as a single oral dose of up to 1800 mg, a multiple oral daily dose of up to 100 mg,
or a single SC dose of up to 927 mg.
In in vitro studies, LEN was tested at concentrations corresponding to tissue culture equivalent
inhibitory quotients (IQs) of 4, 8, 16, and 24, with an IQ of 1 defined as a concentration
corresponding to the protein-adjusted 95% effective concentration (paEC95) for wild-type HIV-1
(paEC95 3.87 ng/mL in MT-4 cells). In Study GS-US-200-4072 (Study 4072), the mean
maximum HIV-1 RNA decline over 10 days was 1.8 to 2.2 log10 copies/mL in HIV-1–infected
subjects administered LEN monotherapy as a single SC dose of 50 to 450 mg (Figure 1). All
subjects achieved at least 1 log10 copy/mL decline in their HIV-1 RNA at Day 10. Day 10

NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
antiviral activity was comparable across the 50 to 450 mg dose groups. A target plasma
concentration of 15.5 ng/mL (four-fold the IQ or IQ4), which is comparable to the in vivo 90%
effective concentration of 12.6 ng/mL in Study 4072, was anticipated to provide antiviral activity
in the heavily treatment-experienced (HTE) population.

6.1.2. Study GS-US-200-5709
Under this NDA, the Applicant proposed a simplified regimen that is different from the regimen
studied in Study 4625. This proposed simplified regimen is 927 mg by SC injection and 600 mg
orally on Day 1, followed by 600 mg orally on day 2, and 927 mg by SC injection every

NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
6 months. Because this proposed simplified regimen was not evaluated in Study 4625, there were
no clinical efficacy or safety data available for the simplified regimen. Therefore, Study GS-US200-5709 (Study 5709; which compared the systemic exposures of LEN after the regimens
shown in Table 8) was considered pivotal from a clinical pharmacology standpoint for
determining the acceptability of the proposed simplified regimen. An inspection was requested
for the clinical and analytical sites for this study. The Division of New Drug Study Integrity
within the Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance determined that inspections are not
warranted. The Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance inspected the site in August 2018,
which falls within the surveillance interval. After reviewing the inspection findings and the
written response from the site, the Office of Study Integrity and Surveillance recommended that
all study data be accepted for Agency review.
Comparability of LEN exposures between the regimen used in the ongoing phase 2 Study GSUS-200-4334 (Study 4334) and phase 2/3 Study 4625 and the Applicant’s proposed simplified
regimen is demonstrated based on modeling and simulation and ongoing PK bridging Study

evaluated in Study 5709 with the same regimen used in the ongoing phase 2 and phase 2/3
studies (Cohort 1) and the simplified regimen (Cohort 2). Because LEN dosing is the same after
month 6 irrespective of the regimen, LEN SC was not administered after Month 6 in Cohort 1
and Cohort 2 in Study 5709.

6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate
Efficacy
6.2.1. Study 4625 (CAPELLA)
6.2.1.1. Study Design
Results from Study 4625 serve as the basis of the efficacy evaluation. This trial was designed in
accordance with the recommendations set forth in FDA’s Guidance for Industry Human
Immunodeficiency Virus-1 Infection: Developing Antiretroviral Drugs for Treatment (November
2015).
Study 4625 had two cohorts. Eligible subjects who were HTE people living with HIV-1 (PLWH)
with multidrug resistance were screened and then had a Cohort Selection visit 14 to 30 days after
the Screening visit. During the Cohort Selection visit, if the subject’s HIV-1 RNA was
≥400 copies/mL and had <0.5 log10 decline from Screening, the subject would be enrolled into
Cohort 1 for randomization. If Cohort 1 was fully enrolled or the criteria for Cohort 1 were not
met, the subject would be enrolled into Cohort 2.
Cohort 1 was a randomized, placebo-controlled, superiority trial in which subjects were
randomized to receive 14 days of oral LEN or placebo in addition to their failing ARV regimen
(functional monotherapy period [FMP]). At the end of the FMP, subjects who were randomized
to receive oral LEN received SC LEN and initiated their optimized background regimen (OBR)
on Day 1 SC (defined as 14 days after the first dose of oral LEN). Subjects who were
randomized to placebo arm received oral LEN and initiated their OBR on Day 15, and then
received SC LEN at Day 1 SC while continuing their OBR. Subjects enrolled in Cohort 2
received oral LEN for 14 days together with an OBR on Day 1. At Day 1 SC, Cohort 2 subjects
received SC LEN and continued their OBR (Figure 7).
Figure 7. Study Design Schema, Study 4625

Source: Figure 1 of the Applicant’s Clinical Summary.
Eligible subjects were enrolled into Cohort 2 if Cohort 1 was fully enrolled.
Abbreviations: GS-6207, lenacapavir; HIV, human immunodeficiency virus; OBR, optimized background regimen; SC, subcutaneous

Key eligibility criteria are summarized in this section, and the full criteria are available in
Section III.15.
The study enrolled subjects who met the following inclusion criteria:
•

Adults aged ≥18 years or adolescents aged ≥12 years and weighing ≥35 kg, receiving a
stable failing ARV regimen for >8 weeks before screening.
Have HIV-1 RNA ≥400 copies/mL at screening.
HIV resistance reports showing resistance to ≥2 antiretroviral medications from each of ≥3
of the 4 main classes of antiretroviral medications (integrase strand-transfer inhibitor
[INSTI], non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor [NNRTI], nucleoside reverse
transcriptase inhibitor [NRTI], protease inhibitor [PI]).
Have ≤2 fully active ARV remaining from the 4 main classes that can be effectively
combined to form a viable regimen.
Willing to receive an optimized background regimen together with LEN.

6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan

The Applicant and the review division agreed on the statistical analysis plan prior to trial
completion.
The primary efficacy endpoint for Study 4625 was the proportion of subjects in Cohort 1
achieving a reduction in HIV-1 RNA of ≥0.5 log10 copies/mL from baseline at the end of the
FMP. The efficacy analysis population included all subjects who were randomized in the FMP
and received at least one dose of blinded study drug (full analysis set [FAS]). Only data collected
up to Day 1 SC or Day 15 visit were included in the primary efficacy analysis. For subjects with
missing HIV-1 RNA values at the end of the FMP, the value was imputed using the last
observation carried forward method. The difference in proportions between two treatment groups
were compared using an unconditional exact method with two inverted 1-sided tests at alpha
27
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
level 0.05 to evaluate superiority. The p-value and 95% exact confidence interval (CI) for the
point estimate of treatment difference in proportions were provided.
The secondary efficacy endpoints were the proportions of subjects in Cohort 1 with HIV-1 RNA
<50 and <200 copies/mL at Weeks 26 and 52 using the U.S. FDA-defined snapshot algorithm.
Analyses were based on the all LEN population that was defined as all subjects who were
enrolled in the study and received at least one dose of SC LEN. Only results at Week 26 were
included as the majority of subjects had not reached Week 52 at the time of NDA submission.
Subgroup analyses were planned for the proportion of subjects with HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL
at Week 26 as determined by the U.S. FDA-defined snapshot algorithm. Subgroups of interest
included age group, sex, race, region, baseline cluster of differentiation (CD)4+ categories,
baseline viral load categories, baseline INSTI resistance categories, genotypic sensitivity score,
phenotypic sensitivity score, and overall susceptibility score.
6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses of Clinical Trial Intended to
Demonstrate Efficacy, Study 4625 (CAPELLA)
This section summarizes the subject disposition, baseline demographics, clinical characteristics,
and primary and key secondary efficacy results to support the efficacy of LEN in HTE subjects
with multidrug resistant (MDR) HIV-1 infection. Data from Cohort 1 and Cohort 2 are often
shown side-by-side for ease of presentation. However, the two cohorts represent discrete groups
with different populations and are not intended to be compared to one another.
6.2.1.4.1. Disposition, Baseline Demographics, and
Baseline Clinical Characteristics

Disposition
The study screened a total of 144 subjects. Among them, 72 were enrolled in the study (Table 9).
All of the 72 subjects completed the FMP and received SC LEN injection on Day 1 SC. All of
the Cohort 1 subjects also completed the Week 26 assessment. One subject in Cohort 2
prematurely discontinued study drug in the maintenance period due to death.

Baseline Demographics and Clinical Characteristics
The majority of subjects in the trial (Cohort 1 and Cohort 2) were male (75%) and white (40%),
the median age was 52 years ranging from 23 to 78 years, and the median body mass index
(BMI) was 26 kg/m2 ranging from 15 to 43 kg/m2 (Table 10).
For Cohort 1, notably more subjects in the LEN arm had baseline HIV-1 RNA
≤100,000 copies/mL (96%) compared to the placebo arm (50%). The mean baseline CD4+ cell
count and CD4+ percentage were higher in the LEN arm. The median number of prior ARV
medications was nine for both arms. Further discussion of Cohort 1 baseline differences between
the LEN and placebo arms is discussed in Section 6.3.1.
Compared to Cohort 1, Cohort 2 subjects had more prior antiretroviral (ARV) regimens, higher
mean baseline CD4+ percentage and CD4+ cell count.

6.2.1.4.2. Primary and Key Secondary Efficacy Results

Primary Endpoint
The Applicant’s primary efficacy results were confirmed by the statistical review team, and the
results demonstrate superiority of LEN compared to placebo for the endpoint of proportion of
HIV-1 RNA reduction ≥0.5 log10 from baseline to the end of the FMP at Day 14. The 95% CI for
the difference in the proportions (LEN − placebo) excluded zero and the p-value was statistically
significant (p<0.0001), indicating superiority (Table 11).
Table 11. Primary Efficacy Endpoint of HIV-1 RNA Reduction ≥0.5 Log10 at the End of Functional
Monotherapy Period, Study 4625

The 95% CI of the difference in proportions between groups and the corresponding p value were calculated based on an
unconditional exact method using two invert one-sided tests.
Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; HIV-1, human immunodeficiency virus 1; SD, standard deviation

Secondary Endpoints
Assessing the durability of virologic response in HTE subjects is an important component of
understanding the overall efficacy of LEN. At the end of the FMP, all subjects were transitioned
from their initial (failing) background regimen to an optimized regimen in combination with
open-label LEN. It was planned that the Week 26 and Week 52 HIV-1 RNA would be evaluated
as secondary endpoints. The majority of subjects did not complete the Week 52 assessment at the
time of NDA submission; thus, only Week 26 data are presented (Table 12). Approximately 81%
subjects in Cohort 1 achieved HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL at Week 26: 66.7% and 87.5% for
subjects in the placebo arm who later switched to LEN and the LEN arm, respectively. We did
not find imbalances in baseline characteristics that account for the numerical difference between
the two groups (see Section 6.3.1). In addition, variations between the two groups could happen
due to the small sample size. In Cohort 2 only six subjects completed the Week 26 evaluation,
therefore, the review team will revisit this analysis once more data are received.

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
6.2.1.4.3. Subgroup Analyses of the Primary Endpoint
Subgroup analyses were conducted to assess the potential for differences in the treatment effect
for various demographic and baseline characteristics subgroups (Table 13). Overall, the
treatment effect of LEN compared to placebo appeared consistent across subgroups. Of note, the
sample sizes for these subgroups were small, which limits the ability to evaluate trends with
certainty.

6.2.2. Study 4334 (CALIBRATE)
6.2.2.1. Study Design
Results from Study 4334 serve as supplementary data for the efficacy and safety evaluation of
LEN for the indication of treating the HTE HIV-1 population. This was a phase 2, randomized,
open-label, active-controlled study conducted in the treatment-naïve PLWH population. Eligible
subjects were randomized in a 2:2:2:1 ratio to SC LEN + (emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide
[F/TAF] → tenofovir alafenamide [TAF]), SC LEN + (F/TAF → bictegravir [BIC]), Oral LEN +
F/TAF, or bictegravir/emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide (B/F/TAF) group (Figure 9).
Randomization was stratified by HIV-1 RNA level (≤100,000 copies/mL or
>100,000 copies/mL) at screening.

6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan

The Applicant and the review division agreed on the interim analysis (IA) plan prior to the
Week 28 IA.

33
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
The study had four prespecified IAs: Week 28, Week 38, Week 54 (primary analysis), and Week
80. The Week 28 IA was conducted when all subjects completed the Week 28 evaluation. The
results of this analysis were used to supplement the efficacy and safety of LEN for the HTE
population. There were no multiplicity adjustments planned because this was stated as a phase 2,
non-confirmatory study.
The primary endpoint of this study was the proportion of subjects with HIV-1 RNA
<50 copies/mL at Week 54 as determined by the U.S. FDA-defined snapshot algorithm. Because
the majority of subjects had not yet reached Week 54 at the time of the NDA submission, the
primary endpoint was not analyzed.
A secondary endpoint was the proportion of subjects with HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL at
Week 28, as determined by the U.S. FDA-defined snapshot algorithm. The FAS was used as the
analysis population. Point estimates and 95% CI for the difference in the response rates between
each of the LEN-containing regimen groups (Treatment Groups 1 to 3) and the B/F/TAF group
(Treatment Group 4) were constructed using stratum-adjusted Mantel-Haenszel proportion as
described in Chapter 13 of Categorical Data Analysis (Koch et al. 1989), stratified by baseline
HIV-1 RNA level (≤100,000 copies/mL or >100,000 copies/mL).
6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses of Clinical Trial Intended to
Supplement Efficacy, Study 4334 (CALIBRATE)
6.2.2.4.1. Disposition, Baseline Demographics, and

Disposition
A total of 183 subjects was randomized into Study 4334. Of these, 182 subjects received at least
one dose of study drug and were included in the FAS. All subjects completed Week 28 visit or
discountined the study. All of the 13 subjects who prematurely discontinued the study were from
the LEN-containing arms, with the reasons listed as lost to follow up, withdrew consent,
investigator’s discretion, and lack of efficacy (Table 14).

Demographic and baseline characteristics were generally similar across the treatment groups.
The majority of subjects were male (93%) and Black (52%). The mean age was 32 years, and the

≤100,000 copies/mL and were in the CD4+ category of ≥200 cells/μL at baseline.

6.2.2.4.2. Secondary Efficacy Results
The proportion of subjects with HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL at Week 28 was 49 of 52 (94.2%),
49 of 53 (92.5%), 49 of 52 (94.2%), and 25 of 25 (100%) for the arms of SC LEN + (F/TAF →
TAF), SC LEN + (F/TAF → BIC), oral LEN + F/TAF, and B/F/TAF, respectively. For the
combined LEN-containing regimens, 147 of 157 (93.6%) subjects met this criterion (Table 16).
Compared to the B/F/TAF arm, the difference of the response rate was -6.3 (LEN combined –
B/F/TAF), with a p-value of 0.2. A rapid decrease in HIV-1 RNA after drug initiation was
observed, which became stable after Day 30 (Figure 10) in all four arms.

This finding suggests that subjects in the LEN arm may have had an advantage with regard to

Assessment
Post hoc analyses were conducted to explore the association between baseline HIV-1 RNA and
CD4+ imbalance across Cohort 1 treatment arms and the outcome of HIV-1 RNA reduction of
≥0.5 log10. The distributions of baseline HIV-1 RNA by arm and by outcome are presented in
Table 18. Within each arm, subjects who had reduction ≥0.5 log10 at the end of the functional
monotherapy period generally had higher baseline HIV-1 RNA. Therefore, it appears that the
lower baseline HIV-1 RNA level in the LEN arm is not likely associated with more reduction in
HIV-1 RNA at the end of the functional monotherapy period. In addition, subgroup analyses
(Table 13) showed that the effects of LEN were consistent across the subgroups of HIV-1 RNA
≤100,000 copies/mL and >100,000 copies/mL, and the subgroups of CD4+ <200 cells/μL and
≥200 cells/μL.

6.3.2. Justification for the Proposed Simplified Dosing
Regimen
Issue
The Applicant proposes a simplified dosing regimen that was not studied during the LEN clinical
development program.

Background
The LEN dosing regimen evaluated in the phase 2/3 study allowed for a 14-day oral lead-in
period, where subjects received oral LEN on Day 1 (600 mg), Day 2 (600 mg), and Day 8
(300 mg) before SC LEN injection on Day 15 (927 mg), then every 26 weeks thereafter. The oral
lead-in period enabled an assessment of safety and tolerability of LEN prior to SC LEN
administration. Given the established safety profile and lack of exposure-safety relationship from
the phase 2 and phase 2/3 trials, the Applicant proposed a simplified dosing regimen as a strategy
to potentially improve adherence. The following simplified regimen is proposed: oral LEN
600 mg and SC LEN injection 927 mg on Day 1, oral LEN 600 mg on Day 2, followed by SC
LEN injection 927 mg every 26 weeks thereafter.

Assessment
The Applicant performed population PK simulations using their final PK model (see
Section III.14.3 for further details on the population PK) to compare LEN PK from both the
phase 2/3 dosing regimen and the proposed simplified regimen. The simulated LEN
concentration-time profiles and the predicted exposure metrics (AUCtau, Cmax, and Ctrough)
indicated that the simplified regimen has a similar LEN PK profile compared to that evaluated in
the phase 2/3 study (Figure 12 and Table 19). Similar LEN accumulation is expected after the
second SC and the seventh SC dose after administration of either regimen (Figure 12).

Conclusion
Based on observed and predicted data, the exposures of LEN are anticipated to be similar
between the regimen used in Study 4625 and the proposed simplified regimen. The review team
agreed that the proposed label should include recommendations for both the simplified regimen
and the regimen used in Study 4625.

6.3.3. Impact of Subjects Not Maintaining Eligibility
Criteria: Not Failing on Background Regimen or
Nonadherence to Background Regimen
Issue
In a clinical trial designed for study in an HTE patient population, it is important to determine if
the subjects are truly failing on their pre-trial ARV regimen rather than due to nonadherence to
the ARV drugs with no evidence of resistance in their supposed “failing” regimen. If subjects are
nonadherent to the regimen and do not have resistance to the “failing” regimen during the LEN
functional monotherapy period, it will be difficult to determine the contribution of LEN to
efficacy in the background of other active or partially active ARVs.
42
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

Background
The entry eligibility criteria for Study 4625 included:
•
•

Resistance to ≥2 ARVs from each of ≥3 of the 4 main classes of ARV drugs (INSTI,
NNRTI, NRTI, PI), which could include historical reports.
Plasma HIV-1 RNA ≥400 copies/mL at the screening visit while taking a failing
background regimen.

Assessment
Eligibility criteria for Cohort 1 included 0.5 log10 HIV-1 RNA decline compared to the
Screening visit and HIV-1 RNA ≥ 400 copies/mL at the Cohort Selection visit. There were
subjects who did not maintain eligibility criteria for Cohort 1 because of the following reasons:
•
•

There was evidence that a subject was not failing background regimen:
— Day 1 viral load <400 copies/mL.
— Decline in viral load from the Screening visit to Baseline.
There was evidence that a subject was nonadherent to background regimen:
— <3 class resistance (by genotype, phenotype, or historical)
— No reported resistance to drugs in failing background regimen

Of the 24 subjects in LEN Cohort 1A, 5 subjects (21%) had evidence that they were not failing

) had resistance to DTG and DRV at screening, but

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
The efficacy (proportion of subjects achieving ≥0.5 log10 copies/mL reduction from baseline in
HIV-1 RNA) of Cohort 1A and 1B was assessed after removing the subjects who had evidence
they were not failing their background regimen or were nonadherent to the background regimen.
After removing the five subjects who had evidence they were not failing their background
regimen, 95% (18/19) of subjects in Cohort 1A (LEN) achieved ≥0.5 log10 copies/mL reduction
in HIV-1 RNA from baseline compared to 17% (2/12) in the placebo Cohort 1B arm (difference
of 78%) (Table 24). Removing the six subjects with evidence of nonadherence narrowed the
difference between groups (86% [18/21] versus 22% [2/9]; difference of 63.5%) but was still
significantly different. Removing both the subjects with evidence of not failing background
regimen and subjects with evidence of nonadherence showed efficacy of 94% (15/16) versus
22% (2/9) (difference of 72%) compared to the overall efficacy of 88% (21/24) versus 17%
(2/12) (difference of 71.5%). Thus, this supplementary sensitivity analysis confirmed the
efficacy of LEN in comparison to placebo and was consistent with the primary endpoint analysis.
