Although the sample size of Cohort 1 was small, and decreased further following sensitivity
analysis exclusions, the demonstrated treatment effect for this efficacy analysis remains large. Of
note, recent prior studies in the HTE patient population had a similar sample size (e.g., 40 HTE
subjects in the phase 3 trial TMB-301 for ibalizumab-uiyk), which may reflect the challenge of
enrolling trials in this limited patient population.

Conclusion
Five subjects (21%) in LEN Cohort 1A had evidence that they were not failing on their regimen
at study entry, four of whom had <400 copies/mL on Day 1. Additionally, three subjects in each
LEN Cohort 1A and placebo Cohort 1B had evidence that they were nonadherent to their
“failing” background regimen based on genotypic and phenotypic susceptibility to their
background drugs. Supplementary sensitivity efficacy analyses removing these subjects
confirmed the primary endpoint analysis of LEN.

6.3.4. Dosing Interval and Approach to Missed
Subcutaneous Maintenance Doses
Issues
•

Is administration of SC LEN within the Applicant’s proposed dosing window of ±2 weeks
expected to compromise the safety or efficacy of LEN?

46
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
•

Is reinitiating therapy with the indicated regimen in patients who did not receive SC LEN
up to 28 weeks since the previous SC LEN dose expected to compromise the safety of
LEN?

Background
The Applicant has proposed a dosing window of 26±2 weeks (i.e., 24 to 28 weeks) after the
previous SC injection. In phase 2 and phase 2/3 studies, SC LEN doses were to be administered
within 24 to 28 weeks after the previous SC LEN dose as per protocol.
The Applicant proposes to restart LEN regimen with the oral loading dose, if more than
28 weeks have elapsed since the previous SC LEN injection. For subjects in phase 2 and phase
2/3 studies who were not dosed by Week 28 and continued in the studies, the protocol indicated
that the regimen be reinitiated with oral LEN followed by SC LEN administration (oral LEN
600 mg daily for 2 days followed by oral LEN 300 mg on Day 8, with SC LEN injection 927 mg
restarting 1 week later) with the intent to rapidly correct LEN concentrations.

Assessment
Applicant’s Proposed ±2-Week Dosing Window
The Applicant predicted the following arithmetic mean Ctrough (90% CI of mean) in adult patients
at Weeks 24, 26, 28, 30 and 32, following the phase 2/3 dosing regimen: 34.6 (29.4, 38.5), 29.2
(24.6, 32.5), 24.5 (20.4, 27.3), 20.6 (17.0, 23.1), and 17.3 (14.2, 19.7) ng/mL, respectively.
According to the analysis conducted by the Applicant (Figure 13), the lower bound of the 90% CI
of the simulated arithmetic mean Ctrough was consistently above the IQ4 threshold of 15.5 ng/mL,
indicating that LEN will maintain efficacious concentrations when dosed within the proposed
±2 week dosing window.

47
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Figure 13. Predicted LEN Arithmetic Mean Ctrough (90% CI of Mean) on Weeks 24 to 32 After the
First SC Injection in Adult Patients Receiving the Phase 2/3 Dosing Regimen

Source: Applicant’s Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Studies, Figure 13, page 77.
Abbreviations: CI, confidence interval; Ctrough, minimum concentration; IQ4, inhibitory quotient 4

According to the phase 2 study (4334) and phase 2/3 study (4625) protocols, SC LEN doses were
to be administered with a dosing window of 24 to 28 weeks, and the regimen was to be restarted
(with oral lead-in) for dosing delays longer than 28 weeks from the last SC injection. However,
>75% of the subjects were evaluated for PK or efficacy endpoints 26 weeks after the first SC
injection, hence there were insufficient data to assess the appropriateness of the proposed dosing
window based on observed LEN PK data.
The review team assessment of the dosing window found that unlike arithmetic mean Ctrough (as
shown in Figure 14), the geometric mean Ctrough (Figure 15) and the median Ctrough (Figure 16)
showed that at Week 28, after the first SC injection, the average Ctrough is expected to be around
the IQ4 (15.5 ng/mL). Therefore, about 50% of subjects are expected to be below IQ4 at
Week 28 (Figure 16, lower panel), which is lower than the predicted average Ctrough and IQ4
attainment for Week 26 (reference dosing interval, Figure 15 and Figure 16). The discrepancy
between the arithmetic mean Ctrough (as shown by the Applicant in Figure 14) and the geometric
mean or median Ctrough (Figure 15 and Figure 16) is likely, in part, due to the imbalance in LEN
exposure between BMI groups in studied patients (4334 and 4625), with underweight patients
having higher LEN exposure and overweight/obese patients having lower LEN exposure
compared to patients with normal BMI. In addition, as depicted in Figure 16 the difference in the
average Ctrough between Study 4334 (in treatment-naïve patients with HIV) and Study 4625 (in
HTE patients with HIV), due to drug-drug interactions (co-medications with cobicistat or
ritonavir) in Study 4625, likely contributed to the discrepancy between the overall mean and
geometric mean or median Ctrough (calculated from both studies). The exclusion of subjects with
interacting co-medication from Study 4625 decreased the average Ctrough to levels similar to those
observed in Study 4334 (see Section III.14.3 for further details).
48
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
The geometric (±90% CI) LEN Ctrough after administration of the LEN second SC dose either 24
or 26 weeks after the first SC dose is expected to be above IQ4 (Figure 15 and Figure 16, middle
column of each panel; data not shown for Week 24). By contrast, dosing at 28 weeks (Figure 15
and Figure 16, right column of each panel) is expected to maintain the geometric (±90% CI)
Ctrough either closer to or at the IQ4 threshold, particularly for overweight and more likely obese
patients, as depicted by the lower right panel of Figure 15.
In conclusion, a second injection given 26 weeks instead of 28 weeks after the first injection is
more likely to ensure that the geometric mean LEN Ctrough is well above the IQ4 threshold in all
BMI groups. A second injection given 28 weeks after the first injection is expected to maintain
the geometric mean Ctrough either closer to IQ4 or at the IQ4 for a long period of time (due to
slower accumulation), particularly in overweight/obese patients. Because most patients in phase
2 and phase 2/3 studies received SC LEN at Week 26, the clinical implications of LEN
concentrations falling below IQ4, especially in a subset of overweight/obese patients, at Week 28
are unknown.

Conclusion
The optimal dosing interval for SC LEN injection is 26 weeks, particularly in overweight and
obese patients to maintain LEN geometric mean Ctrough and its 95% CI well above the IQ4
threshold. However, ± 2 weeks dosing window is acceptable to provide flexibility and facilitate
adherence to the SC LEN regimen.

Applicant’s Proposal of Reinitiating the Indicated Regimen in Patients Who Did Not
Receive SC LEN Up To 28 Weeks After the Previous SC LEN Dose
The Applicant proposed that during the LEN SC maintenance period, if more than 28 weeks
have elapsed since the last LEN SC injection and if it is clinically appropriate to continue LEN
treatment, then reinitiate the indicated dosing regimen.
PK simulations were performed for different dosing delays (Weeks 30 to 48) after the last SC
injection followed by either the administration of an SC LEN injection or the reinitiation of the
dosing regimen starting with oral loading.
Figure 17 (upper panel) shows that the median LEN concentration from the previous LEN
injection stays above IQ4 as long as the next LEN SC injection is administered within the dosing
window (±2 weeks).
The lower panel of Figure 17 shows that reinitiation of the dosing regimen (starting with oral
loading) for dosing delays >28 weeks and up to 32 weeks is expected to yield a median Ctrough
and SC Cmax comparable to the second SC injection from the regular 24 to 28-week dosing
window. However, the median Cmax resulting from the oral loading dose is anticipated to be
relatively higher than the median Cmax observed and predicted with the indicated regimen. Even
though the median Cmax from the loading dose is relatively higher, it remains within acceptable
safety ranges and well below the LEN concentrations and Cmax values observed in the thorough
QT study (Study 4332).

Conclusion
The Applicant’s proposal to reinitiate the indicated dosing regimen if more than 28 weeks have
elapsed since the last LEN SC injection, and if it is clinically appropriate to continue LEN
treatment, is acceptable.

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
feces, bile, and/or liver samples from rats, dogs, monkeys, and humans. Oxidative metabolites
were not detected. No new or additional metabolites were observed in human plasma or tissues
that were not identified in human isolated microsomes and hepatocytes or in other animal
species.
The major routes of elimination in rats and dogs is feces resulting from lenacapavir excretion
into the intestines by the p-glycoprotein transporter.

General Toxicology
Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted with oral and subcutaneous formulations of
lenacapavir to evaluate toxicity from systemic and local toxicity and irritation. The most
common toxicity noted among all of the toxicology studies was injection site reactions.
The acute toxicity and tolerability of lenacapavir was evaluated in single dose intravenous
studies in both Wister Hanover rats and Beagle dogs up to 30 mg/kg. Infusion site reactions
(cellular inflammation, thrombus, congestion/hemorrhage, edema, and fibrosis) were noted at all
doses. Additionally, clinical signs of toxicity in rats included: red urine, ataxia, hypoactivity,
vocalization, squinting and/or pale eyes, audible/labored/irregular breathing, and sternal
recumbence. Clinical signs of toxicity in dogs included: skin lesions, intermittent tremors,
vocalization, and/or discharge of the left eye and front foot.
Pivotal repeat-dose toxicology evaluation for lenacapavir included GLP studies of up to 6-month
duration in rats and 9-month duration in dogs via oral or subcutaneous administrations. The skin
at the injection site was the target organ of toxicity in both animal species. Additionally, in the 9month dog study using the clinical formulation, the liver and kidney were also identified as
targeted organs of toxicity.
Based on systemic exposure in the repeat-dose studies in rats via subcutaneous administrations,
there was no target organ identified at doses up to 100 mg/kg. However, based on local toxicity,
the injection site was the target organ identified in the rat studies via subcutaneous
administrations in all studies. These injection site reactions were observed after the recovery
periods, albeit at a lesser degree of intensity. Toxicities at the injection sites included necrosis,
granulomatous inflammation, mixed cell inflammation, and/or macrophage infiltration.
Based on systemic exposure in the repeat-dose studies in dogs via subcutaneous administrations,
there was no target organ identified at doses up to 100 mg/kg up to 2 months. However, repeatdose studies up to 9 months in dogs identified the liver, gall bladder, kidneys, and stomach as
targeted organs of toxicity. Liver findings included bile duct degeneration and cell/bile ductule
hyperplasia. Gallbladder findings included edema and hyperplasia detected at doses up to
411 mg/kg. Kidney findings included tubule degeneration and dilatation, and findings in the
stomach included degeneration/atrophy of mucosa at the highest dose of 411 mg/kg.
Additionally, based on local toxicity, the injection site was the target organ identified in all
repeat-dose dog studies via subcutaneous administrations up to 411 mg/kg. Toxicities at the
injection sites included necrosis, granulomatous inflammation, mixed cell inflammation, and/or
macrophage infiltration. These injection site reactions were observed after the recovery periods,
albeit at a lesser intensity. Based on systemic exposure, at the no observed adverse effect level
(NOAEL), lenacapavir was 32-fold the human exposure at the recommended human dose (RHD)
in the 2-month rat study and 6- to 8-fold in the 6-month rat study. Additionally, based on
59
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
systemic exposure, at the NOAEL in the 2- and 9-month dog studies, lenacapavir was 24- and 9fold the human exposure at the RHD.
In clinical trials, the most notable adverse events (AEs) associated with lenacapavir were nodules
that formed at the injection sites, thus reflecting the main toxicities of injection site reactions
observed in nonclinical species.

Genotoxicology and Carcinogenicity
Lenacapavir was negative for mutagenesis in the in vitro reverse mutation assay, negative for
clastogenicity in the in vitro assay using human peripheral lymphocytes, and negative for
genotoxicity in the in vivo micronucleus assay in rats. Additionally, several impurities were
identified and tested in a battery of genotoxicology studies for mutagenesis and clastogenicity.
Overall, the weight of evidence indicates that lenacapavir does not exhibit genotoxic potential.
The carcinogenicity potential of lenacapavir is being evaluated in a 26-week study (single
subcutaneous injection given once every 13 weeks for a total of two doses) in 001178-T
(hemizygous) RasH2 mice and a 2-year study (single subcutaneous injection given once every
13 weeks for a total of eight doses) in Wister Hanover rats. These studies are ongoing at the time
of NDA submission and will be part of a future PMR.

Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
No lenacapavir-related effects on male and female fertility parameters occurred with up to
100 mg/kg lenacapavir when administered via a single subcutaneous injection to rats at
exposures seven- and five-fold (in males and females, respectively) the RHD. No lenacapavirrelated effects on embryo-fetal development (EFD) occurred at doses up to 30 mg/kg lenacapavir
when orally administered to rats once daily from gestation day (GD) 6 to 17 at exposures 16-fold
the RHD.
In the rabbit EFD study, lower body weight gains at doses up to 20 mg/kg correlated to a dosedependent decrease in food intake following intravenous administration of lenacapavir. Body
weights returned to those of controls levels after 9 days without treatment. No other maternal or
fetal toxicity occurred with up to the highest intravenous administration of 20 mg/kg at
exposures 39-fold the RHD.
In the pre-and postnatal development (PPND) toxicity study, lenacapavir was administered to
pregnant rats via a single subcutaneous injection of doses of 30 and 300 mg/kg on GD 6. There
were no maternal toxicities or effects on PPND at doses up to 300 mg/kg at exposures five-fold
the RHD. Lactation endpoints were not measured in the PPND study.

Additional Toxicology Studies
Lenacapavir resulted in local toxicity at the injection sites at concentrations up to 500 mg/mL in
rabbits. Lenacapavir was not phototoxic, cytotoxic, or considered an eye irritant. Using in vitro
skin models, lenacapavir was not considered corrosive but was considered positive in two of
three skin sensitization assays. However, in the in vivo assessment, lenacapavir was considered
negative for sensitization potential. In the clinical formulation for marketing, the hemolytic
potential of lenacapavir is low.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Lenacapavir is the first in a new class of antiretroviral drugs, so no previously described clinical
experience is available to inform any specific safety concern regarding capsid inhibitors.
Immune reconstitution syndrome has been reported with combination antiretroviral therapy,
particularly in the initial phase of treatment, in patients whose immune systems develop an
inflammatory response to indolent or residual opportunistic infections. This is also often
particularly relevant in a heavily treatment-experienced patient population. Loss of virologic
response due to drug-drug interactions has been included as a warning in labeling for other
ARVs.
For other ARVs that are administered in an injectable form, such as Cabenuva (a long-acting
formulation of two drugs, cabotegravir and rilpivirine), injection site reactions are the most
common adverse reactions. These were described usually (79%) as localized pain or discomfort.
The Cabenuva label notes that “Other manifestations included nodules (14%), induration (12%),
swelling (8%), erythema (4%), pruritus (4%), bruising (3%), warmth (2%), and hematoma (2%).
Abscess and cellulitis at the injection site were each reported in less than 1% of subjects”
(Cabenuva 2021). Serious post-injection reactions were reported in less than 1% of subjects
within minutes after the injection of rilpivirine and begin to resolve within a few minutes after
the injection. These reactions were characterized by signs and symptoms including dyspnea,
agitation, abdominal cramping, flushing, sweating, oral numbness, and changes in blood
pressure.
Additionally, given the long-acting properties of Cabenuva, the label notes that “residual
concentrations of both cabotegravir and rilpivirine may remain in the systemic circulation of
patients for prolonged periods”; therefore, a warning is included that nonadherence could lead to
loss of virologic response and development of resistance.

62
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

7.3. Potential Safety Concerns Identified Through
Postmarket Experience
Lenacapavir has not been approved in any country; therefore, there has been no postmarketing
experience with lenacapavir.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that preclude
performing a safety review. No major issues were identified with respect to recording, coding,
and categorizing AEs. The Applicant’s translations of verbatim terms to Medical Dictionary for
Regulatory Activities preferred terms for the events reported in Studies 4625 and 4334 were
coded according to Version 23.1 and reviewed and were found to be acceptable.
AEs were protocol-defined as: “any untoward medical occurrence in a clinical study participant
administered an investigational product, which does not necessarily have a causal relationship
with the treatment.”
Treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were defined in the Applicant’s analysis, and for
the purpose of this review, as any AE that occurred on or after the day of treatment initiation.
Adverse drug reactions were defined for the purpose of this review as any TEAE that was
considered by the investigator to be related to the study drug with reasonable possibility.
"Reasonable possibility" is meant to convey that there are facts or evidence or arguments to
suggest a causal relationship, rather than a relationship cannot be ruled out.
Serious adverse events (SAEs) were protocol-defined as any untoward medical occurrence that,
at any dose, resulted in:
•
•
•
•
•
•

Death.
Was life-threatening (note: the term “life-threatening” in the definition of “serious” refers
to an event in which the subject was at risk of death at the time of the event; it does not
refer to an event that hypothetically might have caused death if it were more severe).
In-patient hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization.
Persistent or significant disability/incapacity.
A congenital anomaly/birth defect.
A medically important event or reaction: such events may not be immediately lifethreatening or result in death or hospitalization but may jeopardize the subject or may
require intervention to prevent one of the other outcomes constituting SAEs. Medical and
scientific judgment must be exercised to determine whether such an event is reportable
under expedited reporting rules. Examples of medically important events include intensive
treatment in an emergency room or at home for allergic bronchospasm; blood dyscrasias or
convulsions that do not result in hospitalization; and development of drug dependency or
drug abuse.

Severity grades were defined by the Division of AIDS Table for Grading the Severity of Adult
and Pediatric Adverse Events (corrected Version 2.1, July 2017) (NIAID 2017).

Approach to Assessment of Clinical Trial Data
The review approach for assessment of risk consisted of evaluation of the safety data from the
phase 2/3 trials separately as well as the pooled safety data from phase 1 studies. These trials
were reviewed separately because the studies enrolled different patient populations. Study 4625
included an HTE population, while Study 4334 enrolled a treatment-naïve population, and the
phase 1 studies were healthy adults. Results of individual trials are highlighted where important
differences may have emerged.
Clinical trial data from Studies 4635 and 4334 were independently analyzed using JMP and JMP
Clinical software. Additional analyses were provided by the Clinical Data Scientist support team.
All safety assessments and conclusions are those of the clinical review team unless otherwise
specified. Prespecified hypothesis testing was not proposed for safety outcomes in Studies 4625
or 4334. Comparisons are therefore based on descriptive analyses.
Comparisons across arms in Study 4625 were limited because assessment during the randomized
portion of the study were limited to the FMP with only the oral administration of LEN. As a
general approach, attribution of events due to LEN comparisons across study arms were derived
largely from Study 4334 and selected cohorts from phase 1. For relevance to the proposed SC
dosing, study arms that included SC LEN were combined and compared to another active control
arm randomized to B/F/TAF. Because the pooled phase 1 data were placebo-controlled in
healthy volunteers, comparisons across arms were generally more acceptable for the purpose of
assessing contribution of LEN; however, the overall sample size achieved in this analysis was
small and duration of follow-up was shorter.

Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
In the Applicant’s proposed label, Adverse Reactions (Section 6.1) included only data from
Study 4625, as this study included the relevant patient population for the proposed indication,
whereas Study 4334 included treatment-naïve subjects that were anticipated to have significant
differences in underlying disease severity and comorbid conditions.

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
Overall, the safety database is adequate to assess the safety of lenacapavir for the proposed
indication, dosage regimen, and patient population. See Table 27.
At the time of NDA filing, 803 individuals were enrolled in clinical studies and received at least
one dose of LEN, regardless of route (SC, per oral [PO], or intravenous [IV]), formulation, or
population (healthy, PLWH). A total of 284 subjects received at least one dose of 927 mg SC
LEN (with same proposed SC formulation or equivalent oral dose that is expected to achieve the
targeted exposure for at least 6 months). A total of 232 subjects received at least one 927 mg SC
dose of LEN with the same proposed formulation for marketing. The total number of PLWH
who received SC LEN at the proposed dose/duration (LEN exposures ≥26 weeks) was 177.

Study 4625 Clinical Safety Experience
In the phase 2/3 study, Study 4625, 72 subjects in Cohorts 1 and 2 received at least one SC dose
of LEN. Safety comparisons between randomized subjects, however, were limited to 36 subjects
in Cohort 1 during the 14-day FMP, 24 of whom received oral doses of LEN. During the
maintenance phase of the study, during which all subjects received SC LEN:
•
•

Thirty-nine subjects received a second dose (Cohort 1: N=36, Cohort 2: N=3).
Eleven subjects received a third dose (Cohort 1: N=10, Cohort 2: N=1).

7.6. Safety Findings and Concerns Based on
Review of Clinical Safety Database
7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Studies 4625
(CAPELLA) and 4334 (CALIBRATE)
7.6.1.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event
Summary
An overiew of the TEAEs in Studies 4625 and 4334 through at least the Week 26 and Week 28
visit dates, respectively, is shown in Table 33. While the comparisons across the grouped study
arms (total LEN in Study 4625 and Study 4334) revealed a trend of higher incidence of AEs as
compared with the oral-only regimen of B/F/TAF, there were no serious AEs and very few
moderate to severe (Grades 3 to 5) AEs that were considered by the investigator to be study-drug
related in any of the studies. Overall, these trends suggest that differences in AEs reported across
studies likely reflect underlying differences in patient populations.

One subject in Cohort 2 of Study 4625 died on Study Day 90; the cause of death was cancer and
was considered by the investigator not to be related to the study drug. Additional details are
provided in Section 7.6.3.2. There were no deaths reported in Study 4334.

7.6.1.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events
In the original NDA submission, no TEAEs were reported in Study 4625 that led to
discontinuation through the follow-up period ending 01 April, 2021. For Study 4334, with
follow-up through March 26, 2021 (Week 28 interim analysis), 2 of 105 subjects (1.9%) in the
total SC LEN group discontinued study drug prematurely due to AEs of injection site induration.
Both occurrences were nonserious and Grade 1 in severity.

7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
In Study 4625, TEAEs were defined as an AE that began on or after the first dose date of blinded
study drug and prior to the first dose date of the open-label study drug. In Study 4334, TEAEs
began on or after the first dose date of study drug up to last exposure date after permanent
discontinuation of study drug, or led to premature study drug discontinuation. Table 36 includes
TEAEs occurring at 5% or higher frequency in Study 4625. The most common TEAEs were
injection site reactions (ISRs) in the SC LEN arms. The B/F/TAF arm, however, did not include
any injectable study drug. Other frequent TEAEs in LEN arms of Studies 4625 and 4334
included nausea, headache, and diarrhea.

7.6.2. Laboratory Findings
Most laboratory abnormalities in Studies 4625 and 4334 were generally not clinically significant
(Grade 1 or 2), and occurred in subjects who had previous underlying conditions such as
diabetes.
In Study 4625, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 15 (21%) of 72 subjects. Grade
4 laboratory abnormalities were reported in four (6%) subjects. Grade 3 and 4 abnormalities that
occurred in more than two subjects included high creatinine and high fasting glucose. None of
the subjects had Grade 3 or higher increase in creatine kinase (CK) or CK-associated AEs such
as rhabdomyolysis. Please see additional discussion in Section 7.6.3.6.
In Study 4334, Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported in 19 (12%) of the 157 subjects
who received LEN (the total LEN group) and 3 (12%) of the 25 subjects in the B/F/TAF group.
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported for seven (5%) subjects in the total LEN group,
including three subjects with high CK and one subject each with the following abnormalities:
high alanine aminotransferase (ALT), hyperbilirubinemia, fasting triglycerides, high lipase, and
nonfasting hyperglycemia. The Grade 4 high ALT was attributed by the investigator to the use of
Bactrim for the treatment of Pneumocystis jirovecii pneumonia. The Grade 3 and 4 CK
elevations were all attributed to strenuous activity, dehydration, or use of creatine, and the levels
returned to normal at a later visit. Please see additional discussion in Section 7.6.4.6.

Trends in vital signs and body weight in Studies 4625 and 4334 were also reviewed. There were
no clinically relevant changes from baseline or in median values for systolic blood pressure,
diastolic blood pressure, pulse, respiration rate, or body temperature. In Study 4625 at Week 26,
the median (Q1, Q3) increase in body weight was 2.0 (−0.9, 5.5) kg. In Study 4334 by Week 28,
the median increase in body weight in the Total LEN group was 2.3 (−0.2, 4.8) kg, and the
increase in the B/F/TAF group was 2.7 (0, 4.8) kg.

7.6.3. Safety Findings and Concerns, Study 4625
(CAPELLA)
7.6.3.1. Overall Treatment-Emergent Adverse Event
Summary
An overview of TEAEs for Study 4625 was previously discussed in Section 7.6.1.1. Table 40
lists the TEAEs experienced during the FMP only. One of the limitations of Study 4625 is the
lack of a comparator beyond the FMP. Because the two cohorts were not randomized to different
regimens during the maintenance period (and all subjects in Cohort 1 received SC LEN

following the FMP), comparisons across treatment arms were not expected to provide trends that
demonstrate the impact of LEN on safety outcomes (Table 41).

7.6.3.2. Deaths
One treatment-emergent adverse event resulting in death was reported in Study 4625. This event
was not considered related to LEN. No additional deaths were reported with follow-up through
July 27, 2021 in the Applicant’s 90-Day Safety Report.
(b) (6)

Subject
was a 61-year-old white male in the United States. At baseline, he had a
prior history of non-Hodgkin lymphoma, his HIV-1 RNA was 112,000 copies/mL, and his CD4+
cell count was 7/µL (0.8%). His background regimen included ibalizumab, dolutegravir,
emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide fumarate, and fosamprenavir. On Study Day 15, his HIV-1
RNA was 1250 copies/mL but rebounded to 101,000 copies/mL on Day 50.

78
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
On Day 38 the subject experienced serious dizziness of Grade 3. LEN dose was not changed.
The event was considered not related to study drug and resolved on Day 43.
On Day 80 the subject experienced experienced a Grade 5 SAE of “neoplasm malignant”. Due to
deteriorating clinical status during hospitalization, the subject was transferred to hospice, where
he subsequently died. His cause of death was updated to “metastatic cancer”, and this was a
suspected recurrence of his prior non-Hodgkin’s lymphoma. He expired prior to biopsy of the
new lesions, no autopsy was performed, nor was any further specific diagnosis on the type of
cancer provided by the investigator.
Reviewer comment: Given the prior history and timing of the onset of symptoms, the actual start
of the adverse event of “metastatic cancer” was likely prior to the time of study enrollment and
was not related to LEN.

of 72 subjects); no SAEs were reported in more than one subject. No SAEs were considered

(b) (6)

Subject
was a 24-year-old male in Cohort 1 who had COVID-19. The event
started on Day 266, resolved/recovered on Day 275, and was considered to be unrelated to
the study drug.
(b) (6)
Subject
was a 50-year-old male in Cohort 1 who had the SAE of pneumonia
(reported term: multifocal pneumonia). The event started on Day 465, resolved/recovered
on Day 467, and was considered to be unrelated to the study drug.
(b) (6)
Subject
was a 51-year-old female in Cohort 2 who had a baseline CD4+ cell
count of 14 cells/μL experienced the SAEs of septic shock (Days 177 to 197), and renal
impairment and shock (both events, Days 221 to 236). The SAEs of renal impairment and
the second event of shock were initially considered related to the study drug by the
investigator. Renal function improved promptly while LEN exposure continued, and both

(b) (6)

7.6.3.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events

No TEAEs were reported in Study 4625 that led to discontinuation through follow-up period
ending April 01, 2021. Since the original NDA data cut date through follow-up period ending
July 27, 2021, one subject prematurely discontinued study drug due to TEAEs of injection site
nodules, both of which were Grade 1 and were ongoing with follow-up through June 25, 2021.

presented in Table 43. Nausea was the only AE reported in more than one subject. See Table 36
for a listing of listing of TEAEs from the Week 26 interim analysis of Study 4625, including the
Maintenance Period.

7.6.3.6. Laboratory Findings
In both the LEN and placebo groups, 75% of subjects had a postbaseline laboratory abnormality
of Grade 1 or higher during the FMP. Grade 3 laboratory abnormalities were reported for 3
subjects (12.5%) in the LEN group (increased creatinine, hyperglycemia [nonfasting], and
increased lipase) and no subjects in the placebo group. No subjects experienced any Grade 4
laboratory abnormalities. See Table 44.

subjects in the comparator arm (B/F/TAF) had a graded abnormality. Please see Table 37,
Table 38, and Table 39 in Section 7.6.2.
Grade 4 laboratory abnormalities were reported only in the Total LEN group (5% [7 of 157
subjects]) and included high CK (3 subjects), high ALT (1 subject), hyperbilirubinemia (1
subject), fasting triglycerides (1 subject), high lipase (1 subject), and nonfasting hyperglycemia
(1 subject).

7.6.5.2. Deaths
There were no subjects in the pooled phase 1 studies that experienced any AE leading to death.
7.6.5.3. Serious Adverse Events
In the pooled analysis, SAEs were reported for one subject (1.8%) in the LEN group (tibia
fracture) and one subject (5.0%) in the placebo group (sepsis). Neither of the SAEs were
considered related to study drug or led to discontinuation of study drug.
7.6.5.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse
Events
For Study 5709, one subject prematurely discontinued study and study drug due to a Grade 1 AE
of severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2. The AE was considered not serious and not
related to study drug. No new study drug discontinuations due to AEs were reported in Study
5709 with the 90-Day Safety Update.

In the pooled phase 1 analysis, the four most commonly reported AEs in each treatment group
were all ISRs. A listing of TEAEs (reported in >5% of subjects overall) are provided in Table 49.

Although not part of the Applicant’s Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) analysis for Studies
4538 and 5709, in Cohort 3 of Study GS-US-200-4329 (Study 4329; a phase 1 study evaluating
the safety, tolerability, PK, metabolism, and excretion of LEN following a single IV dose in
healthy subjects), one subject who received LEN 20 mg had a Grade 4 SAE of rhabdomyolysis
which was considered related to study drug and resolved during continued exposure to LEN. The
serum creatine phosphokinase level elevated to a peak of 380,000 U/L (normal range 0 to
250 U/L), which was accompanied by increased lactate dehydrogenase (585 U/L), but the subject
remained asymptomatic. There were no reports of recent strenuous exercise, trauma, illicit
substance use, or prior creatine phosphokinase elevations. The time of onset of the
rhabdomyolysis (Day 22) for this subject was 21 days after the Cmax of LEN was reached (within
1 hour of dosing), and the LEN concentration was approximately 150-fold lower on Day 22 than
Cmax on Day 1 (Day 1: 210 ng/mL; Day 22: 1.43 ng/mL). In addition, creatine phosphokinase
levels for this subject continued to decrease in the presence of ongoing LEN exposure.
Clinical reviewer’s comment: Given the lack of presenting symptoms, delayed time of onset, and
resolution despite continued LEN exposure, this presentation would be unusual for drug-induced
rhabdomyolysis.

7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of
Risk
7.7.1. Resistance Assessments of Virologic Failures
Issue
The assessment of resistance emergence in virologic failures of an LEN-containing ARV
regimen is critical to interpreting the overall risk-benefit of LEN and understanding the
durability of LEN as a component of an ARV regimen in an HTE patient population.

Background
Genotypic and phenotypic analyses of the HIV-1 protease, reverse transcriptase (RT), integrase,
and capsid (CA) proteins were performed at screening for all subjects enrolled in Study 4625.

91
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Resistance testing at postscreening timepoints was performed for any subject meeting the criteria
of the resistance analysis, which included any subject who received at least one dose of study
drug and met one of the following virologic failure (VF) criteria:
•
•
•
•

HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL and <1 log10 HIV-1 RNA reduction from the start of oral LEN
at the Week 4 visit, confirmed at a scheduled or unscheduled visit at least 2 weeks
following Week 4.
At any visit, after achieving HIV-1 RNA <50 copies/mL, a rebound in HIV-1 RNA to
≥50 copies/mL, which was subsequently confirmed at the following scheduled or
unscheduled visit; OR
At any visit, a >1 log10 increase in HIV-1 RNA from the nadir, which was subsequently
confirmed at the following scheduled or unscheduled visit.
Any study subject with HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL at the study endpoint or study
discontinuation who did not meet any of the criteria above.

Monogram Biosciences, Inc. (South San Francisco, CA, USA) was the designated reference
laboratory for all resistance analyses at screening and virologic failure. The PhenoSense GT®
assay (Monogram Biosciences), which determines the genotype for the entire HIV-1 protease
and amino acids 1 to 305 of RT and measures the drug susceptibility of all marketed protease
and RT inhibitors based on that sequence, was performed for all subjects at screening. The
GeneSeq Integrase assay was used to determine the sequence of the entire IN protein, and the
PhenoSense Integrase assay measured the drug susceptibility of all marketed INSTIs based on
that sequence (Monogram Biosciences). In cases of assay failure, alternative assays such as
GeneSeq, GenoSure mg, or GenoSure Integrase (Monogram Biosciences) were used to obtain
genotypic information.
HIV-1 CA genotype was determined using the GenoSure Gag-Pro assay, which uses nextgeneration sequencing (NGS) to detect codon variants present at >10% frequency (Monogram
Biosciences). The PhenoSense Gag-Pro assay (Monogram Biosciences) was used to assess
phenotypic response to LEN.
The phenotypic susceptibility of screening samples from enrolled subjects to entry inhibitors
(enfuvirtide, fostemsavir, ibalizumab, maraviroc) was analyzed at the end of the enrollment
period. These analyses were conducted using a combination of the Trofile and PhenoSense Entry
assays (Monogram Biosciences), which determine the co-receptor tropism and the susceptibility
to the inhibitors, respectively, based on the HIV-1 envelope from the subject’s sample.

Assessment
Key Capsid Resistance-Associated Substitutions Selected in Cell Culture
LEN-resistant viruses were selected with increasing concentrations of LEN in MT-2 cells
following 19 to 103 days in cell culture passage (see Section III.18).
•
•

At low concentrations, LEN selected for N74D, which conferred 16-fold decreased
susceptibility to LEN.
At higher drug concentrations, LEN selected for Q67H+N74D, which conferred
>2757-fold decreased susceptibility to LEN.

92
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
•

For the LEN selected resistance substitutions, there was no change in susceptibility to any
of the tested inhibitors from other antiretroviral classes (atazanavir [PI], dolutegravir
[INSTI], efavirenz [NNRTI], and elvitegravir [INSTI]).

LEN-resistant viruses were selected at fixed LEN concentrations in PBMCs over 35 days (See
Section III.18).
•
•

Within the projected human plasma exposure range, breakthrough viruses exhibited 5.5- to
>200-fold decreases in LEN susceptibility and encoded substitutions N74D (16-fold), L56I
(204-fold), Q67H (5-fold), Q67H + N74S (20-fold), or T107N (87-fold).
At higher concentrations, the treatment-emergent viruses encoded substitutions M66I
(>2757-fold), L56I (204-fold), K70N (assay failure), or M66I+Q67H (1594-fold).

Variants containing single or double substitutions conferred reduced susceptibility to LEN (3.8to >2757-fold) with highest reductions seen with L56I (204-fold), M66I (>2757-fold), M66I
+Q67H (1594-fold), Q67H+N74D (>2757-fold), and Q67H+T107N (87-fold) (Table 51 and
Section III.18). These mutant viruses were fully susceptible to PIs atazanavir (fold-change range:
0.9- to 1.5) and darunavir (fold-change range: 0.9- to 1.7), indicating the absence of crossresistance to PIs for these LEN-resistant mutants.

Gag-Pro Assay

Next-Generation Sequencing Analysis
An independent assessment was performed by Dr. Eric Donaldson, clinical virology reviewer,
using the next-generation sequencing data derived from subjects in clinical Study 4625 who were
determined to be treatment failures (Section III.19). The independent analysis compared the
capsid gene changes from baseline and postbaseline samples for each virologic failure subject
(Table 55). This independent assessment confirmed the emergent substitutions shown in
Table 55. Based on the next-generation sequencing analysis, the A78S substitution appears to be
polymorphic, because it is present at baseline and later timepoints in several subjects.
Resistance to Drugs in Optimized Background Regimen
Of the subjects who had postbaseline OBR resistance data (two in the LEN arm and five in the
placebo arm), none had evidence of resistance emergence to the drugs in the OBR (Table 56).
(b) (6)
However, three virologic failure subjects (one in the LEN arm [Subject
] and two in
(b) (6)
placebo arm [Subjects
]) had no active drugs in their OBR based
on baseline and postbaseline genotypic and phenotypic data. We note that two of these subjects
(b) (6)
(Subjects
) also had emergent LEN resistance.
Most of the virologic failures had BIC or DTG (82%; 9/11) or DRV (63%; 7/11) as a component
of their OBR with 45% (5/11) using DRV plus BIC or DTG (Table 56). In addition, ibalizumab
and maraviroc were each used in two different virologic failures and one virologic failure had
both ibalizumab and maraviroc in their OBR. Three of the virologic failures had resistance to

Effect of Optimized Background Regimen on Efficacy
Given that OBR was added with LEN at the end of the FMP, the ARVs in the OBR were critical
to the durability of the LEN-containing regimen. As shown in Table 57, the INSTIs BIC and
DTG were used in the OBR of 69% (25/36) of the subjects and the PI DRV was used in the OBR
of 58% (21/36) of the subjects with 36% (13/36) of the subjects having both DRV plus BIC or
DTG as components of their OBR. Only 8% (3/36) of subjects in Cohort 1 did not have DTG,
BIC, or DRV as a component of their OBR. Additionally, 28% (10/36) were taking IBL, 8%
(3/36) were taking FTR, and 3% (3/36) were taking ENF, as a component of their OBR.
The effect of these anchor drugs as components of the OBR on efficacy at Week 26 was
assessed. Sixty-four percent (16/25) of subjects with DTG or BIC, 67% (14/21) of subjects with
DRV, and 62% (8/13) of subjects with DRV+DTG or BIC in the OBR had HIV-1 RNA
<50 copies/mL at Week 26 (Table 57). Only three subjects did not have BIC, DTG or DRV as
components in their OBR and all three had <50 copies/mL at Week 26. One of these subjects
(b) (6)
(Subject
) had a baseline viral load of only 274 copies/mL, which indicates they were
not failing on their background regimen. The other two subjects were resistant to BIC, DTG and
(b) (6)

Cross-Resistance
Lenacapavir demonstrated antiviral activity in cell culture against a broad spectrum of HIV-1
site-directed mutants and patient-derived HIV-1 isolates with resistance to the four main classes
of antiretroviral agents (INSTIs, NNRTIs, NRTIs, and PIs; n=58), and to viruses resistant to
fostemsavir (gp120-directed attachment inhibitor), ibalizumab (CD4-directed postattachment
inhibitor), maraviroc (C-C chemokine receptor [CCR5] coreceptor inhibitor), and enfuvirtide
(gp41 fusion inhibitor) (n=42) (Section III.18). In addition, the antiviral activity of LEN in
patient isolates was unaffected by the presence of naturally occurring Gag polymorphisms at gag
cleavage sites with or without PI resistance.
PIs (atazanavir, darunavir, nelfinavir, and saquinavir) showed wildtype antiviral activity against
LEN site-directed mutants with capsid substitutions Q67H or Y, Q67H+N74D, Q67H+N74S,
and Q67H+T107N, N74D, and T107N indicating a lack of cross-resistance with PIs.
Conclusion
HIV-1 variants with reduced susceptibility to lenacapavir have been selected in cell culture.
Resistance selections with lenacapavir identified seven substitutions in capsid: L56I, M66I,
Q67H, K70N, N74D/S, and T107N singly or in dual combination. Phenotypic susceptibility to
lenacapavir was reduced 4- to >3,226-fold, relative to wild-type virus.
In the randomized Cohort 1 of CAPELLA, 31% (11/36; six in LEN Cohort 1A and five in
placebo Cohort 1B) of heavily treatment-experienced subjects were virologic failures at Week 26
(confirmed HIV-1 RNA ≥50 copies/mL). Lenacapavir resistance-associated capsid substitutions
were found in 36% (n=4) of the 11 virologic failure subjects with the M66I CA substitution
emerging in all 4 virologic failures, alone or in combination with other lenacapavir resistanceassociated capsid substitutions including Q67Q/H, K70K/N/R/S, N74D, and A105T. These
treatment-emergent capsid resistance substitutions were confirmed by FDA independent
analyses. The virologic failure isolates with emergent capsid resistance-associated substitutions
had reduced lenacapavir susceptibility ranging from 46- to >1428-fold. There were no treatmentemergent resistance substitutions to components of the optimized background regimen in the

98
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
virologic failures, but based on genotypic and phenotypic data, three virologic failures had no
active drugs in their OBR.
•
•
•

LEN resistance emerges readily in virologic failures and thus it is important to use LEN as
a component of a complete active ARV regimen.
LEN resistance substitutions do not confer cross-resistance to other ARVs.
Longer term data from all subjects after Week 26 is required to determine durability of
LEN and the other ARVs in the OBR.

7.7.2. Injection Site Reactions
Issue
Injection site reactions were identified as the most common adverse drug reaction among
subjects who received subcutaneous injections of lenacapavir; however, long-term experience of
subjects after multiple injections was limited in Studies 4625 and 4334.

Background
Other prior experience with long-acting subcutaneously injectable antiretrovirals that have a
similar dosing regimen includes Cabenuva (cabotegravir and rilpivirine). Injection site reactions
were the most common adverse reactions reported with Cabenuva and were described usually
(79%) as localized pain or discomfort (Cabenuva 2021). Fuzeon (enfuvirtide) is another
injectable antiretroviral drug with 98% of subjects experiencing at least one injection site
reaction (described as pain, discomfort, erythema, nodules and cysts, and ecchymosis) (Fuzeon
2019); however, this product is indicated to be administered twice daily.
Injection site nodules have been reported with a number of other drug classes, including
glucagon-like peptide 1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists used for treatment of patients with type 2
diabetes. General injection-site reactions (e.g., erythema, pain, or rash) have been reported with
all of the commercially available GLP-1 receptor agonists (i.e., exenatide, lixisenatide,
liraglutide, dulaglutide, and semaglutide (Hearn and Sherman 2021). Injection site nodules have
rarely been described with other long-acting or extended-release drug products, such as
exenatide. Although the mechanism for development of such nodules is not entirely clear,
available histopathology data reveal the presence of eosinophils and granuloma at these sites, and
therefore, hypersensitivity to the formulation may be one plausible mechanism (Jones et al.
2015). Surgical excision or some other medical intervention, such as serial intralesional
corticosteroid injections, may be required for treatment of persistent lesions.
Lenacapavir is a capsid inhibitor with not only a new mechanism of action, but also unique,
albeit not fully understood, physical properties when injected that provide its sustained exposure.

Assessment
Injection site reactions were the most common adverse reaction reported with LEN SC injection.
Preferred terms related to injection site in Studies 4625 or 4334 were further explored in the
following table (Table 58). Injection site swelling, erythema, pain, nodule, and induration were
the specific MedDRA preferred terms that were most frequently (>10%) reported. None of the
ISRs were considered serious.

A consultation request was submitted to the Division of Dermatology and Dentistry in the Office
of New Drugs that requested any recommendations for additional clinical safety information to
be submitted regarding these reactions and recommendations for communication of injection site
reactions in labeling. The Division of Dermatology and Dentistry recommended that in ongoing
trials, where possible, injection site nodules be followed to resolution or stabilization. Human
histopathology should be obtained to rule out the presence of findings such as necrosis,
hypersensitivity, granulomas, foreign bodies, and fibrous tissue. In addition, the Division of
Dermatology and Dentistry agreed with the expanded characterization of injection site reactions
in Section 6 (Adverse Reactions) of the label, and that elevation to Section 5 (Warnings and
Precautions) was not necessary at this time given there were no reports to date of AEs that were
considered serious or life-threatening.
As discussed in further detail in the Integrated Quality Review, the LEN injectable drug
substance has a high pH (>9) and no intrinsic release controls. Additionally, the proposed vial
containers (borosilicate), which were also used in clinical trials, demonstrated incompatibility
with the drug product, degradation of the vial surface, and glass particles found in the solution.

Conclusion
Injection site reactions can be characterized as having a short-term phase lasting a few days (e.g.,
pain, swelling, and erythema) or a long-term phase (e.g., indurations and nodules), some of
which were still resolving over a period of weeks to months. Indurations and nodules were
generally described as palpable with an elongated shape (up to 1 to 4 cm), not visible, not
painful, and not associated with any erythema. Nodules were more visible in subjects who were
107
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
leaner with little fat tissue in the abdomen. Several subjects in Study 4625 had indurations or
nodules that had not fully resolved at the time of the last safety update. Due to the lack of
understanding of the histopathology at the injection sites in patients who experienced longer
times to resolution of indurations and nodules, and the relationship with the unique properties of
the drug product solution, it is unclear whether these subjects may have experienced permanent
tissue damage. Additionally, given the concern about the incompatibility of the drug substance
with borosilicate glass and resulting delamination of the vial containers (please refer to the
chemistry, manufacturing, and controls review), contribution of glass particulate contamination
cannot be ruled out.
During the initial exchange of updates to the draft labeling for LEN, the Division of Antivirals
(DAV) requested additional description of injection site reactions similar to the presentation in
the Cabenuva label, with additional description of nodules and induration and details of time to
resolution provided as a separate subsection entitled “Injection-Associated Adverse Reactions”
in Section 6 (Adverse Reactions) of the proposed label.
From a clinical safety perspective, additional information is needed to understand the
contributing factors of injection site reactions with LEN, particularly for subjects who have
prolonged resolution with nodules and indurations. Where clinically indicated, at the discretion
of an independent dermatologist, DAV requested that the Applicant obtain human histopathology
(e.g., punch biopsy), and specimens should be examined by an independent dermatopathologist.

on Full Clinical Hold due to the lack of information needed to assess risks of lenacapavir
injection and the extent to which glass particulate contamination, including subvisible particles,
may be associated with injection site nodules, indurations, or other clinical effects.

7.7.3. Background Regimens Containing Boosted
Protease Inhibitors
Issue
The risk of supratherapeutic LEN exposures when LEN is combined with a background regimen
containing boosted protease inhibitors was not prospectively assessed in Study 4625.

Background
Most regimens containing boosted protease inhibitors (BPIs) are typically used as second-line
options, or when INSTIs are not available. The HTE patient population by definition has limited
alternative options to construct a complete regimen; therefore, BPIs may be an important
component of an optimized background regimen.
108
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Based on in vitro data, LEN was found to be a substrate of cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A and
uridine diphosphate glucuronosyltransferase (UGT)1A1 enzymes and p-glycoprotein (P-gp)
transporters. In the dedicated DDI study, Study GS-US-200-4333 (Study 4333), an approximate
two-fold increase in LEN exposures was observed with mixed CYP3A/P-gp inhibition associated
with cobicistat, and an approximate four- to six-fold increase LEN exposure was observed with
mixed CYP3A/P-gp/ UGT) inhibition associated with atazanavir/cobicistat; therefore, given the
potential for clinically significant drug-drug interactions with LEN due to the combined strong
inhibition of CYP3A, P-gp, and UGT1A1 together, Study 4625 did not allow concomitant use of
atazanavir (ATV, an inhibitor of CYP3A and UGT1A1) when boosted with either cobicistat
(COBI) or ritonavir (RTV). The results from Study 4333 also showed an 84% decrease in LEN
AUC when LEN was coadministered with rifampin, a strong CYP3A inducer; and 56% decrease
in LEN AUC when LEN was coadministered with efavirenz, a moderate CYP3A inducer.
CYP3A inducers (e.g., rifampin, efavirenz, tipranavir) were disallowed in Study 4625.
Background regimens with other BPIs were allowed. Please refer to Section 8.2.2 for additional
discussion of drug interactions.

Assessment
Clinical Safety Experience With Supratherapeutic Doses of LEN
Clinical safety experience was reviewed from two clinical studies where doses of LEN were
predicted to provide higher exposures than what was studied in Study 4625. Study 4332 was a
phase 1 QT study, which evaluated a 600 mg dose of LEN (2×300 mg tablets) taken orally twicedaily for 8 days to achieve supratherapeutic exposure. Study 5709 was a phase 1 PK study, and
Cohort 3 evaluated a 600 mg dose of LEN (2×300 mg tablets) taken orally twice daily for
10 days. Although direct comparisons between these studies are limited (because they are in
different patient populations: healthy volunteers versus treatment-naïve and heavily treatmentexperienced subjects), the clinical safety experience from this analysis may be informative
regarding potential exposure-related effects attributable to LEN.

Conclusion
Analysis of safety experience with LEN and regimens containing BPIs revealed adverse event
and laboratory profiles previously anticipated with BPIs. With the exception of regimens
containing ATV/COBI or ATV/RTV, no additional significant safety concerns were revealed
with dose exploration studies and similar supratherapeutic exposures of LEN (up to
approximately two-fold higher) that would be expected when combined with BPIs.
Labeling for LEN should include the recommendation against concomitant administration with
ATV/COBI or ATV/RTV, but no clinically significant drug interactions have been observed
with DRV/COBI.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
8.1.1. Renal Impairment
Results from Study 4329 (mass balance study) showed that renal excretion is a minor excretion
pathway of LEN, representing 0.237% of total dose. However, severe renal impairment (RI) may
affect uptake and efflux transporters and metabolic enzymes in the liver and gastrointestinal
tract. Increases in exposure in subjects with severe RI for drugs that are not renally excreted may
be due to the effects of uremic toxin on either intestinal or hepatic cell transporters, metabolizing
enzymes, or both. This phenomenon has been observed for other drugs, such as raloxifene and
revefenacin, which show significantly higher exposures in subjects with severe RI despite not
being primarily eliminated renally.
In the RI Study 4330, subjects with severe RI (estimated creatinine clearance ≥15 mL/min) were
administered 300 mg single oral dose of LEN. LEN geometric least-squares mean (GLSM) AUC
111
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
and Cmax were 162% and 84% higher, respectively, in subjects with severe renal impairment
compared to subjects with normal renal function. As LEN has linear PK, these results can be
extrapolated to the recommended dosing regimen. Based on cross-study PK comparisons, even
larger exposure increases were observed without safety concerns in other studies. As shown in
Table 65, in Studies 4332 and 5709, the magnitude of increase in LEN Cmax and AUC following
600 mg BID relative to the therapeutic exposure was higher compared with the magnitude of
increase in LEN Cmax and AUC in Study 4330. Therefore, the impact of RI on LEN PK is not
considered clinically significant.

The review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposed labeling that no dose adjustment of LEN
is required in patients with mild, moderate, or severe RI, and that LEN has not been studied in
patients with end-stage renal disease (estimated creatinine clearance <15 mL/min)
(Section III.13.2).

8.1.2. Hepatic Impairment
In vitro studies indicated that LEN is a substrate of enzymes CYP3A5 and UGT1A1. In addition,
metabolism of LEN was also observed in the clinical Study 4329. Therefore, hepatic impairment
(HI) is expected to have an impact on LEN PK.
In HI Study 4331, subjects with moderate HI (Child-Pugh Class B) were administered a 300 mg
single oral dose of LEN. GLSM LEN AUC and Cmax were 47% and 161% higher, respectively,
in subjects with moderate HI compared to subjects with normal hepatic function. As shown in
Table 65, in Studies 4332 and 5709, the magnitude of increase in GLSM mean Cmax and AUC
following 600 mg BID relative to the therapeutic exposure was higher compared with the
magnitude of increase in Cmax and AUC in Studies 4331. Therefore, the impact of moderate HI
on LEN PK is not considered clinically significant.
The review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposed labeling that no dose adjustment of LEN
is required in patients with mild or moderate HI (Child-Pugh Class A or B), and that LEN has
not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh C) (Section III.13.2).

8.1.3. Other Intrinsic Factors
Population PK analyses indicate that sex, race, age (18 to 78 years), and body weight do not have
a clinically significant effect on LEN PK. No subjects >78 years old were enrolled in the clinical
studies (see Section III.14.3).

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
8.2.1. Food Effect
Study GS-US-200-4071 evaluated the impact of a standard light meal (400 kcal and 25% fat) and
the impact of a high-fat meal (1000 kcal and 50% fat) on LEN PK. Two LEN solution-in-capsule
formulations and the to-be-marketed oral tablet formulation were used in the study.
Regardless of the calorie and fat content of the meal, food had no significant effect on plasma
LEN PK. LEN was given without regard to food in the Phase 3 trials CAPELLA and
CALIBRATE. The review team agrees with the Applicant’s proposed labeling that LEN can be
administered with or without food (see Section III.14.2).

8.2.2. Drug-Drug Interactions
Effects of Other Drugs on LEN
In vitro studies (see Section III.13.1 for details) indicate that LEN is a substrate of CYP3A, P-gp
and UGT1A1, but not a substrate of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19,
CYP2D6, breast cancer resistance protein, or organic anion transporter polypeptide (OATP)1B1
and OATP1B3. In vitro studies to evaluate if LEN is a substrate of renal transporters were not
conducted because the absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion data suggest that active
renal secretion is not expected to be significant.
Drug-drug interaction Study 4333 (see Section III.14.2 for details) was conducted in healthy
volunteers to evaluate the effect of CYP3A, P-gp, and UGT1A1 modulators as well as the effect
of an acid-reducing agent on the PK of LEN.

Coadministration With Inhibitors of CYP3A, P-gp, and/or UGT1A1
Coadministration of drugs that are inhibitors of CYP3A (strong), P-gp, and UGT1A1 together
(i.e., atazanavir [ATV]/COBI or ATV/RTV) with LEN during treatment is not recommended. Of
note, use of ATV was not allowed in Studies 4625 and 4334. Clinical DDI results confirmed the
in vitro findings and showed that an inhibitor of CYP3A (strong), P-gp, and UGT1A1 together
(ATV/COBI) increased LEN concentrations to a larger degree than coadministration with an
inhibitor of CYP3A (strong) and P-gp (COBI and DRV/COBI) or a strong CYP3A inhibitor
alone (voriconazole) (Table 67).
Coadministration of LEN with a strong inhibitor of CYP3A and P-gp (e.g., DRV/RTV,
DRV/COBI) together, a strong CYP3A inhibitor alone, or a P-gp inhibitor alone is not expected
to result in a clinically significant effect on LEN concentrations. In Study 4625, 18 (29%)
subjects coadministered LEN with COBI, 19 (30.6%) subjects with RTV, and 19 (30.6%) with
DRV. In addition, the increase of LEN Cmax (<3-fold) due to coadministration with a strong
inhibitor of CYP3A and P-gp together, a strong CYP3A inhibitor alone, or a P-gp inhibitor alone
is much less than the 9-fold higher Cmax observed following oral LEN 600 mg BID (Studies 5709
and 4332, up to 11 days) versus the Cmax observed with the regimen studied in the pivotal trial
without safety concerns. Safety data from Cohort 3 of Study 4334 (LEN oral 600 mg on day 1
and day 2, and oral 50 mg once daily starting day 3), which provided comparable Cmax and Ctrough
but about 2-fold higher AUC0-6month as compared to the proposed regimen, also supports
coadministration of LEN with a strong inhibitor of CYP3A and P-gp together, a strong CYP3A
inhibitor alone, or a P-gp inhibitor alone.
Coadministration With Inducers of CYP3A
Coadministration of strong inducers of CYP3A during treatment with LEN is contraindicated
and coadministration of moderate inducers of CYP3A during treatment with LEN is not
recommended. Drugs that are strong inducers of CYP3A (e.g., rifampin), or that are moderate
inducers of CYP3A (e.g., efavirenz), may significantly decrease plasma concentrations of LEN,
which may result in loss of therapeutic effect of LEN and development of resistance. A strong
inducer of CYP3A is expected to be an inducer of P-gp and UGT1A1 with lower potency
because CYP3A, P-gp, and UGTs are all regulated by the pregnane X receptor. Therefore, other
strong inducers of CYP3A are expected to decrease LEN concentrations by a similar magnitude
as rifampin. Although exposure-response (E-R) relationship analyses for Study 4625 indicated
that there was no clear E-R relationship identified for efficacy or safety (see Section 6.1), strong
or moderate CYP3A inducers were not allowed in pivotal trials. In addition, although based on
the exploratory E-R relationship analysis (Study 4072), near maximum efficacy is expected in
the lowest quartile of Ctrough (see Section III.25). A decrease in LEN exposures by strong or
moderate CYP3A inducers will most likely result in LEN exposures in the range for which the
current E-R relationships may not be applicable.
Coadministration With Acid-Reducing Agents
LEN is practically insoluble in aqueous media, gastric fluid, or intestinal fluid and exhibits
higher solubility at pH ≥6. The effect of famotidine LEN exposures was assessed.
Administration of a single dose of LEN 2 hours after famotidine did not result in clinically

115
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
significant changes in LEN PK (28% increase in mean LEN AUCinf). Accordingly, there are no
restrictions for use of LEN with acid-reducing agents.
Effect of LEN on Other Drugs
Considering the results of both in vitro and in vivo DDI studies, LEN is a moderate inhibitor of
CYP3A and is an inhibitor of P-gp.
The DDI potential evaluation was based on FDA Guidance for Industry: In Vitro Drug
Interaction Studies—Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions
(January 2020). In vitro studies indicate that LEN is an inhibitor of both gut and hepatic CYP3A,
mainly due to time-dependent or mechanism-based inhibition. Using static mechanistic models,
the ratio of LEN AUC (AUCr) of oral LEN coadministered with a CYP3A inhibitor to oral LEN
alone is predicted to be approximately 5-fold, with about 1.7-fold due to gut CYP3A inhibition
and 2.9-fold due to hepatic CYP3A inhibition. The predicted AUCr is about 2.8-fold following
coadministration of a CYP3A inhibitor with SC LEN compared to SC LEN alone. In vitro
studies also indicate that LEN is not expected to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, CYP2D6 or UGT 1A1. LEN is an inhibitor of OATP1B1 or OATP1B3, but is not
likely to inhibit P-gp, breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), OAT1, OAT3, organic cation
transporter (OCT)1, OCT2, multidrug and toxin extrusion protein (MATE)1, or MATE2K.In
vitro studies indicate LEN is not expected to be an inducer of CYP enzymes.
As shown in Table 67, DDI Study 4333 showed that LEN is a moderate inhibitor of CYP3A (as
observed by coadministration of LEN with midazolam [MDZ] resulting in a 259% increase in
mean AUCinf). MDZ administered 1 day after LEN resulted in a similar magnitude of effect on
MDZ concentrations, indicating that the effect of LEN on MDZ concentrations is mainly through
hepatic CYP3A inhibition.
Concomitant use of LEN with CYP3A substrates can increase the exposures and risk of adverse
reactions of these substrates. Avoid coadministration of LEN with CYP3A substrates where
minimal concentration changes may lead to serious toxicities.
Study 4333 also showed that LEN appeared to inhibit P-gp transporter (as observed by
coadministration of LEN with TAF resulting in a 32% increase in AUCinf) and BCRP transporter
(as observed by coadministration of LEN with rosuvastatin resulting in a 31% increase in
AUCinf). Although in vitro data suggested that LEN is a weak inhibitor of OATP1B1, LEN does
not inhibit OATP transporters in vivo (based on no significant changes in PIT exposures).
Therefore, substrates of BCRP and OATP can be coadministered with LEN while therapeutic
monitoring of digoxin (a P-gp substrate) concentrations is recommended when LEN is
coadministered with digoxin.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
This application was not taken to an FDA advisory committee because the application did not
raise significant safety or efficacy issues that were unexpected and there were no controversial
issues that would benefit from discussion by an advisory committee.

III. Appendices
12. Summary of Regulatory History
Lenacapavir (LEN), formerly known as GS-6207, is a human immunodeficiency virus (HIV)-1
capsid inhibitor that is being developed as a solution for subcutaneous injection under
investigational new drug (IND) 136260 and as a tablet for oral use under IND 138311. The
dosage forms were intended to be used in conjunction for the treatment of HIV-1 infection.
On 30 August, 2017, Gilead Sciences submitted a meeting request under Pre-IND 136260 for
LEN, injection. Pre-IND guidance on the nonclinical studies needed to support the alternative
injectable formulations of LEN that Gilead planned to use in their clinical trials was provided to
Gilead on 25 October, 2017 via a Written Response correspondence.
IND 136260 was submitted 21 December, 2017 and the Agency deemed it safe-to-proceed on 20
January, 2018. Fast Track Designation was granted on 23 March, 2018 for the treatment of HIV1 infection on the basis that the submitted information was sufficiently likely to support use of
(b) (4)
LEN as
parenteral formulation with a new mechanism of action, and
therefore, LEN demonstrated the potential to address an unmet medical need.
On 20 March, 2018, Gilead submitted a new IND 138311 for lenacapavir tablets, for the
treatment of HIV-1 infection and the Agency deemed the IND safe to proceed on 19 April, 2018.
Fast Track Designation was granted on 19 September, 2018 for the treatment of HIV-1 infection.
On 30 May, 2019, the development program for LEN (IND 136260) for the treatment of HIV-1
infection in heavily treatment-experienced (HTE) patients with multidrug resistant (MDR) virus
was granted Breakthrough Therapy Designation. The decision was based upon the need for an
antiretroviral agent with a new mechanism of action and no overlapping resistance with any
approved agent that could potentially meet the high unmet medical need in HTE patients with
limited treatment options. A Type B, Initial Breakthrough Therapy meeting was held on 6
August, 2019 during which the phase 2/3 development program and proposed registration plan to
support an indication for treatment of HIV-1 infection in HTE patients with MDR HIV-1 was
discussed. The Agency generally agreed with the proposed phase 2/3 Study 4625 to support the
submission of a new drug application (NDA). The clinical trial would investigate the use of a 14day lenacapavir oral lead-in dosing, followed by LEN, subcutaneous injections on Day 15 and
every 6 months.
On 19 March, 2020, a rat carcinogenicity Special Protocol Assessment was submitted to IND
136260; and on 26 June, 2020, a mouse carcinogenicity Special Protocol Assessment was
submitted. The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee agreed with the Special
Protocol Assessments, but they provided recommendations on the design of the studies.
An Agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan was issued by the Agency on 27 May, 2020. The agreed
(b) (4)
initial Pediatric Study Plan covered
, HTE adolescents ≥35 kg with MDR HIV would be enrolled in Study
(b) (4)
4625. However, difficulties in enrollment of this population were anticipated,

(b) (4)

On September 6, 2020, Meeting Preliminary Comments were issued for a Type C Chemistry,
Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC) meeting requested to discuss the proposed excess volume
for LEN 300 mg/mL and the design validation strategy for the lenacapavir vial kit. The meeting
was canceled by the firm following receipt of preliminary comments.
On January 13, 2021, the Agency issued Preliminary Comments for a Type B Pre-NDA meeting
requested under INDs 136260 and 138311, the purpose of which was to discuss the strategy for
submission of NDAs for lenacapavir, key aspects related to content and format of the NDAs, to
obtain feedback on the proposed content of the NDA Safety Update, and to seek feedback and
agreement on the revisions to the dosing regimen used in the Phase 3 clinical trial. In the
Preliminary Comments, the Agency agreed that clinical studies 4625 (primary registrational
study in intended population), 4334 (supportive efficacy and safety study), and 4538 and 5709
(supportive safety studies) were appropriate to submit in the initial NDAs. Regarding the
proposal to submit a simplified regimen that would include oral loading dose on Day 1
and Day 2 with the injection administered on Day 1, the Agency stated that the acceptability of
the dosing regimen is contingent upon availability of adequate pharmacokinetic (PK), safety and
efficacy data and would be a review issue. The Agency further agreed that having a single U.S.
Prescribing Information for both injection and tablet dosage forms is appropriate. Regarding a
transgenic mouse study draft report and SEND databases, the Agency requested that these be
submitted either with the NDA or after approval. Following receipt of the Preliminary Comments
the meeting was canceled.
On 11 February, 2021, the Agency provided the firm with Human Factors Validation Study
Protocol Advice containing recommended protocol revisions in response to a Human Factors
Validation Protocol submitted 11 December, 2020, regarding a co-package kit containing
injection vials, syringes, vial access devices, and injection needles.
On 31 March, 2021, a Proprietary Name Request Unacceptable letter was issued by the Agency,
detailing that the proposed proprietary name, SUNLENCA, could result in medication errors due
to confusion with another product currently under review. Ultimate acceptability of the proposed
name would be dependent upon which underlying application is approved first.
On 25 May, 2021, a Final Written Response was issued by the Agency in response to a type B,
meeting, the purpose of which was to discuss phase 2/3 clinical data from Studies 4625 and 4334
in advance of NDA filing. The Agency agreed that the preliminary data from these studies were
appropriate to support the firm’s proposed initial NDAs.

13. Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional
Information and Assessment
13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted
Under the IND
Selected nonclinical studies were originally submitted and reviewed under INDs 136260 and
138311. All nonclinical safety studies conducted in support of LEN were also submitted to the
present NDA and are reviewed in the following sections.

13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted to
the NDA
The nonclinical studies were originally submitted under IND 138311 for the oral route of
administration and under IND 136260 for the subcutaneous route. Both routes of exposure were
used in clinical trials; the oral as a lead-in and the subcutaneous route for maintenance, given
once every 6 months. All selected nonclinical safety studies conducted in support of LEN (also
referred to as GS-6207 in nonclinical study reports) were also submitted to NDA 215973 and are
reviewed in the following sections.

13.2.1. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted to the
NDA
13.2.1.1. Secondary Pharmacology
At 10µM, LEN showed no response (inhibition or induction >50%) against a panel of 87
receptors, ion channels, and transporters.
13.2.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
