Table 76. Pharmacokinetic Parameters of GS-6207 (12 mg/kg) Following a Single SC
Administration in Male Beagle Dogs (Mean±SD, n=3)

Source: Pharmacokinetic study report AD-200-2051.
Abbreviations: AUC, area under the concentration-time curve; Cmax, maximum concentration; SC, subcutaneous; SD, standard
deviation; Tmax, time to maximum concentration

The PK profile of GS-6207 following intravenous (IV) or oral administration was evaluated in
portal vein cannulated male Cynomolgus monkeys and Beagle dogs with or without the
cytochrome P450 (CYP) inhibitor, aminobenzotriazole, to understand the role of CYP
metabolism of GS-6207. The results from these studies indicated that pretreating dogs and
monkeys with aminobenzotriazole did not change GS-6207 oral expose in dogs or monkeys,
suggesting role of intestinal metabolism of GS-6207.

Distribution
In Vitro
•
•

•

Protein binding was evaluated in plasma from CD-1 mice. GS-6207 was 99.8% proteinbound (unbound fraction was 0.18%) at a concentration of 2µM.
The distribution between the cellular and soluble fractions of blood was evaluated in whole
blood from Sprague Dawley rats, Beagle dogs, Cynomolgus and Rhesus monkeys, and
humans. At an initial concentration of 0.5µM, GS-6207 was nearly excluded from the
blood cellular fraction with the blood:plasma concentration ratios in animals being 0.59 to
0.67 and 0.64 in human.
Protein binding was evaluated in plasma from Sprague Dawley rats, Beagle dogs,
Cynomolgus and Rhesus monkeys, and humans. GS-6207 was ≥98% protein-bound in all
animals and human (unbound fraction was 0.13, 0.83, 1.11, 1.45, and 1.46% in rat, dogs,
Cynomolgus monkey, Rhesus monkey, and human, respectively) at 10mM.

In Vivo
•

The absorption, tissue distribution and excretion of [14C]GS-6207 was evaluated following
a single IV (3 mg/kg-IV; n=3 to 26) dose of GS-6207 in both intact and bile ductcannulated nonpigmented male Wister Hanover and intact pigmented Long Evans rats. The
highest concentration was in the liver in both species of rats up to 1008 hours (42 days)
followed by kidney cortex, kidneys, pancreas, kidney medulla, brown fat, and thyroid. GS6207 was cleared from all organs by 672 hours and in the liver by 1344 hours (56 days).

Metabolism
In Vitro
•

The metabolic profile of GS-6207 was evaluated in Wistar Han rat, Beagle dog,
cynomolgus and Rhesus monkey, and human microsomes and hepatocytes in vitro.
— In all animal species and human microsomes, 90 to 94% of radioactivity detected was
from unchanged [14C]GS-6207. In microsomes, [14C]GS-6207metabolism occurred
via oxidation, reduction, defluorination, and glutathione conjugation.
130

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
— Metabolism in hepatocytes occurred via glutathione conjugation, pentose conjugation,
and glucuronidation. Oxidative metabolites were not detected.
— There were no unique human metabolites.
In Vivo
•
•

The metabolism of [14C]GS-6207 was evaluated in plasma, urine, bile, and feces from
intact and bile duct-cannulated Sprague Dawley rats and Beagle dogs.
The proposed metabolic pathways in the rat, dog and human are presented in Figure 33.

Figure 33. Proposed Biotransformation Pathways of LEN in Sprague-Dawley Rats, Beagle Dogs,
and Humans

Source: Pharmacokinetic written summary.
Abbreviation: LEN, lenacapavir

Excretion
•

•

The excretion of GS-6207 was evaluated following a single 30-minute IV infusion
(1 mg/kg; n=3) in male bile-duct cannulated male Sprague Dawley rats. The results
indicate that GS-6207 is excreted into the intestines by the P-glycoprotein (P-gp)
transporter and excreted in rat feces.
The absorption and excretion of [14C]GS-6207 was evaluated following a single 1 mg/kg
IV dose in intact and bile-duct cannulated Beagle Dogs and a 2 mg/kg oral dose in intact
dogs. The results indicated that GS-6207 was primarily excreted in the feces.

13.2.3. General Toxicology
13.2.3.1. Single-Dose Toxicology/Toxicokinetics
A single dose subcutaneous (SC) dose range-finding toxicology study was conducted to
determine the doses to use in future carcinogenic studies to characterize the potential long-term
carcinogenic effects of GS-6207. The study was evaluated in rasH2 Transgenic mice (0, 30, 99,
or 300 mg/kg) with a 13-week recovery period.
•
•
•
•
•

The skin at the injection site and the liver appeared to be the targeted organs of toxicity.
The liver weight was increased at 99 and 300 mg/kg and was correlated with slight
centrilobular hepatocyte vacuolation, an increased incidence of minimal hepatocyte
degeneration/necrosis, and an increase in alanine aminotransferase (ALT) at 300 mg/kg.
Injection site reactions were observed in various animals at each dose in females and at
300 mg/kg in males; a palpable mass was detected at 300 mg/kg.
Hematology findings (increases in white blood cells, lymphocytes, and eosinophils in
males and leukocytes) were detected sporadically in males and females at each dose.
The no observable adverse effect level (NOAEL) was 300 mg/kg in males and 99 mg/kg in
females due to clinical observations and gross and histopathology findings at the injection
site and histopathology findings in the liver at 300 mg/kg.

A single dose intravenous toxicology study to characterize potential systemic effects of GS-6207
was evaluated via a 30-minute IV infusion to Wister Han rats (0, 3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) with a 15day recovery period.
•
•
•
•
•
•

Several rats dosed with GS-6207 or control had to be euthanized early because of declining
health due to the infusion procedure.
Clinical observations were noted in various rats at each dose (red urine, ataxia,
hypoactivity, vocalization, squinting and/or pale eyes, audible/labored/irregular breathing,
and sternal recumbence).
Decrease in body weight gain was observed in male rats at 30 mg/kg.
Increases in neutrophil counts, ALT, creatine kinase (CK), positive blood urine, and liver
weights were observed at 10 and/or 30 mg/kg.
Hemorrhage of valve leaflets, mixed cell inflammation, and myocardial
degeneration/necrosis was found in dosed and control animals.
Infusion site reactions (cellular inflammation, thrombus, congestion/hemorrhage, edema,
and fibrosis) were noted at all doses.

To characterize potential systemic effects, GS-6207 was evaluated via a single 30-minute IV
infusion to Beagle dogs (0, 3, 10, or 30 mg/kg) with a 15-day recovery period.
•

•

Clinical observations (skin lesions, intermittent tremors, vocalization, and/or discharge of
the left eye and front foot) were observed at all doses and in controls. These observations
were unlikely related to GS-6207and IV dosing procedure because they were noted in
controls.
A GS-6207-related decrease in body weight gain was observed in all dogs at doses
≥3 mg/kg that only correlated with a decrease in food consumption in female dogs. The
decrease in weight in dogs lasted throughout recovery.

132
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
•
•

Hematology findings (red blood cell count, hemoglobin, and hematocrit), prothrombin time
or activated partial thromboplastin time prolongation, and an increase in hepatic enzymes
were noted at 30 mg/kg.
Histopathology findings in the liver included hepatocyte degeneration (at ≥3 mg/kg in
males and ≥10 mg/kg in females), individual necrosis (30 mg/kg in two males and at
10 mg/kg in one female), mixed cell infiltrate (at ≥10 mg/kg in males and females), and
hemorrhage (one male and three females and 30 mg/kg and one female at ≥3 mg/kg). The
GS-6207-related liver findings at Day 2 were not detected at Day 15.

13.2.3.2. Repeat-Dose Toxicology/Toxicokinetics

A 4-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study of GS-6207 in
Wister Han Rats (TX-200-2011)
Key Study Findings
•
•

•
•

No target organ of toxicity was identified.
Longer clotting time (activated partial thromboplastin time) in males was noted at all doses
and an increase in cholesterol was noted in females at all doses. These findings were not
considered adverse and the significance of these findings is unclear as there was no other
data to correlate with the findings.
The NOAEL was 30 mg/kg.
— The Day 28 AUC0-24hr (male and female combined) was 34,500 ng•h/mL.
The exposure margin was 26× based on predicted human exposures based on the
recommended human dose (RHD).

Major Findings

Animals were examined twice daily for mortality, abnormalities, and signs of

Table 89. Nine-Month (Once Monthly Dosing) Dog SC Toxicity Study Findings
Parameter
Major Findings
Mortality
Animals were examined twice daily. There were no treatment related findings.
Clinical signs
Clinical signs were monitored at least once daily. Vocalization; struggling
during dosing; barrel rolling; walking in circles; and/or licking, scratching, and
snapping/biting at the dose site occurred (typically lasting 5 minutes or less) in
control and GS-6207 dosed dogs immediately postdosing after each monthly
dose. These observations did decrease in recurrence at later doses. One
female dosed with 20 mg/kg (with (b) (4) ) was hypoactive, had oral discharge,
slowed heart rate, and labored breathing postdose on Day 29. The veterinarian
treated the animal for anaphylactoid reaction with diphenhydramine and
epinephrine. Later the same day, the animal appeared normal.
GS-6207 related raised area of the skin at the dosing site occurred in
controls and dosed animals. GS-6207 related slight to severe edema and
erythema were observed in dosed animals beginning 24 hours postdose at the
injection sites and noted at all schedule necropsies. Edema persisted longer in
animals dosed with 40 mg/kg than those with 20 mg/kg.

Food intake was measured weekly. No treatment related effects.

necropsy.

No treatment-related effects.

Major Findings
Mortality
Animals were examined twice daily. In the 411 mg/kg group, dosing did not
proceed past Day 57 due to a decline in health due to treatment. One male
and two females were euthanized early on Days 51, 71, and 82, respectively.
(unscheduled interval). An interim necropsy of 411 mg/kg dosed animals

(Day 143) or terminal (Day 268) necropsy.

vocalization, circling, excessive biting and aggression towards handler were
noted in dosed and control animals on Days 1 and 29. Beginning on day 32
animals were anesthetized prior to dosing.

palpable mass, edema, and erythema were noted at 130 and 411 mg/kg.

Body weights were recorded weekly. GS-6207 related decrease in body
weight up to 19% was noted in animals dosed at 411 mg/kg that were
euthanized early.

on Days 87, 115, 143, 171, 199, 227, 225, and 268. GS-6207 related effects
on remaining animals dosed with 411 mg/kg were similar to those from the
animals that were necropsied early and included increased white blood cells
and/or neutrophile counts and fibrinogen concentrations noted at necropsy 1
(Day 73) but not present at terminal necropsy (Day 143) suggesting
reversibility of these effects.
GS-6207 related effects on animals dosed with 130 mg/kg included
increased fibrinogen concentrations on Days 87 and 115 which correlated with
inflammation at the injection sites noted during terminal necropsy (Day 268).

GS-6207-related findings included the following:
• Liver: slight vacuolar hepatocyte; minimal bile duct epithelium degeneration;
minimal to slight bile pigment, and/or minimal to moderate oval cell/bile duct
hyperplasia.
• Gall bladder: Minimal to slight epithelial hyperplasia; minimal edema.
• Kidney: Minimal tubule degeneration and dilatation; and minimal pigment.
• Stomach: minimal to marked degeneration/atrophy of the mucosa.
• Injection sites A, B, and/or C: minimal to marked adipose tissue necrosis,
granulomatous or mix cell inflammation, and/or fibrosis.

• Findings previously noted at 411 mg/kg during necropsy 1 in the stomach
and kidney were not detected, indicating reversal.
• Liver: Previous findings were reverse except oval cell/bile ductule
hyperplasia. These findings were detected at a lower incidence, indicating
partial reversal.
• Gall bladder: Mucosal edema was fully reversed. All previous findings
detected during the previous necropsy were detected at a lesser degree,
indicating partial reversibility.
• Injection sites A, B, and/or C: All previous findings at injection site A were
still detected but at a lesser incidence, indicating partial reversibility.
However, adipose tissue necrosis in females was fully reversed. All findings
previously noted at injection site B were no longer detected, indicating full
reversibility. All previous findings at injection site C were still detecting but
at a lesser degree and/or severity indicating partial reversibility. However,
adipose tissue necrosis was no longer detected in females indicating full
reversibility.
Terminal Necropsy 2 (Day 269)
The only animals remaining at this time point were those dosed with
130 mg/kg.
• Liver: Minimal to slight oval cell/bile ductule hyperplasia; minimal
periductular fibrosis (males only); and a minimal increase in pigmented
macrophages.
• Gall bladder: Minimal epithelium hyperplasia, minimal to slight increase
secretion in the lumen; and minimal to slight pigment in the lumen.
• Injection sites A to J: In general, the following was noted in most, but not all,
injection sites: minimal to moderate granulomatous inflammation, adipose
tissue necrosis, fibrosis, and pseudocyst.

13.2.3.3. General Toxicology; Additional Studies
(Nonpivotal)

A 7-Day Once-Daily Oral Gavage Tolerability and Toxicokinetic Study of GS6207 in Female Nonpregnant New Zealand White Rabbits (TX-200-2035)
(b) (4)

In a 7-day, non-GLP-compliant study, GS-6207 formulated in
was
administered to nonpregnant female new Zealand white rabbits (3/group) at doses of 0 (control),
100, 300, and 1000 mg/kg with dose volumes of 1.3, 0.2, 0.6, and 2 mL/kg, respectively. On
Day 4, one female was found dead and on Day 5 another had to be euthanized in extremis in the
1000 mg/kg dose group. The mortality and extremis condition were due to intubation errors at
dosing. There were no test article related findings in this study. The NOAEL was 1000 mg/kg.

Assessment of Subcutaneous Depot Following a Single Subcutaneous Infusion
of GS-6207 in Wister Han Rat (TX-200-2027)

A 7-Day Subcutaneous and Intramuscular Dose Range-Finding Toxicity
Study of GS-714207 in Male Wister Han Rats (TX-200-2002)
(b) (4)

In a 7-day, non-GLP-compliant exploratory study, GS-6207 formulated in
% sterile water for injection was administered to male Wister Han rats (5
males/group) at doses of 0 (control), 5, 30, and 100 mg/kg at a dose volume of 1 mL/kg via
subcutaneous or intramuscular injection. Changes in hematology (increases in neutrophil,
lymphocytes, and monocytes), coagulation (increase in fibrinogen), and serum chemistry
(increase globulin and decrease in albumin) were observed in animals dosed ≥30 mg/kg via SC
administration, indicating an inflammatory response. Thickening of the injection site was noted
in animals dosed ≥30 mg/kg via SC administration, and discoloration was noted at the injection
site in two animals dosed with 5 mg/kg via intramuscular administration. There was minimal to
marked necrosis at the injection site at all doses via SC administration. The NOAEL for SC
administration, based on injection site reactions, could not be determined due to necrotic tissue at
injection sites. The NOAEL for SC administration based on systemic effects was 100 mg/kg. The
NOAEL for intramuscular administration based on no injection site reactions or systemic effects
observed was 5 mg/kg.

A 7-Day Subcutaneous Injection Dose Range-Finding Toxicity Study of GS714207 in Male Beagle Dog (TX-200-2004)
(b) (4)

In a 7-day, non-GLP-compliant exploratory study, GS-6207 formulated in
% sterile water for injection was administered to male beagle dogs (3
males/group) at doses of 0 (control), 3, 10, and 30 mg/kg at a dose volume of 0.75 mL/kg via
subcutaneous injection. Changes in hematology (increases in neutrophil, monocytes, and white
blood cells; decrease in reticulocytes), coagulation (increase in fibrinogen), and serum chemistry
(increase in ALP; increase globulin and cholesterol; and decrease in the albumin globulin ratio)
were observed at 10 and/or 30 mg/kg. A decrease in thymus weight that correlated with a
decrease in thymus cellularity was observed during histopathology analysis. Injection site
reactions included thickening at the site and a mass noted during gross pathology. Cellular
inflammation, hemorrhage, and fibrin were detected at each dose. An abscess formation was
observed at doses ≥10 mg/kg and vascular inflammation in some animals was observed at 3 and
10 mg/kg. The NOAEL for SC administration, based on injection site reactions and abscess
formation at doses ≥10 mg/kg, was 3 mg/kg. The NOAEL for SC administration based on
systemic effects was 30 mg/kg.

A 30-Minute IV Infusion Dose Range-Finding Study of the Effect of GS-6207
on Embryo-Fetal Development in New Zealand White Rabbits (TX-200-2032)
GS-6207 was administered to New Zealand White rabbits (6 presumed pregnant/group) once
(b) (4)
daily from GD 7 to 19 at doses of 0 (
), 5, 10, or 20 mg/kg at a dose volume of 0.667 mL/kg via intravenous infusion over a 30minute period. One female in the 10 mg/kg group was euthanized in extremis on GD 13. This
animal had a reduction in body weight when compared to the controls and a decrease in food
intake. Other clinical observations noted in this animal included a decrease in fecal output, soft
or mucoid feces, and brown staining on the tail and hindlimbs. There were no test article-related
deaths or effects on the endpoints evaluated (clinical signs, body weights, body weight gains,
gravid uterine weights, food consumption, toxicokinetic parameters, gross necropsy findings,
intrauterine growth and survival, and fetal morphology). The NOAEL for maternal and embryofetal development was 20 mg/kg. The day 19 AUC0-24h at the NOAEL was 178,000 ng·h/mL.

A 30-Minute IV Infusion Study of the Effect of GS-6207 on Embryo-Fetal
Development in New Zealand White Rabbits (TX-200-2037)

•

Lower body weight gains across all doses correlated to a dose-dependent decrease in food
intake.

measured before necropsy.

Impurity Studies
A Single-Dose Subcutaneous Injection Impurity Qualification Study With GS-6207 in
Wister Han Rats With a 4-Week Observation (Recovery) Period (TX-200-2042; GLP)
In single dose impurity study, GS-6207 (pure), GS-6207-A (with impurities), or GS-6207-B
(b) (4)
(with impurities) formulated in PEG-300/sterile water for injection/
was
administered to male and female Wister Han rats (3/sex/control and 3/sex/dose group) at a dose
of 0 (control) or 100 mg/kg (GS-6207-treated groups) with a dose volume of 1.34 mL/kg via
subcutaneous injection. GS-6207-related changes in serum chemistry detected 5 days postdose
(increases in globulin; decrease in albumin:globulin ratio; and a decrease in albumin [males
only]) suggested inflammation. At the end of the recovery period, these findings were not
detected. Injection site reactions were detected in all GS-6207-treated animals and included
minimal to marked granulomatous inflammation. The inclusion of the impurities did not affect
the injection site reactions detected.

14.1.4. Drug-Drug Interaction Potential—LEN as a
Victim
LEN was evaluated as a substrate of cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes, UDP glucuronosyl
transferase (UGT) enzymes, P-gp, BCRP, organic anion transporter peptide (OATP)1B1, and
OATP1B3 in Studies AD-200-2030, AD-200-2054, AD-200-2033, AD-200-2005, AD-2002025, and AD-200-2027. Very slow metabolism of LEN by CYP3A5 or UGT1A1 was observed
in in vitro phenotyping studies, indicating LEN is a substrate for CYP3A5 and UGT1A1. LEN is
also a substrate for P-gp. LEN is not a substrate for other CYP or UGT isozymes, nor a substrate
for breast-cancer resistance protein (BCRP) or hepatic uptake transporters.

14.1.5. Drug-Drug Interaction Potential—LEN as a
Perpetrator
The potential of LEN as an inhibitor for CYP enzymes, UGT1A1, P-gp (MDR1), BCRP,
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, organic cation transporter (OCT)1, OCT2, multidrug and
toxin extrusion protein (MATE)1, MATE2K, and bile salt export pump was evaluated in Studies
AD-200-2028, AD-200-2050, AD-200-2031, AD-200-2026, AD-200-2035. The results showed
that LEN is a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, mainly through mechanism-based inhibition. The
potential of LEN as an inhibitor was evaluated using static mechanistic models (Report AD-2002055), described in the FDA Guidance for Industry: In Vitro Drug Interaction Studies —
Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug Interactions (January 2020). Based
on the criteria defined in the guidance, LEN is not expected to inhibit CYP1A2, CYP2B6,
CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6 or UGT1A1. LEN is an inhibitor of OATP1B1 or
OATP1B3, but is not likely to inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2, MATE1, or
MATE2K. LEN is expected to increase the concentrations of CYP3A substrates by about fiveand two-fold with LEN oral and SC injection, respectively.
The induction potential of LEN on the aryl hydrocarbon receptor, constitutive androstane
receptor, and pregnane X receptor was evaluated in Study AD-200-2029. In vitro
characterization of LEN for CYP induction potential was conducted using Sandwich Cultured
Transporter-Certified™ hepatocytes in Study AD-200-2049. The data suggested no potential for
activation of genes regulated by the aryl hydrocarbon receptor or constitutive androstane
receptor. LEN activated pregnane X receptor, exceeding two-fold activation at concentrations of
LEN ≥2.5μM. In Study AD-200-2049, CYP3A4 mRNA was unchanged in two of the three
donors; the mRNA fold over control increased more than two-fold in the third donor and reached
a maximum of 23% of the positive control at 3µM LEN (a cytopathic dose). Thus, while this
effect was only seen in one donor it suggests that induction of CYP3A4 and pregnane X
receptor-regulated gene products by LEN cannot be ruled out completely. The potential of LEN
as an inducer was evaluated using static mechanistic models. Based on the FDA in vitro drugdrug interaction (DDI) guidance, LEN would not be classified as an inducer following either SC
or oral administration (Report AD-200-2055).

Table 104. Study GS-US-200-4072
Location
GS-US-200-4072 Final CSR
Title
A Phase 1b Randomized, Double-Blinded, Placebo Controlled, Multi-Cohort Study
of the Safety, Pharmacokinetics, and Antiviral Activity of GS-6207 Administered
Subcutaneously in HIV-1 Infected Subjects
Trial design
This was a randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled, multicenter,
multicohort study of LEN (Part A) and TAF (Part B) monotherapy in people with
HIV. Results from Part B of the study are not discussed here.
In Part A (Cohorts 1-5), participants in each cohort (n=8) were randomized in a
3:1 ratio to receive active LEN (n=6) or placebo (n=2). A single SC dose of LEN or
placebo was administered in the abdomen on Day 1. The LEN doses evaluated in
Cohorts 2 to 5 were determined based on cumulative safety data (to at least
Day 14) and available PK and antiviral activity data from previous cohorts, as well
as available safety data (to at least Day 14) and PK data from Study GS-US-2004070. Doses of LEN 150, 50, 450, 20, and 750 mg were administered in Cohorts
1, 2, 3, 4, and 5, respectively. The formulation of LEN used was LEN 100 mg/mL
suspension, free acid.
All participants initiated once-daily bictegravir/emtricitabine/tenofovir alafenamide
(B/F/TAF, Biktarvy® [BVY]) on Day 10 after completion of all assessments and
continued dosing through Day 225, as a standard-of-care oral combination ART to
mitigate the potential risks of developing resistance to LEN.
PK Sample collection Plasma PK Sampling
time points
Intensive plasma PK sampling occurred relative to study drug dosing time at the
following time points for each study part. Study Part A (Cohorts 1 to 5):
• Day 1: 0 (predose), and up to 225 days postdose.

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Results

Study 4072: LEN Plasma Pharmacokinetic Parameters Following SingleDose Administration of LEN—Part A (LEN PK Analysis Set)

Data are means (%CV), except for Tmax, Tlast, and t1/2, which are medians (Q1, Q3) hours
[median days].

Pharmacokinetics/Pharmacodynamics
Plasma concentrations of LEN were fit to an Emax model to evaluate the
concentration-response relationship between LEN concentration at Day 10 and
the reduction from baseline in HIV-1 RNA at Day 10. Due to the limited amount of
data at low concentration values and reduction in HIV-1 RNA in the placebo
group, the Emax model was not forced through the origin (0,0).
Following single-dose SC administration of LEN, the Emax was predicted to be a
2.13 log10 copies/mL decline in HIV-1 RNA at Day 10. The in vivo 90% effective
concentration (EC90) of LEN on Day 10 demonstrated by the Emax model was
12.6 ng/mL. Based on this analysis, mean LEN concentrations ≥12.6 ng/mL are
predicted to provide near maximal (94%) antiviral activity.

Table 105. Study GS-US-200-4330
Study number
GS-US-200-4330

GS-US-200-4330 Final CSR
Study title
A Phase 1 Open-Label, Parallel-Design, Single-Dose Study to Evaluate the
Pharmacokinetics of GS-6207 in Participants With Normal Renal Function and
Severe Renal Impairment
Description of trial
Inclusion Criteria
design
Male and nonpregnant/nonlactating female subjects, ages 18 to 79 years with
severe renal impairment or normal renal function (creatinine clearance [CLcr]
≥90 mL/min), were eligible. Subjects must have had 12-lead electrocardiogram
(ECG) without clinically significant abnormalities, with either severe renal
impairment or normal renal function (CLcr ≥90 mL/min using the Cockcroft Gault
equation). Subjects with renal impairment must have had a severe renal
impairment classification (15≤ CLcr ≤29 mL/min using the Cockcroft Gault
equation) at screening that was unchanged during the 3 months prior to study
drug dosing. Subjects with severe renal impairment requiring or anticipated to
require dialysis within 90 days of study entry were not eligible.
Treatment
Subjects with renal impairment received a single dose of lenacapavir (LEN)
(1×300 mg tablet) administered orally on Day 1. Dosing in matched healthy
controls with normal renal function began after a matched subject with renal
impairment had completed pharmacokinetic (PK) assessments through Day 4.
Matched healthy controls received a single dose of LEN (1×300 mg tablet).
Duration of dosing was 1 day. The study duration (not including screening) was
approximately 60 days (±1 day) including follow-up.
PK Sampling
Plasma PK Collection
scheme
Intensive PK sampling occurred relative to the morning dose of LEN at the
following time points for each cohort:
Day 1: 0 (predose, ≤5 minutes before dose), 0.5, 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, and
48 hours postdose.

Days 4, 6, 8, 15, 22, 29, 36, 43, and 50: A single anytime PK sample was

Urine PK Sampling
All urine samples were collected predose (void; within a 12-hour period prior to
Day 1 dose), and over the following collection intervals after Day 1: 0 to 12, 12 to
24, 24 to 48, 48 to 72, 72 to 96, 96 to 120, 120 to 144, and 144 to 168 hours
postdose.
The plasma PK parameters of LEN (by renal group) are presented below. Cmax
and AUC were 2.6- and 1.9-fold higher, respectively, in subjects with severe renal
impairment compared to subjects with normal renal function. However, larger
increases in exposures were observed in other clinical studies. In Study 4332, the
Cmax and AUCtau to Day 12 were 1070 ng/mL and 10,641 h·ng/mL, respectively,
more than 7- and 12-fold higher, respectively, than the therapeutic exposure in the
CAPELLA study. Similar results were observed in Study 5709. There are no
safety concerns for both studies. Therefore, the observed exposure elevation in
subjects with severe renal impairment is not clinically significant.

design

Plasma PK Collection
scheme

64, 78, 92, and 120, as applicable. Additional plasma samples were collected for

GS-US-200-4329 Final CSR
A Phase 1 Study to Evaluate the Safety, Tolerability, Pharmacokinetics,
Metabolism and Excretion of IV GS-6207 in Healthy Subjects
Inclusion Criteria (Clinical Pharmacology Related)
Eligible subjects were aged 18 to 55 years, inclusive (male only for Study Part B),
with a creatinine clearance (CLcr) ≥90 mL/min, and with a normal liver function at
screening.
Treatment
Study Part A: On the morning of Day 1, each subject received a single dose of
LEN 10 mg or placebo, administered as a 1-hour IV infusion, without regard to
food.
Study Part B: On the morning of Day 1, each subject received a single dose of
LEN 20 mg, containing a mixture of both unlabeled LEN and [14C]LEN (equivalent
to approximately 200 μCi), administered as a 1-hour IV infusion, without regard to
food.
Plasma PK Collection (Study Part A)
Plasma samples for intensive PK analysis were collected at the following time
points relative to the start time of infusion on Day 1: (predose [≤30 minutes before
dose]), 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, and 48 hours after the start of the infusion. A single
anytime PK sample was collected on Days 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 15, 22, 29, 36, 43, 57,
64, 71, 78, 85, and early termination (if applicable).
Study Part B (Plasma and Urine PK Collection)
Blood samples for plasma PK analysis of unlabeled LEN (and metabolites, if
appropriate) were collected at the following time points relative to the start time of
infusion on Day 1: 0 (predose [≤30 minutes before dose]), 1, 2, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24, and
48 hours after the start of the infusion. A single anytime PK sample was collected
on Days 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 15, 22, 29, 36, 43, 50, and early termination (if applicable).
Subjects who participated in the extended confinement period had a single
anytime PK sample collected on Days 57, 64, 71, 78, 85, 99, 113, 127, and 141,
as applicable.
Mean whole blood-to-plasma concentration ratios of 14C-radioactivity ranged from
0.500 to 0.695 through 336 hours postdose, indicating low association of
radioactivity with red blood cells. Statistical comparisons of dose-normalized
primary LEN PK parameters after single dose of LEN 10 mg and 20 mg in
Cohort 1 and 3, respectively, are summarized in the table below.

Analysis of Plasma Samples for [14C]LEN and [14C]Metabolites
Of the circulating radioactivity, 68.8% was associated with unchanged LEN. No
single metabolite contributed >10% of total radioactivity exposure through
1176 hours postdose. The total radioactivity exposure from the sum of all
identified components in the plasma was approximately 88%.
Analysis of Fecal Samples for [14C]LEN and [14C]Metabolites
Unchanged LEN was the most abundant component recovered in feces and
accounted for a mean abundance of 43.4% of the total fecal radioactivity.
Adjusting for the 75.9% mean cumulative fecal recovery of the administered
radioactive dose, LEN and the LEN-hexose conjugate metabolite (M42, M35)
were the two most abundant components recovered in the feces and accounted
for a mean abundance of 43.4% (32.9% of dose) and 8.7% (6.6% of dose) of the
administered 14C dose, respectively. Each of the other metabolites identified in
feces accounted for <2% of the dose.
Summary of Mass Balance
A mean 75.9% of the dose was recovered in feces and 0.237% was recovered in
urine through the last collection interval, indicating that fecal elimination is the
primary elimination route of LEN and its metabolites. Most of the administered
radioactive dose was recovered in the first 1608 hours postdose (74.7% in feces
and urine combined).

the PM dose.
When ECGs and PK samples were collected at the same time point, the ECG
collection time was adjusted such that it occurred prior to the PK sample collection.

PK/PD Results
The results of the Applicant’s and reviewer’s separate analyses suggest an absence
of significant QTc prolongation at the proposed therapeutic dose. The peak
concentration observed with highest dose studied (i.e., 600 mg twice daily; Day 12 of
study) is expected to offer approximately 8.8-fold margin over the therapeutic
exposures associated with the maximum proposed dose at the steady-state (Phase
2/3 Study 4625 in HTE PLWH). At LEN concentrations approximately 9-fold the
therapeutic concentrations from the recommended dosing regimen, lenacapavir does
not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent.

14.3.1. Applicant’s Population PK Analysis
The population PK (popPK) analysis was based on PK data from seven studies (Table 114): five
phase 1 studies in healthy subjects (4071, 4329, 4333, 4538, and 5709), one phase 2 study in
treatment-naïve patients with HIV (Study 4334), and one phase 2/3 study in HTE patients (Study
4625). In phase 1 studies, LEN was administered as either an oral dose, a 1-hour IV infusion, an
SC injection, or a combination of oral loading and SC injection. In phase 2 and phase 2/3 studies,
LEN was administered as an oral lead-in followed by SC administration.
The analysis dataset comprised 7053 PK observations from 384 subjects, including 198 below
limit of quantification concentrations. The limit of quantification was 0.5 ng/mL for Studies
4071, 4334, 4538, 4625 and 5709, and 0.1 ng/mL for Studies 4329 and 4333. below limit of
quantification samples represented about 2.8% of the PK data, and therefore were excluded from
the analysis.
The summary statistics for the continuous and categorical covariates are presented by study in
Table 115. The analysis population was primarily male (77.6%) white (56.8%) subjects, with a
median (range) age of 34 years (18 to 78 years), weight of 75.9 kg (41.4 to 164 kg), body mass
index (BMI) of 26.1 kg/m2 (14.9 to 51.1 kg/m2), and 54.9% of subjects were patients with HIV.

The prediction-corrected visual predictive checks plots (Figure 36) of concentrations versus time
after last dose showed a reasonable agreement between the observed and simulated data. The
central tendency and variability of the LEN concentrations across analyzed patients populations
and different formulations was adequately captured. The IIV is slightly overpredicted in healthy
volunteers studies only, irrespective of the formulation used (i.e., higher 5th percentiles from
Studies 4329, 4071, 4333, and 4538). However the median and 95% percentiles of the observed
concentration are well captured.

17. Clinical Safety: Additional Information
and Assessment
No additional clinical safety assessments were conducted. Analysis of clinical safety outcomes
by demographic subgroups were specifically not included here because of the small sample sizes
anticipated with Studies 4625 and 4334, which would limit meaningful interpretation of the
analyses.

18. Mechanism of Action/Drug Resistance
Mechanism of Action
Co-Crystal Structure of Lenacapavir Bound to Cross-Linked Capsid Hexamer (Report No.
PC-200-2022)
A 2.0-Angstrom protein X-ray crystal structure was determined for the cross-linked capsid
hexamer in complex with LEN to characterize the interaction of LEN with the HIV-1 capsid.
LEN binds directly at the interface formed by two adjacent capsid monomers, such that up to six
molecules of LEN can bind to each capsid hexamer. LEN forms extensive interactions with the
binding pocket (Figure 39). The central amide and pyridine ring of LEN form hydrogen bonds
with Asn57 within the N-terminal domain of one monomer, whereas the indazole and
sulfonamide groups of LEN make hydrogen bonds with Gln179 and Asn183 within the
C-terminal domain of the neighboring monomer. Thus, LEN binds at the intermolecular interface
between the N-terminal domain of one subunit and the C-terminal domain of the neighboring
subunit within the same capsid hexamer. Additionally, the LEN binding site is shared by capsidbinding host nuclear import factors (Price et al. 2012; Matreyek et al. 2013; Bhattacharya et al.
2014). This observation supports that LEN may compete with nuclear import machinery for
binding to capsid and thereby interfere with nuclear transport.

218
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Figure 39. Co-Crystal Structure of LEN Bound to Cross-Linked HIV-1 CA Hexamer
(b) (4)

Abbreviations: Asn, asparagine; CA, capsid; CTD, C-terminal domain; Gln, glycine; HIV, human immunodeficiency virus; LEN,
lenacapavir; Lys, lysine; NTD, N-terminal domain

LEN Binding to Recombinant Capsid Proteins (Report No. PC-200-2022)
The binding affinities of LEN to wild-type capsid hexamers and hexamers containing the M66I
substitution (identified in resistance selection studies) (Report PC-200-2025) were evaluated by
surface plasmon resonance. Surface plasmon resonance sensorgrams showed dose-dependent and
saturable binding of LEN to cross-linked wild-type capsid hexamer with an equilibrium binding
constant of 1.4nM. LEN binding to cross-linked M66I mutant hexamers was 80-fold weaker,
with an equilibrium binding constant of 110nM.
Effect on Capsid Assembly in a Biochemical Assay (Report No. PC-200-2022)
Capsid protein can spontaneously oligomerize under high ionic strength conditions to form openended, tube-like structures comprised of repeating capsid hexamers. The assembly kinetics of
full-length capsid were measured under high ionic strength condition (2M NaCl) in the presence
and absence of LEN using an assembly assay based on monitoring the change in sample
absorbance at 350 nm. LEN showed a dose-dependent increase in the rate and extent of capsid
assembly with a mean concentration yielding 50% activation of 0.32μM (mean of six
independent determinations).

219
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Additionally, assembly products formed under both low ionic strength (0.5M NaCl) and high
ionic strength (2M NaCl) conditions were visualized by negative staining and transmission
electron microscopy. Under low ionic strength (0.5M NaCl) conditions, full-length capsid did
not undergo self-assembly, but in the presence of 40μM LEN, formed large numbers of capsid
polymers that were shorter and generally more disordered (Figure 40A) relative to the long
capsid tube-like structures universally observed in the absence of the compound in the high ionic
strength buffer, a condition that is permissive for capsid protein assembly in the absence of LEN
(Figure 40A). At a higher NaCl concentration (2M) with LEN, capsid polymers were also shorter
and malformed (disordered and closed) relative to the uniform tubular polymers formed by fulllength capsid in the absence of this compound (Figure 40B). LEN induced the production of
poorly organized multimeric capsid polymers.

Full Length CA Products Assembled in a Biochemical Reaction

Effects on Capsid Morphology (Report PC-200-2023)
The morphology of capsid structures was examined in ultrathin-sectioned virions produced in the
presence and absence of LEN by transmission electron microscopy (Figure 41). The vehicle
220
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
(dimethyl sulfoxide)-treated controls showed the expected conical capsids with an encased dark
signal at the wide end of the cone consistent with negative staining of viral nucleic acids. In
contrast, virions produced in the presence of LEN contained predominantly malformed capsid
cores in which the nucleic acids were either regionally or diffusely confined within the
irregularly shaped capsid core. This altered capsid morphology is consistent with the finding that
LEN accelerates the assembly of recombinant capsid protein into poorly organized capsid
polymers. The morphology of HIV-1 particles produced in the presence of LEN was distinct
from virions produced in the presence of the protease inhibitor atazanavir, which uniformly
contained an immature, discontinuous Gag/Gag-Pol polyprotein lattice. Virions produced in the
presence of LEN or atazanavir were noninfectious when serially diluted well below the EC50
concentration of each compound and used to infect CEM-NKR CCR5+Luc+ indicator cells.

Inhibition of Production of Mature HIV-1 Virions by Capsid Binding
The effects of LEN on virus production and cell viability was assessed in HEK293T cells
following transient transfection with plasmids encoding the vesicular stomatitis virus envelope
and a single-cycle HIV-1 reporter virus. LEN showed a dose-dependent reduction in the amount
of mature wild-type HIV-1 released into cell culture supernatants, with a mean 50% effective
concentration (EC50) value of 0.305nM as determined by p24 (capsid) enzyme-linked
immunosorbent assay (Figure 42). The effect on the production of mature extracellular virions
occurred at concentrations of LEN within the range of its late-stage antiviral EC50 value of
0.44nM, which suggests that this effect may represent the major mechanism by which LEN
mediates its late-stage antiviral activity. The effect of LEN on virus production required the
wild-type capsid domain since this effect was not observed with the M66I capsid mutant (mean
221
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
EC50 >2µM). In addition, the compound effect is virus-specific since LEN showed no
cytotoxicity in HEK293T cells up to the highest concentration tested (10µM) (Figure 42).
Figure 42. Effect of LEN (GS-6207) on the Production of Mature Extracellular WT and the M66I
Capsid Mutant in HEK293T Cells

Source: Report PC-200-2023, page 11.
Abbreviations: LEN, lenacapavir; HEK, human embryonic kidney; HIV, human immunodeficiency virus; WT, wild type

Dose-Dependent Reduction in Intracellular HIV-1 Gag/p24 Levels by a Mechanism That
Requires Capsid Domain Binding
The effect of LEN on HIV-1 Gag polyprotein expression and maturation was assessed by antip24 western blot analyses using cell lysates from HEK293T cells producing infectious virus
(Figure 43). Cells producing wild-type HIV-1 in the presence of 10 to 100nM LEN (23- to 227fold its late-stage EC50 value) showed reduced intracellular levels of capsid (p24) and Gag (p55)
proteins relative to the vehicle-treated (mock) control sample. At lower concentrations of LEN
(0.1 to 1nM), Gag levels remained unaffected by the presence of the compound whereas capsid
levels were reduced relative to the mock control. The Applicant states LEN is unlikely to act by
inhibiting Gag/Gag-Pol polyprotein cleavage since reduced capsid levels were not associated
with a concomitant increase in Gag levels, as would be expected, and because malformed capsid
cores were readily observed in virus particles produced in the presence of LEN. The intracellular
downregulation of these viral proteins is dependent on a specific interaction between LEN and
the capsid domain of mature (p24) and immature (Gag/Gag-Pol) capsid proteins, because these
proteins were not reduced by LEN when using the M66I capsid HIV-1 mutant (Figure 43). These
data suggest that the reduced production of virus particles observed with LEN is likely due to an
increased rate of Gag/p24 intracellular turnover following specific binding of LEN to the capsid
domain of intracellular Gag/Gag-Pol precursor polyproteins by a mechanism that is not yet
determined.

Effect of LEN on the Early Stages of HIV-1 Replication (Report PC-200-2024)
LEN retains full antiviral efficacy when the presence of the compound is selectively restricted to
the target cell infection phase (i.e., the early stage of the replication cycle), with a mean early
stage EC50 value of 0.023nM. The antiviral activity of LEN was assessed in CEM-NKr
CCR5+Luc+ T cells infected with pseudotyped HIV-1 expressing one of four different viral
envelope proteins. These viral envelope glycoproteins were selected because their surface
expression confers either broad host cell tropism (VSVG) or restricted HIV-1 entry into CD4+
cells expressing the C-X-C chemokine receptor (CXCR)4 coreceptor (NL4-3), the C-C
chemokine receptor (CCR)5 coreceptor (JRFL), or other chemokine coreceptor (HT593.1). LEN
displayed similar antiviral activity against all pseudotyped HIV-1 viruses tested with a range of
EC50 values between 0.046 and 0.106nM (Table 122). These data indicate that LEN functions
independently of the viral envelope protein.

223
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Table 122. Effect of Virus Envelope Glycoprotein on LEN Antiviral Activity in MT-2 Cells

Source: Report PC-200-2024, page 12.
Abbreviations: CCR, C-C chemokine receptor; CXCR, C-X-C motif chemokine receptor; EC50, 50% effective dose; HIV, human
immunodeficiency virus; LEN, lenacapavir; SD, standard deviation; VSVG, vesicular stomatitis virus G protein

The BlaM-Vpr reporter assay was used to quantify HIV entry into peripheral blood mononuclear
cells (PBMCs) treated with LEN. This assay uses HIV-1 variant containing copackaged βlactamase enzyme which, when transferred to the target cell’s cytosol following virus-host cell
membrane fusion, can be detected and quantified through enzymatic cleavage of the fluorescent
substrate CCF2. The PBMCs from three donors were independently infected with BlaM/HIV-1
(NL4-3 strain) in the presence of LEN or control compounds, loaded with CCF2 substrate dye,
and CD3+ CD4+ CD8- cells containing virus entry were enumerated by flow cytometry to detect
the cleaved CCF2 dye after 16 hours of incubation. Whereas only a low proportion of blue CD3+
CD4+ CD8- cells were detected in PBMC cultures following a mock-infection (mean 0.6%),
mean infection rates with BlaM/HIV were 17% in the presence of dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO;
vehicle control). As expected, treatment with the CXCR4 small-molecule control inhibitor
AMD3100 (1µM) greatly reduced virus entry to near background levels (mean 0.8%). In
contrast, treatment with 1µM of either LEN or the INSTI dolutegravir did not reduce HIV entry
into PBMCs (mean 18 to 21%). These data indicate that LEN induces a post-entry block in HIV
replication.
A time-of-drug-addition assay was used to determine how long after initiating a synchronized
HIV-1 infection LEN could be added to the culture before a 50% decline in its maximal antiviral
efficacy was observed. MT-2 cells were infected with VSVG-HIV-LUC reporter virus, washed
to remove unabsorbed virus, and then compounds were added to the culture either immediately
postinfection (time 0) or at 4-hour intervals (+4, +8, +12, +16, +20, +24, +28, and +32 hours)
postinfection. Compounds were added at a fixed drug concentration conferring maximal viral
suppression, equivalent to 10- or 100-fold their respective EC50 value. The mean time
postinfection to maintain at least half maximal inhibition (T1/2) for LEN was ~ 15.3 hours. The
T1/2 observed with the non-nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitors (NNRTIs) efavirenz and
nevirapine were 10.2 and 11.3 hours, respectively, whereas the T1/2 for each of two integrase
strand-transfer inhibitors (INSTIs) (raltegravir, DTG) was 16.6 hours. These data indicate that
LEN inhibits HIV infection sometime after reverse transcription but before HIV integration.
The effect of LEN on the accumulation of early HIV-1 replication products in acutely infected
MT-2 cells was assessed by quantitative PCR. Cells were infected with single-cycle VSVG-HIVLUC virus and then treated with DMSO (vehicle control), LEN, efavirenz, dolutegravir, or
atazanavir. Total DNA was harvested 12, 24, and 48 hours postinfection. The amounts of reverse
transcribed strong-stop and second-strand transfer DNA products (early and late reverse
transcription, respectively) and abortive 2-long terminal repeat (2-LTR) circles produced only in
224
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
the nucleus were quantified by quantitative PCR at 12 and 24 hours postinfection, respectively,
whereas the amounts of integration products (Alu-LTR) were quantified by digital-droplet PCR
at 48 hours postinfection. All measured quantities were normalized against the corresponding
CCR5 gene copies in the same sample and expressed as a fold-change value relative to the
DMSO-treated infected control (set to 100%) for each target amplicon measured (Table 123). As
expected, treatment with the late-acting PI atazanavir had no effect on the accumulation of any of
the four replication products analyzed in this single-cycle infection study. In contrast, the NNRTI
efavirenz significantly reduced the accumulation of all four replication products as expected.
Treatment with the INSTI dolutegravir specifically reduced integration products, and thus,
increased integration failures, leading to a three-fold increase in the accumulation of abortive 2LTR circles in the nucleus. Although high concentrations of LEN (244-fold its EC50 value)
produced an HIV replication product profile similar to efavirenz, indicating LEN can inhibit
reverse transcription under conditions of extremely high drug exposure, this was not the case at
lower and still fully suppressive LEN concentrations. For instance, MT-2 cells treated with 0.25
to 2.5nM LEN (6.1- to 61-fold its EC50 value) showed a unique impact on HIV infection product
profile relative to the control inhibitors, with no effect on the accumulation of reverse
transcription products, but profoundly reduced levels of integration products and abortive 2-LTR
circles. Thus, LEN primarily inhibits HIV replication at a step after reverse transcription, but
before integration. Two-LTR circles did not accumulate in the presence of LEN as observed with
dolutegravir, but instead were reduced, suggesting that the compound likely prevents integration
by inhibiting the translocation of viral preintegration complexes into the nucleus.

Reverse Transcription, HIV Integration, and Integration Failure

In summary, there is evidence that LEN acts through mechanisms both at early and late stages of
HIV-1 replication and is a multistage inhibitor (Figure 44). LEN binds directly binds to the
interface between capsid protein subunits. Lenacapavir inhibits HIV-1 replication by interfering
with multiple essential steps of the viral lifecycle, including capsid-mediated nuclear uptake of
HIV-1 proviral DNA (by blocking nuclear import proteins binding to capsid), virus assembly and
release (by interfering with Gag/Gag-Pol functioning, reducing production of capsid protein
subunits), and capsid core formation (by disrupting the rate of capsid subunit association, leading
to malformed capsids).
Figure 44. LEN Inhibition of Early and Late Stages of HIV-1 Replication

Source: Report PC-200-2021, page 10.
Abbreviations: HIV, human immunodeficiency virus; LEN, lenacapavir

Antiviral Activity
The antiviral activity of a novel HIV-1 capsid inhibitor LEN was evaluated using a 5-day
cytopathic assay in the MT-4 T-lymphoblastoid cell line acutely infected with HIV-1IIIB (Report
PC-200-2018). In parallel, the cytotoxicity of LEN was assessed in corresponding uninfected
MT-4 cells. LEN had an EC50 value of 0.19nM in MT-4 cells and a selectivity index (50%
cytotoxic concentration [CC50]/EC50) of 140,000. In addition, the EC95 value of LEN in MT-4
cells was calculated as 0.23nM from the EC50 value and Hill slope values that were accurately
determined from a 40-point dose response curve. Taking into account the human serum shift
determined by equilibrium dialysis (AD-200-2020), LEN exhibited a plasma protein bindingadjusted EC95 (paEC95) value of 4nM.
226
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
The antiviral activity of LEN was evaluated in acutely infected human CD4+ T-lymphocytes and
monocyte-derived macrophages from three independent donors using 7- and 12-day virus
production assays, respectively (Report PC-200-2019). In parallel, the cytotoxicity of LEN was
determined in these cells. In CD4+ T lymphocytes, LEN had a mean EC50 and CC50 values of
0.06 nM and >50μM, respectively, with a selectivity index of >833,330. LEN had mean EC50
and CC50 values of 0.03nM and >50µM, respectively, in monocyte-derived macrophages with a
selectivity index of >1,670,000. In comparison, the EC50 values of LEN in primary CD4+ T cells
and macrophages were lower compared to control INSTI dolutegravir and PI atazanavir.
When LEN was tested against HIV-1NL4-3 in CEM-NKR CCR5+Luc+ T-lymphoblastoid cells
infected with a wide range of multiplicities of infection (MOIs; 0.01 to 1.25), there was antiviral
activity at all tested MOIs (Report PC-200-2031). EC50 values increased as MOI increased, with
EC50 values ranging from 0.028 to 0.455nM (16-fold increase) across the 125-fold range.
Antiviral Activity Against Clinical Isolates
The antiretroviral activity of LEN was tested against 23 clinical isolates of HIV-1 and two
isolates of HIV-2 in freshly isolated human PBMCs (Report PC-200-2020). LEN showed
antiviral activity against all tested HIV-1 clinical isolates representing all major subtypes with
mean and median EC50 values of 0.05 and 0.04nM, respectively, and a range of EC50 values
between 0.02 and 0.16nM (Table 124). The range of means for subtype B isolates was 0.03 to
0.08nM and median was 0.04nM. LEN had lower activity against the two tested isolates of HIV2 with EC50 values of 0.57 and 1.02nM, which is 4- to 50-fold.

In a separate study testing the activity of LEN against 40 clinical HIV-1 isolates of diverse

system and assay endpoint (PBMCs infected by primary isolates with RT endpoint) from the one
utilized in this study. Thus, the Applicant states that direct comparisons of antiviral activity
values cannot be made between the studies. However, in both studies, LEN demonstrated
antiviral activity within a narrow range of EC50 values across the HIV-1 subtypes tested.

Activity of LEN Against HIV Capsid Protein Polymorphisms (Report PC-200-2032)
A publicly available source of 10,512 capsid population sequences representing eight major
HIV-1 subtypes was used to evaluate the conservation of the capsid protein and the LEN binding
site (Table 126). Capsid consensus sequences across the eight subtypes were found to have
>91% sequence identity across the whole protein sequence. Of the 24 amino acid residues within
the LEN binding site defined by x-ray crystallography, 20 residues (83%) had a sequence
conservation between 94% and 100% across all 8 subtypes, including all 7 capsid amino acid
positions associated with cell culture resistance to LEN (L56 [100%], M66 [100%], Q67 [99%
for CRF01_AE, 100% for other subtypes], K70 [100%], N74 [100%], A105 [100%], and T107
[94-99%]). The CRF01_AE subtype contained a 1% prevalence of the Q67H substitution that
confers ~6-fold resistance to LEN. None of the T107 substitutions identified was the T107N
variant that is associated with reduced susceptibility to LEN. The T107N substitution conferring
4-fold resistance to LEN was identified in a single patient and was the only established LEN
resistance-associated substitution detected in capsid by deep sequencing among the analyzed
patient isolates.
The remaining four binding site residues (S41, T54, Y169, and Q179) were mutated in >5% of
patient isolates (n=104) and three of them (S41, T54, and Y169) were variable in >25% of
patient isolates in at least one major HIV-1 subtype. The impact of these identified substitutions
on the susceptibility of isolates to LEN is assessed below.
Collectively, these data indicate a high degree of sequence conservation within the LEN binding
site in HIV-1 capsid, which suggests a low-risk of capsid binding site polymorphisms having an
impact on LEN antiviral efficacy.

The antiviral activity of LEN was measured against a panel of 36 site-directed HIV-1NL4.3 capsid
mutants in MT-4 T-cells (Table 127). These capsid variants were comprised predominantly of
LEN binding site polymorphisms (n=30) previously identified by population-level and higher

resolution next generation sequence analyses above. The 30 capsid polymorphisms spanned eight

as defined by x-ray cocrystallography, with asparagine 57 and, to a lesser extent, asparagine 183

Ninety-three percent of the capsid polymorphisms (28/30) remained fully susceptible to LEN

selected for in cell culture with LEN, showed reduced infectivity in MT-4 cells (20% and 30% of
wild-type, respectively) and were detected in HIV-1 at only 1% prevalence exclusively in either
subtype C (Q50E) or subtype D (N57H) isolates.

H87P, L111I, V165I, and L172I) previously identified by cell culture selection with the Pfizer
capsid inhibitor PF74 or its close analogue PF-3759857. These capsid inhibitors bind a site on
capsid that partially overlaps with that of LEN. LEN retained full activity against five of the
PF74-related resistance substitutions with susceptibility ranging from 1.1- to 1.6-fold. One
substitution (V165I) could not be evaluated for potential LEN resistance due to a significant loss
of HIV-1 infectivity (0.2% of wild-type).

Inhibition of Early and Late HIV-1 Replication (Report PC-200-2021)
The antiviral activity of LEN was evaluated in the MT-2 T-lymphoblastoid cell line using a
single-cycle reporter assay. These assays were set up such that the compound was present
selectively at the early stage, late stage, or during the full course of a single round of HIV
infection. LEN had a mean EC50 value of 0.025nM against a single round infection of MT-2 cells
with wild-type HIV-1 reporter virus (Table 128). In contrast, LEN had decreased antiviral
activity against the capsid M66I resistant HIV-1 variant during a single round infection with an
EC50 value >50nM. LEN retained full antiviral efficacy when the presence of the compound was
selectively restricted to the target cell infection phase with a mean early stage EC50 value of
0.023nM, indicating LEN predominately targets at least one essential capsid-mediated step
involved in the early stages of HIV replication. However, even when restricted to the late stage
in virus producer cells, LEN had a mean late stage EC50 value of 0.439nM with no cytotoxicity
in the HEK293T producer cells up to the highest concentration tested (CC50 value >50nM). Thus,
LEN also targets with ~18-fold reduced activity late-stage capsid-mediated HIV replication
functions. As controls, the INSTI raltegravir and the PI atazanavir showed a much more
restricted antiviral effect specific only to the early and late stages of HIV replication,
respectively. Finally, LEN was capable of irreversibly inactivating fully infectious cell-free
HIV-1 with a mean EC50 value of 25nM. Collectively, these data show that LEN has antiviral
activity against both early- and late-stage essential processes in the HIV-1 replication cycle.

Antiviral Activity of LEN Against Non-HIV Viruses
LEN was tested against hepatitis B virus (HBV), hepatitis C virus, human rhinovirus, and
respiratory syncytial virus in cell-based assays. LEN did not display selectivity in cell culture
antiviral activity against HCV or HRV up to the highest concentration tested (44 to 50μM)
(Table 129). LEN did demonstrate antiviral activity against HBV and respiratory syncytial virus
at low micromolar concentrations, with EC50 values and corresponding CC50/EC50 ratios of
1.5µM and >34, and 8.2µM and >6.1, respectively. These data indicate that LEN is
approximately 25,000-fold more active against HIV as compared to HBV. However, a proper
clinically active therapy against HBV should be used in HIV/HBV coinfected patients to
maintain the HBV suppression.

Cytotoxicity
The cytotoxicity of LEN was assessed in parallel uninfected MT-4 cultures. LEN showed low
cytotoxicity in MT-4 cells with CC50 values of 26,600nM, yielding a selectivity index of 140,000
(Report PC-200-2018).
LEN showed mean CC50 values of >50µM in both human CD4+ T-lymphocytes and monocytederived macrophages giving selectivity indices of >833,330 (Report PC-200-2019). LEN
cytotoxicity was measured in normal resting and activated human PBMCs, demonstrating a lack
of cytotoxicity, with CC50 values of >44 and >50μM, respectively.
LEN was tested for cytotoxicity in a panel of four human cell lines nonpermissive to HIV-1
infection, including two hepatoma cell lines (Huh7 and HepG2), a prostate carcinoma cell line
(PC-3) and a normal embryonic lung fibroblast line (MRC-5) (Report PC-200-2028). The
HepG2 and PC-3 cells used in this study were galactose-adapted for enhanced sensitivity to
mitochondria toxicity (Marroquin et al. 2007). LEN did not display any significant cytotoxicity
against the four tested cell lines. CC50 values for LEN in the tested cell types were >44μM.
Relative to its EC50 value for anti-HIV-1 activity in primary human CD4+ T-lymphocytes (EC50
0.06nM), a selectivity index of >730,000 was achieved for LEN in all cell types tested.
Puromycin was used as a positive control and showed CC50 values of 0.3 to 1.0μM.
In addition to these cell lines, the cytotoxicity of LEN was tested in primary human hepatocytes
from three independent donors. LEN yielded CC50 values >50μM in primary hepatocytes
following a 5-day incubation with the tested compounds, while CC50 values for the positive
control puromycin ranged from 0.93 to 2.10μM in these cells.

Antiviral Combination Activity in Cell Culture
The anti-HIV-1 activity of LEN was examined in combination with representative inhibitors
from four major classes of antiretrovirals comprising the INSTI dolutegravir, the NNRTI
efavirenz, the nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor (NRTI) tenofovir alafenamide, and the
HIV protease inhibitor (PI) darunavir. Pair-wise combinations of LEN with dolutegravir,
efavirenz, tenofovir alafenamide, or darunavir showed synergistic anti-HIV activity when tested
using HIV-1IIIB-infected MT-2 cells (Table 130). No antagonistic antiviral interaction was
observed between LEN and any of the clinically approved anti-HIV drugs tested.

Resistance Selection
Selection for HIV-1 variants resistant to LEN was performed in cell culture using two
approaches (Report PC-200-2025). In the first approach, resistance selection using an increasing
concentration method was performed with LEN in MT-2 cells infected with clonal HIV-1HXB2D.
Parallel resistance selections were performed in the presence of efavirenz and elvitegravir as
comparative controls and in the absence of drug. The kinetics of resistance emergence was
similar among all three compounds early in the selection, but then LEN progressed more slowly
relative to efavirenz and elvitegravir as drug concentrations were increased (Figure 45).

Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Figure 45. Dose Escalation Resistance Selections With LEN, Efavirenz (EFV), and Elvitegravir
(EVG) in MT-2 Cells

Source: Report PC-200-2025, page 13.
Abbreviations: CPE, cytopathic effect; EC50, 50% effective concentration; LEN, lenacapavir

At lower LEN drug pressure (8- to 16-fold the EC50), LEN selected for virus encoding the capsid
substitution N74D (Table 131). At higher drug concentrations, LEN selected for virus encoding
the dual capsid mutant N74D+T107N (passage 6), which was immediately replaced in the next
passage by another dual capsid variant Q67H+N74D, which persisted to the end of selection
after 103 days in culture at LEN concentrations 512-fold the EC50. These substitutions are
located in the LEN binding site defined by X-ray crystallography. Clonal sequencing results
confirmed that the Q67H and T107N substitutions were each individually linked to the N74D
substitution on the same viral genome. Additional sequencing of the matrix and SP1 regions
adjacent to the capsid gene, as well as the viral protease, reverse transcriptase, and integrase
coding regions, showed no additional genotypic changes relative to the HXB2D parental
sequence.

The passage 4 (P4) virus selected with 0.56nM LEN (8× EC50) and encoding the capsid N74D
substitution showed 5.5-fold reduction in susceptibility to LEN with no change in susceptibility
to EFV, dolutegravir, EVG, or atazanavir. Passage (P) 6 virus grown in the presence of 2.24nM
LEN (32× EC50) and encoding the capsid N74D+T107N variant had an EC50 1.79nM at P7
versus EC50 1.42nM at P4 and showed no cross resistance. Passage 7 virus selected in the
presence of 4.48nM LEN (64× EC50) and encoding the capsid N74D+Q67H variant showed 43fold resistance to LEN. A peak LEN resistance of >150-fold was observed with P9 and P10 virus
isolates.
In the second approach, resistance selection was conducted in MT-2 cells infected with HIV1HXB2D using a 10-fold higher MOI and several fixed concentrations of LEN (0.92, 1.9, 3.7, 5.5,
and 9.2nM) over a 32-day selection. These test concentrations correspond to tissue culture
equivalent (IQs of 4, 8, 16, 24, and 40, respectively, with an IQ of 1 defined as a concentration
corresponding to the EC95 for LEN (0.23nM). The rate and frequency of viral breakthrough for
fixed LEN concentrations was compared to that of emtricitabine, efavirenz, rilpivirine, and
atazanavir, with each used at a fixed drug concentration equal to their respective tissue culture
equivalent (i.e. adjusted for protein binding) of the clinical plasma trough concentration (Cmin).
At a fixed concentration of 0.92nM LEN (IQ of 4), a replicating virus emerged in three of eight
independent infected samples tested and each isolate encoded the capsid N74D substitution
identified at low LEN concentrations. No breakthrough virus emerged at any of the higher
concentrations of LEN tested here. In comparison, parallel selections with emtricitabine and
rilpivirine resulted in rapid emergence of the M184I/V variants in seven of eight samples and a
breakthrough of the Y181I RT variant in one of eight infected samples tested, respectively, while
selections with efavirenz, atazanavir and dolutegravir each did not lead to any breakthrough of
drug-resistant HIV-1 variants.
Viral breakthrough selections were also conducted over a period of 35 days using a similar set of
LEN and control test concentrations in human PBMCs infected separately with six different
patient-derived isolates of HIV-1. Four weeks into these selections, an aliquot (1/10th) of the
cell-free culture supernatants was transferred onto fresh target cells and cultured for another

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
7 days to enrich for HIV-1 breakthrough variants that retained significant infectivity and
replication capacity in the presence of each tested drug. Day 35 cell-free culture supernatants
were harvested and analyzed for the presence of breakthrough virus by measuring the presence
of virus by p24 enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay and virus infectivity on CEM-NKr
CCR5+Luc+ indicator cells.
Among the six analyzed HIV-1 isolates, dolutegravir was the only compound to completely
suppress viral breakthrough. Emtricitabine, rilpivirine and efavirenz each showed some
frequency of viral breakthrough in PBMCs against the six tested isolates, with clinically relevant
mutant HIV-1 variants emerging. At an IQ of 4, LEN showed a breakthrough better than
emtricitabine and comparable to rilpivirine. Within its projected human plasma exposure range
(IQ 4 to 8), LEN breakthrough viruses encoded the capsid substitutions L56I, or Q67H±N74S,
N74D, or T107N. At IQ 16 and IQ 24 equivalent LEN concentrations, N74D and Q67H single
capsid substitutions were less frequently observed and instead, the emergent viruses encoded
capsid substitutions L56I, M66I, K70N or M66I+Q67H.
Assessment of Site-Directed Resistance Mutants
The antiviral activity of LEN was tested against 11 site-directed HIV-1 mutants encoding the
major p24 domain substitutions identified in the drug selection studies including both single and
double capsid mutants (L56I, M66I, Q67H, K70N, N74D, T107N, M66I/Q67H, Q67H/N74D,
Q67H/N74S, Q67H/T107N, and N74D/T107N) (Report PC-200-2026). Antiviral activity was
tested using both single-cycle and multi-round infection assays performed in the MT-2 and
CEM-NKR CCR5+Luc+ T cell lines, respectively. In the single-cycle infection assay, mutant
viruses, Q67H and T107N, conferred 4- to 6.3-fold decreased susceptibility to LEN relative to
the wild-type virus; K70N, N74D and Q67H/N74S conferred 22- to 32-fold decreased LEN
susceptibility; and L56I and M66I, as well as each of the remaining four double mutant viruses,
all conferred 58- to >3,226-fold decreased LEN susceptibility (Table 132). LEN showed 8.8-fold
and 21-fold reduced activity, respectively, against the Q67Y and A105E substitutions previously
identified upon drug selection with structurally similar analogs of LEN but not with LEN itself.
In addition to these drug resistant variants, HIV-1 containing the capsid P90A site-directed
substitution conferring loss of host cyclophilin A binding to capsid was also studied. This P90A
variant remained fully susceptible to inhibition by LEN in MT-2 cells indicating LEN inhibits
HIV replication independent of cyclophilin A binding to capsid. The replication kinetics of the
major drug resistant capsid variants was investigated in parallel with wild-type virus over a
period of 19 days in primary human CD4+ T cells and in monocyte-derived macrophages. With
the exception of the Q67H substitution, each capsid mutant that was tested displayed severely
diminished replication capacity relative to the wild-type virus in both cell types. Moreover, the
replicative fitness of these mutants was not significantly improved in the presence of LEN in
either cell type.

Phenotypic resistance to the mutant variants was similar between the single-cycle and multiround replication assays except the magnitude of fold-resistance to LEN appeared to be ~3-fold

The effect of the major LEN-resistant substitutions on HIV-1 replication capacity was
investigated in parallel with WT HIV-1 over a period of 19 days in primary human CD4+ T cells
and in monocyte-derived macrophages. The Q67H variant displayed 87 to 100% replicative
capacity in CD4+ T lymphocytes and ~60% in monocyte-derived macrophages through 19 days.
The L56I mutant had low replication fitness (3 to 14%) in T lymphocytes but 44 to 57% in
macrophages. The other capsid mutants tested displayed diminished replication capacity in one
or both of the primary target cells tested, with the N74D and Q67H/N74D showing the most
diminished replication fitness (<5%). In general, these mutants were less fit in primary CD4+ T
cells (0.03 to 28%) than in monocyte-derived macrophages (1.9 to 72%) over the 19 days in
culture. The degree of replicative rescue observed in the presence of the LEN compound in both
cell types was marginal for all tested mutants.

Antiviral Activity Against PI-Resistant Clinical Isolates
The antiretroviral activity of the novel HIV capsid inhibitor lenacapavir (LEN; GS-6207) was
tested against 40 clinical HIV-1 isolates of diverse subtypes including isolates harboring HIV PI
resistance substitutions (Report PC-200-2041). LEN EC50 values against tested wild-type isolates
ranged from 0.15 to 0.36nM, with a geometric mean of 0.24nM. In comparison, the geometric

mean EC50 values for the HIV PIs atazanavir, darunavir, and lopinavir against the same tested
isolates were 7.9nM (range 2.47 to 15), 0.95nM (range 0.21 to 2.7nM), and 11nM (range 2.60 to
35nM), respectively. Additionally, three PI-resistant isolates were analyzed (Isolate 20-141494:

PIs atazanavir, darunavir, and LPV exhibited 3.4- to 16.8-fold reduced susceptibility for viruses
containing PI resistance substitutions relative to wild-type, as expected.
Antiviral Activity Against Treatment-Experienced and -Naïve Patient Isolates
The antiviral activity of LEN was tested against a panel of treatment-experienced and treatmentnaïve HIV-1 patient isolates that contained naturally occurring gag polymorphisms. Fifty-one
patient-derived isolates comprising 36 treatment-experienced (TE) and 15 treatment-naïve (TN)
were cloned into pXXLAI, and their drug susceptibilities to capsid inhibitor (LEN), protease
inhibitors (atazanavir, darunavir), and investigational protease cleavage site inhibitors (bevirimat,
GSK-3532795) were measured in the multicycle HIV-1 assay in MT-2 cells.
The cloned Gag and protease fragment sequence of the isolates were assessed for the presence of
resistance-associated substitutions of PIs, capsid inhibitors (CAI), and protease cleavage site
inhibitors (Report PC-200-2036). None of the TE or TN isolates contained CAI resistance
substitutions. A majority of the TE isolates (94%) harbored primary PI resistance substitutions
while none were found in the TN isolates. Most TE (81%) and TN (93%) isolates contained
naturally occurring gag polymorphisms associated with protease cleavage site inhibitor
resistance (V362I, Q369H, V370A/M/I, T371Q/Del/S). In both TN and TE isolates, LEN
exhibited average EC50 values of 88 and 89pM, respectively, with minimal variability across all
isolates. In contrast, the investigational protease cleavage site inhibitors bevirimat and GSK3532795, showed 40- to 94-fold reduced activity in both TN and TE isolates, reflecting the
occurrence of naturally existing gag polymorphisms associated with resistance to the cleavage
site inhibitor class (Table 134). Resistance to the PIs atazanavir and darunavir was higher in TE
isolates as expected in the presence of PI resistance substitutions, with mean fold-change EC50
above wild-type of 21 and 34, respectively, compared to fold-change of 1.0 for both drugs in TN
isolates (Table 134). The NRTI control compound showed wild-type susceptibility across all
isolates, reflective of the wild-type reverse transcriptase sequence.
LEN displayed wild-type-WT-like activity against the panel of patient isolates, regardless of the
level of previous exposure to antiretroviral therapy in these patients. The antiviral activity of
LEN was not affected by the presence of naturally occurring Gag polymorphisms and prior
treatment experience with PI resistance did not alter the antiviral activity of LEN.

Abbreviations: ATV, atazanavir; BVM, bevirimat; DRV, darunavir; EC50, 50% effective concentration; HIV, human immunodeficiency
virus; TAF, tenofovir alafenamide

Of the 36 TE patient isolates, 24 had Gag cleavage-site substitutions (V362I, Q369H,
V370A/M/I, T371Q/Del/S). LEN displayed an average EC50 value of 89pM with minimal
variability (range 0.4 to 1.8pM) regardless of the presence or absence of Gag cleavage site
substitutions (Report PC-200-2037).
Antiviral Activity of LEN Against HIV Gag Cleavage Site Polymorphisms
The antiviral activity of LEN was tested against a panel of site-directed mutant viruses (n=19)
and HIV-1 isolates from TE people living with HIV (PLWH) (TE; n=24) containing Gag
cleavage site polymorphism substitutions with or without protease inhibitor resistance (Report
PC-200-2037). Substitutions at the capsid/SP1 cleavage site (L363F/M, A364V) have been
associated with resistance to investigational protease cleavage site inhibitors, such as bevirimat.
Substitutions at the NC/SP2 (Q430R, A431V, K436E/S, I437T/V) and SP2/p6 (L449H/V/F,
P453L) cleavage sites are associated with resistance to PIs.
Drug susceptibilities of the capsid inhibitor (LEN), protease cleavage site inhibitors (bevirimat,
GSK-3532795), and PIs (atazanavir, darunavir) were assessed in the multicycle HIV-1 assay in
MT-2 cells. LEN showed wild-type activity with an average EC50 value of 102pM across all 19
mutants, with fold-changes from wild-type ranging from 0.7 to 1.9. Reduced susceptibility (>5fold) to bevirimat and GSK-3532795, was seen with substitutions at positions 363 and 364, as
expected.
Susceptibility of LEN Site-Directed Mutants to PIs and Protease Cleavage Site Inhibitors
The antiviral activity of LEN, PIs, and protease cleavage site inhibitors was tested in a
multicycle HIV-1 antiviral assay in MT-2 cells against a panel of twelve site-directed mutants
(SDM) viruses containing LEN resistance substitutions selected in cell culture resistance
experiments (Table 135). The antiviral activity of LEN, PIs, and protease cleavage site inhibitors
was tested in a multicycle HIV-1 antiviral assay in MT-2 cells against a panel of twelve sitedirected mutants viruses containing LEN resistance substitutions selected in cell culture
resistance experiments (Table 135). Data were obtained for only seven of twelve site-directed
mutants, and showed that relative susceptibility to LEN ranged from 4.1- to 306-fold change
(mean 52.3-fold) in these mutants compared to the wild type virus in the multicycle assay, which
correlated well with LEN phenotypic data obtained for these mutants in a single-cycle assay.
244
Integrated Review Template, version 2.0 (04/23/2020)
Reference ID: 4944206

NDA 215973 lenacapavir injection, for subcutaneous use
NDA 215974 lenacapavir tablets, for oral use
Drug susceptibility for the remaining five site-directed mutants could not be evaluated, as the
mutants did not show adequate infectivity in this multicycle HIV-1 antiviral assay, consistent
with the reduced replication capacity previously observed for these mutants in single cycle HIV1 antiviral experiments. Susceptibility of site-directed mutants to the PIs atazanavir, darunavir,
nelfinavir, and saquinavir was similar to the wild-type control (mean FC of 1.2, 1.2, 1.0, and 1.2,
respectively), indicating a lack of cross-resistance to PIs in these LEN-resistant mutants.
Susceptibility of site-directed mutants to the protease cleavage site inhibitors was similar to that
of the wild-type virus, indicating the absence of cross-resistance.

Exploratory Analysis of Other Capsid Substitutions
The subjects in Cohort 1 of Study 4625 were analyzed for presence at screening and postbaseline
of LEN capsid binding site variants including polymorphisms (S41, Q50, T54, N57, T107, Y169,
L172, and N183) and six resistance-associated substitutions (S41A, K70R, H87P, L111I, V165I,
and L172I) previously identified by cell culture selection with the Pfizer capsid inhibitor PF74 or
its close analogue PF-3759857 (Table 141). No subjects in Cohort 1 had substitutions at N57,
K70, L111, V165, Y169, or Q179 at screening.
