Of the 10 subjects with substitution S41T or A at screening in the phase 1A and 1B cohorts
of the trial, 6 (60%) were virologic failures. Phenotypic data for a site-directed mutant with
S41T showed a 1.5-fold change (Table 141).
Only one subject had the Q50A/S substitution at screening and this subject in the placebo
cohort was a virologic failure at Week 26. Site-directed mutant variant Q50E conferred
low-level (3-fold) decreased susceptibility to LEN.
Two subjects had T54M at screening, one in the placebo cohort was a success and the other
in the LEN cohort was a virologic failure at Week 4. Phenotypic data for site-directed
mutant with T54M showed a 1.3-fold change (Table 141).
Six subjects had H87Q, 1 of 3 in placebo arm was a virologic failure and 1 of 3 in the LEN
cohort was a virologic failure. Phenotypic data for a site-directed mutant with H87P
showed a 1.1-fold change (Table 141).
Four subjects had T107S at screening, (2 placebo and 2 LEN) and only one in the placebo
cohort was a virologic failure. Phenotypic data for site-directed mutant with T107S showed
a 1.3-fold change (Table 141).

20. Other Drug Development Considerations:

21. Data Integrity-Related Consults (Office of
Scientific Investigations, Other Inspections)
Routine PDUFA inspections were completed by the Office of Scientific Investigations. The data
from Studies 4625 and 4334 submitted to the Agency in support of NDAs 215973 and 215974,
appear reliable based on available information. Please also refer to Section II.10 for a list of
clinical sites.
Clinical site inspections were conducted for two clinical investigators, Drs. DeJesus and Ruane.
The inspections covered one clinical trial, Protocol GS-US-200-4625. Overall, the study appears
to have been conducted adequately, and the data generated by these two sites appear acceptable
in support of the respective indication.
During the inspections, the primary efficacy endpoint source data (i.e., the HIV-1 RNA test
results at baseline [Day 1] and at the end of the functional monotherapy period [Day 15]) were
verified against the Applicant’s data line listings for all subjects randomized at the two sites
selected for inspection. No discrepancies were noted.
Important enrollment and other protocol deviations were noted for three of the five subjects
enrolled at Dr. DeJesus’ site (Site 00698). These protocol deviations are not likely to have an
impact on the overall study results.

Commitments

Pediatric Research Equity Act PMR-1: Conduct a study to evaluate the pharmacokinetics,
safety, and antiviral activity (efficacy) of lenacapavir in treatment-experienced children
living with HIV-1 infection who are less than 18 years of age and weighing at least 35 kg.
The safety and antiviral activity of lenacapavir in pediatric subjects must be evaluated for a
minimum of 24 weeks.
Injection site reactions PMR-2: Submit final study reports from Studies 4625 and 4334.
Assessments of adverse events related to injection site reactions must be followed until
resolution or until study completion.
Carcinogenicity PMR-3: Submit final study report from a 2-year rat carcinogenicity study.
Carcinogenicity PMR-4: Submit final study report from the 13-week mouse
carcinogenicity study.

PMRs are not applicable for these applications, however, because the recommended approval
action is a complete response.

Reference ID: 4944206

DDD Consult #2116

can be divided into two groups according to their mechanism of action: irritative reactions
(immediate) and allergic reactions (immediate or delayed) to the excipient or to the drug itself. 1
Allergic or hypersensitivity reactions are induced by IgE, IgG/IgM, complement or T cells and can
be categorized into Types I-IV.
There was no standardized definition of nodule or induration provided to Investigators for the
pivotal clinical trial, GS-US-200-4625 or for the supportive trial, GS-US-200-4334.
The Applicant, however, has included the following definitions of induration and nodule in
Injection site manuals for the Phase 1 trials: GS-US-4070, GS-US-200-4538. (IR response
received 10/26/2021, pp. 41/65 and 49/65)
Induration: (Palpable thickening, firmness, or hardening of soft tissue). Usually well demarcated
with palpable borders, can be visible (raised or sunken compared to surrounding skin), is often
‘woody’ to touch and has a flat shape (versus the rounder shape of a nodule); it can best be
described by palpation.2
Nodule: Discrete or well-demarcated soft tissue mass or lump that is firm.3
Specifically, regarding injection site nodules:
In the literature injection site nodules have been reported with a number of drug products.
One class of drug products includes glucagon-like peptide 1 (GLP-1) receptor agonists used for
treatment of patients with type 2 diabetes. General injection-site reactions (e.g., erythema, pain, or
rash) have been reported with all of the commercially available GLP-1 receptor agonists (i.e.,
exenatide, lixisenatide, liraglutide, dulaglutide, and semaglutide).4
Though not common, a number of cases of injection site nodules have been reported with use of
exenatide extended-release. Jones et al (2015) reviewed FAERS reports of injection site nodules
associated with use of exenatide-extended release and reviewed published cases. They found that
exenatide extended-release–associated injection-site reactions may induce large nodule formation
and, in isolated cases, may require hospitalization, surgical excision, or some other medical

2 Kohl KS, Walop W, Gidudu J, Ball L, Halperin S, Hammer SJ, Heath P, Hennig R, Rothstein E, Schuind A,

8. PMID: 17553602.
3 Rothstein E, Kohl KS, Ball L, Halperin SA, Halsey N, Hammer SJ, Heath PT, Hennig R, Kleppinger C, Labadie J,
Varricchio F, Vermeer P, Walop W; Brighton Collaboration Local Reaction Working Group. Nodule at injection site
as an adverse event following immunization: case definition and guidelines for data collection, analysis, and
presentation. Vaccine. 2004 Jan 26;22(5-6):575-85. doi: 10.1016/j.vaccine.2003.09.005. PMID: 14741147.
4 Hearn EB, Sherman JJ. Injection-Site Nodules Associated With Once-Weekly Subcutaneous Administration of

PMC7887535.

4

Reference ID: 4911738

DDD Consult #2116

intervention to mitigate. 1 Riswald and Flynn (2018)2 reported the case of a 60-year-old man who
presented with 6 large, persistent nodules of the abdomen and left thigh, occurring after each of his
exenatide extended-release weekly injections. A nodule that was treated with serial intralesional
triamcinolone injections showed significant improvement obviating the need for multiple surgical
procedures.
The specific mechanism by which exenatide extended-release can cause serious injection-site
nodules is not entirely clear. The clinical description of some cases is consistent with a
hypersensitivity reaction (e.g., local induration, warmth, and pruritus). Available histopathology
data reveal the presence of eosinophils and granuloma in these sites, and therefore hypersensitivity
to the formulation may be one plausible mechanism.3
Hearn and Sherman (2021)4 report a case of injection-site nodules associated with semaglutide
injected subcutaneously once-weekly. The patient did not experience nodules until reaching the
appropriately titrated maximum dose 6 weeks after therapy initiation. The authors state that this
delay in adverse reaction supports the hypotheses of antibody or eosinophilic response. Such
responses occur most commonly after multiple exposures over time. The nodules were reported to
be quarter-sized, hard, erythematous, pruritic, and raised, appearing within minutes of injection
and typically disappearing within 2 to 4 days. The case report did not include histopathology.
Ko et al (2020)5 report a case of eosinophilic panniculitis by histopathology in a patient developing
a nodule on the thigh over the period of a week after exenatide extended-release injection. The
nodule was described as “tender.”
Subcutaneous nodules at the injection site may occur following use of a wide range of vaccines.
Miliauskas and Mukherjee (1993)6 reported on four patients having a single subcutaneous nodule at
the site of a previous vaccine injection. Three nodules occurred after injection of diphtheria,
tetanus. and pertussis vaccination and one after tetanus toxoid vaccination. Presentation was with a
mass 4-22 months after vaccination at the site of injection. Histologically, three patients had a
necrotizing granulomatous reaction with a surrounding infiltrate of lymphocytes, plasma cells,
histiocytes, and associated fibrosis. The fourth patient demonstrated a lymphohistiocytic reaction
with a predominance of histiocytic cells as well as associated plasma cells, fibroblasts, and fibrosis.
The lymphoid infiltration in these reactions showed a predominance of T-lymphocytes versus Blymphocytes. Aluminum was demonstrated in necrotic foci, inflammatory stroma, and the granular

Extended-Release Reported to the U.S. Food and Drug Administration Adverse Event Reporting System. Diabetes

DDD Consult #2116

cytoplasm of histiocytes. The reactions are thought to be immunologic or hypersensitivity reactions
associated with the aluminum contents of the preparation.
Silcock et al (2020)1 studied subcutaneous nodules following immunization in children in Victoria
Australia. They found that nodules were associated with immunizations containing aluminum
(74%, 36/49 nodules), no aluminum (8%, 4 nodules) and unknown (18%, 9 nodules). Most cases
developed symptoms within 3 days postimmunization (59%, 24 cases). Pruritus was associated in
41% (17 cases). Around 1/3 (34%) of nodules resolved 6 months post immunization, 2/3 (68%) by
12 months, however 1/4 (24%) remained persistent for >24 months. Five cases had prior nodules
and 1 case had recurrence with subsequent immunization. Eighty-three % (34 cases) were fully
immunized for age at follow-up.
In a discussion of literature, the authors note that the injection method likely contributes to nodule
development in immunization, with inadvertent subcutaneous injection associated with high rates
of nodules. The subcutaneous injection is associated with greater local inflammatory reaction
compared to intramuscular (IM) delivery. This is supported by evidence that longer needle length
reduces immunization reactogenicity, to ensure vaccine delivery to an intramuscular location
despite variations in individual delivery technique.
Other drug classes reported to be associated with injection site nodules include low-molecular
weight heparin and gonadotropin releasing hormone (GnRH) agonists used to treat prostate cancer.
Val and Val-Bernal (2014)2 reported on a subcutaneous infiltrative nodule occurring at the
injection site of low-molecular weight heparin (LMWH). They describe a 23-year-old woman
having a 3.8-cm solitary, fibrofatty, poorly demarcated nodule involving the subcutaneous tissue
of the infraumbilical abdominal wall. The nodule appeared 4 years after repeated local
administration of LMWH. Microscopically, the nodule showed atypical cells, with bizarre nuclei
in the septa of cellular fibrous tissue dissecting the adipose lobules, in the absence of any other
malignant changes. The authors speculate that the lesion may result from the combination of an
idiosyncratic reaction to the LMWH with local trauma being a probable promoting factor.
Alternatively, they state that the reactive proliferation may be entirely due to the trauma
represented by repeated injections.
Shiota et al. (2007)3 described injection-site granulomas resulting from the administration of both
leuprorelin acetate (Leuprolide acetate Eligard) and goserelin acetate (Zoladex) for the treatment
of prostatic cancer. The authors noted that injection-site granulomas caused by leuprorelin acetate
had been previously reported. They present the case of an 81-year-old man having subcutaneous
nodules of the abdominal wall and upper arm, where 11.25 mg of leuprorelin acetate had been
injected for the treatment of prostate cancer. Because of these nodules, treatment was changed to
goserelin acetate. Nevertheless, the patient developed another subcutaneous nodule at the injection

site. Histological examination showed that these nodules consisted of CD3- positive T

state that the granulomas had likely been caused by delayed-type hypersensitivity to leuprorelin
acetate injection. They state further that the granuloma that formed after goserelin acetate injection
might have developed owing to the immunogenicity of the previous leuprorelin acetate injections.
FDA Approved Drug Product Labeling:
Approved FDA labeling for a number of drug products includes the adverse reaction of injection
site nodules. For some products information regarding injection site nodules is present in Warnings
and Precautions. Among the most extensive descriptions of injection site nodules occurs in
approved FDA labeling for exenatide injection, suspension, extended-release product
BYDUREON BCISE® (see Appendix B).

Regarding the current NDAs under review: 215973, 215974
The primary study that supports the safety and efficacy of LEN is the Phase 2/3 study in HTE
(heavily treatment experienced) PWH (Study GS-US-200-4625), which is further supported by a
Phase 2 study of LEN in treatment-naive PWH (Study GS-US-200-4334).
Supportive safety data are also provided from 2 Phase 1 studies in healthy participants
(Studies GS-US-200-4538 and GS-US-200-5709); these Phase 1 studies also contributed to the
characterization of the pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles of LEN and its metabolites, supporting the
clinical pharmacology of LEN and the dosing interval.
The primary data (for establishing safety and efficacy) is based on 26-week data from GS-US-2004625 (CAPELLA), described by the Applicant as a “partially randomized, placebo-controlled,
double-blind multicenter trial.” The proposed indication, as of the time of this consult (11/30/21 –
labeling sent to applicant 11/23/2021), is “the treatment of (HIV-1) infection in heavily treatment
experienced adults with multidrug resistant HIV-1 infection failing their current antiretroviral
regimen due to resistance, intolerance, or safety considerations.”
Trial GS-US-200-4625 was conducted 31 sites in 9 countries (US, Thailand, Italy, Dominican
Republic, Spain, France, Canada, Taiwan, and South Africa).
The trial was composed of two cohorts. Subjects were enrolled into the randomized cohort (Cohort
1) if they had a < 0.5 log10 HIV-1 RNA decline compared to the screening visit. Subjects were
enrolled into the non-randomized cohort (Cohort 2) if they had a ≥ 0.5 log10 HIV-1 RNA decline
compared to the screening visit or after Cohort 1 reached its planned sample size.
Cohort 1 (N=36, randomized): In the 14-day functional monotherapy period, subjects in Cohort 1
were randomized in a 2:1 ratio in a blinded fashion to receive either drug product or placebo, while
continuing their failing regimen. This period was to establish the virologic activity of the drug
product. After the functional monotherapy period, subjects who had received drug product
continued on drug product along with an optimized background regimen (OBR); subjects who had
received placebo during this period were to initiate drug product along with an OBR (A regimen,
at the discretion of the investigator, that has at least one fully active drug in addition to LEN).
Cohort 2 (N=36, non-randomized): Subjects in cohort 2 initiated drug product and an OBR on Day 1.

There was no standardized definition of nodule or induration provided to Investigators for the
pivotal clinical trial, GS-US-200-4625 or for the supportive trial, GS-US-200-4334.
The Applicant, however, included the following definitions of induration and nodule in Injection
site manuals for the Phase 1 trials: GS-US-4070, GS-US-200-4538. (IR response received
10/25/2021, pp. 41/65 and 49/65)
Induration: (Palpable thickening, firmness, or hardening of soft tissue). Usually well demarcated
with palpable borders, can be visible (raised or sunken compared to surrounding skin), is often
‘woody’ to touch and has a flat shape (versus the rounder shape of a nodule); it can best be
described by palpation.1
Nodule: Discrete or well-demarcated soft tissue mass or lump that is firm.2
A total of 72 participants were enrolled, 36 into Cohort 1 and 36 into Cohort 2.

Following the Day 1 SC dose of LEN in a total of 72 subjects the 4th most frequently reported ISR
(reported in ≥ 10% of subjects overall) was injection site nodules. Of 72 subjects receiving one
injection, 13 or 18.1 % were noted to have injection site nodules after one injection at the NDA
data cut off (for trial GS-US-200-4625) date of April 1, 2021.
 The median time of onset was the same day as injection.

Week 26 Clinical Study Report, p. 89/628).

having nodules 9 of 13 (69%) were ongoing 150 days after injection.
 As of 221 days after injection 10 nodules in 6 (8.3%) subjects were ongoing. Of subjects
having nodules 6 of 13 (46 %) were ongoing 221 days after injection.

Applicant’s assessment of injection site reactions: Response to IR of 10/12/2021 received
10/23/202, pp. 11, 12
“To summarize, we have conducted a comprehensive assessment of injection site reactions
(ISRs) across the LEN program, including detailed review of individual cases with the
investigators. Our overall assessment is as follows. In Studies GS-US-200-4625 and GS-US200-4334, approximately half or fewer of the participants (55.6% and 38.8%, respectively)
experienced at least 1 AEs of LEN-related ISRs, which were mostly non-serous, Grade 1 or
2, and rarely led to study drug discontinuation. Most AEs of ISRs resolved within days, but
those of induration and nodules lasted longer (as described in Gilead's previous response,
Seq 0008). These AEs of induration or nodules were generally small (less than a few
centimeters), not visible to the participant or the investigator, not causing persistent
discomfort to participants, and only detectable upon deep palpation. There were rarely any
gross inflammatory components, such as tenderness or erythema, to these AEs of induration
or nodules, and when there was coincident pain or erythema, they resolved quickly. There
were no reports of tissue destruction (e.g. necrosis), or, upon careful review, suggestion of
other more serious reactions such as sterile abscess or persistent tissue damage. No
participants appeared to have any safety signal suggesting that LEN was being administered
into a deeper tissue than the intended subcutaneous space (e.g. muscle). In written, verbal,
and video communication with investigators who took care of participants with AEs of
persistent induration or nodule, none were concerned with any potential long term permanent
adverse effect, none felt the AEs required additional consultation (e.g. with a dermatologist),
and all agreed that those AEs were resolving slowly over time. In general investigators
commented on the benign nature of nodules, with some noting that nodules might be more
visible in a person who is lean and self-conscious of body image.”
Nodules:
“Based on the totality of Gilead's nonclinical assessment, the induration or nodules after SC
LEN injection appears to be a normal and expected tissue body response, manifesting as
chronic granulomatous inflammation histologically, to a foreign body (i.e. a drug depot)
without permanent adverse effect. We have not found evidence of injection site nodules or
induration being reflective of more serious complications, or resulting from aberrant
injections, nor have we found evidence that nodules are related to body habitus or
immunologic status …. The presence of injection site nodule or induration appears not to in any
way diminish the efficacy of LEN. That assessment is also supported by the findings from the
popPK modeling where LEN exposures were similar between participants with and without
16

Reference ID: 4911738

DDD Consult #2116

injection site induration/nodules, indicating the lack of impact of induration/nodules on LEN
release profile.... In summary, our assessment is that nodules that rise to the level of AE reporting
occur in a subset of individuals receiving LEN, represent subcutaneous deposition of the drug
itself and expected tissue reaction, and are not associated with adverse clinical or pharmacologic
consequences.”

Question for DDD
 Considering the length of time for resolution of injection site nodules and indurations with
SC LEN injections, and with particular concern for individuals with low body fat
percentage, does DDD recommend that additional clinical safety information be submitted
regarding these reactions? In addition, we request any recommendations for
communication of ISRs in labeling.

(Relevant criteria for Warnings and Precautions (per October 2011 Guidance for Industry:
Warnings and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed Warning Sections of Labeling for
Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products – Content and Format) include the following:
Serious AEs: Death, Life-threatening AE, hospitalization, significant disruption of ability to
conduct normal life functions, congenital anomaly; medical judgement may jeopardize patient.
Indication; relative seriousness of condition, Incidence high absolute risk of AE; AE that could
lead to serious outcome unless dosage changed, Preventable by patient selection to avoid
potentially serious outcome, AE significantly affecting compliance.)
Medication Guide:
The DAV reviewer does not recommend inclusion of a Medication Guide since nodules, as a
safety issue, did not satisfy criteria that would necessitate a Medication Guide. DDD agrees with
the review division.
Per the Guidance of November 2011, Medication Guides – Distribution Requirements and
Inclusion in Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS):
Section 208.1(c) authorizes FDA to require a Medication Guide if FDA determines one or more of
the following circumstances exist: (1) The drug product is one for which patient labeling could help
prevent serious adverse effects. (2) The drug product is one that has serious risk(s) (relative to
benefits) of which patients should be made aware because information concerning the risks could
affect patients’ decision to use, or continue to use, the product. (3) The drug product is important to
health and patient adherence to directions for use is crucial to the drug’s effectiveness.

 Additional DDD Recommendations for Ongoing or Future Trials:
DDD recommends that for ongoing trials where possible that nodules be followed to resolution or
stabilization. In addition, DDD recommends that human histopathology be obtained (e.g., punch
biopsy in a few subjects). The presence of findings such as necrosis, hypersensitivity, granulomas,
foreign bodies, fibrous tissue, etc. can aid in understanding the pathophysiology of nodule
formation. This in turn can increase understanding of which treatments are most likely to be
useful.

Appendix B: Examples of Labeling Related to Injection Site Nodules
BYDUREON BCISE® (exenatide extended-release) injectable suspension, for subcutaneous
use. Initial U.S. Approval: 2005
5.9 Injection-Site Reactions There have been postmarketing reports of serious injection-site
reactions (e.g., abscess, cellulitis, and necrosis), with or without subcutaneous nodules, with the
use of BYDUREON [see Adverse Reactions (6.3)]. Isolated cases required surgical intervention.
6.1 Clinical Trial Experience

Adverse Reactions Leading to Discontinuation of Therapy
The incidence of discontinuation of therapy due to adverse reactions was 3.9% for BYDUREON
BCISE-treated patients in the two comparator-controlled 28-week trials in adults. The most
common classes of adverse reactions leading to discontinuation of therapy for BYDUREON
BCISE-treated patients were Gastrointestinal Disorders 2.0% and General Disorders and
Administration Site Conditions 1.2%. For BYDUREON BCISE-treated patients, the most frequent
adverse reactions leading to discontinuation of therapy within each of these respective classes were
diarrhea (0.7%), nausea (0.7%), vomiting (0.5%) and injection-site nodule (0.5%).
Injection-Site Adverse Reactions In the two comparator-controlled 28-week trials in adults,
injection site reactions (including injection site nodule, injection site pruritus, injection site
bruising) were observed in 23.9% of patients treated with BYDUREON BCISE. The formation of
subcutaneous nodules is consistent with the properties of the microspheres used in BYDUREON
BCISE.
Other drug products having labeling describing injection site nodules in Section 5: Warnings and
Precautions include; granisetron (SUSTOL), enfuvirtide (FUZEON), and medroxyprogesterone
acetate (DEPO-SUBQ-PROVERA).

include; cabotegravir extended-release injectable suspension;rilpivirine extended-release injectable
suspension (CABENUVA), enoxaparin sodium injection (LOVENOX), insulin human injection

CABENUVA (cabotegravir extended-release injectable; rilpivirine extended-release
injectable suspension), co-packaged for intramuscular use
Initial U.S. Approval: 2021
Section 6.1 Clinical Trials Experience
Injection-Associated Adverse Reactions
Local Injection Site Reactions (ISRs): The most frequent adverse reactions associated with the
intramuscular administration of CABENUVA were ISRs. After 14,682 injections, 3,663 ISRs were
reported. One percent (1%) of subjects discontinued treatment with CABENUVA because of ISRs.
Most ISRs were mild (Grade 1, 75%) or moderate (Grade 2, 36%). Four percent (4%) of subjects
experienced severe (Grade 3) ISRs, and no subjects experienced Grade 4 ISRs. The most
commonly reported ISR was localized pain/discomfort (79%) regardless of severity or relatedness.
Other manifestations of ISRs reported in more than 1% of subjects over the duration of the
analysis period included nodules (14%),* induration (12%), swelling (8%), erythema (4%),
pruritus (4%), bruising (3%), warmth (2%), and hematoma (2%). Abscess and cellulitis at the
injection site were each reported in less than 1% of subjects. The median duration of ISR events
was 3 days.
